The Noble Great Vehicle Sūtra called The Meeting of the Father and the Son (Āryapitāputrasamāgamananāmamahāyānasūtra)

The Meeting of the Father and the Son, known in Sanskrit as the Pitāputrasamāgamana Sūtra and in Tibetan as Yab dang sras mjal ba, is a Great Vehicle sūtra that also bears the subtitle "the Definitive Teaching on Reality." It recounts the Buddha's meeting with his father, King Śuddhodana, along with members of the Śākya clan, an occasion on which the Buddha teaches the ultimate nature of things, emptiness and the true character of reality, to those assembled. The work opens in the classic sūtra manner, with homage to the buddhas, bodhisattvas, śrāvakas, and pratyekabuddhas, and a description of the Blessed One surrounded by a retinue of liberated, tamed, and peaceful disciples. It is Toh 60 in the Kangyur, where it forms the sixteenth chapter of the Heap of Jewels, or Ratnakūṭa, collection of sūtras, and it became an influential scriptural source for later Indian and Tibetan discussions of emptiness.

From the Noble Great Heap of Jewels, the Dharma discourse in one hundred thousand chapters, the sixteenth chapter: The Noble Meeting of the Father and the Son, the Definitive Teaching on Reality. The first fascicle.

In the language of India: ārya pitāputra samāgaman nāma mahāyānasūtra. In the language of Tibet: The Noble Great Vehicle Sūtra called The Meeting of the Father and the Son.

Homage to all the buddhas and bodhisattvas, and to the noble śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas.

Thus did I hear at one time. The Blessed One, the Buddha, was liberated, with a retinue of the liberated; tamed, with a retinue of the tamed; at peace, with a retinue of those at peace; crossed over, with a retinue of those who had crossed over; gone beyond, with a retinue of those gone beyond; arrived on firm ground, with a retinue of those who had arrived on firm ground; come to relief, with a retinue of those who had come to relief. He was a monk, with a retinue of monks; a destroyer of afflictions, with a retinue of destroyers of afflictions; a renunciant (śramaṇa), with a retinue of renunciants; one who had removed evil, with a retinue of those who had removed evil; a true brahmin, with a retinue of true brahmins; one whose nature was to have removed evil, with a retinue of those whose nature was to have removed evil; possessed of knowledge, with a retinue of those possessed of knowledge; one who had bathed, with a retinue of those who had bathed; a proclaimer, with a retinue of proclaimers; purified of afflictions, with a retinue of those purified of afflictions.

He had eliminated the five factors, with a retinue of those who had eliminated the five factors; he was free of obscurations, with a retinue of those free of obscurations; his mind was clear, with a retinue of those whose minds were clear; he was endowed with the six factors, with a retinue of those endowed with the six factors; he was free of attachment and aversion, with a retinue of those free of attachment and aversion; he had the single guard, with a retinue of those with the single guard; he was perfect in guarding the door of mindfulness, with a retinue of those perfect in guarding the door of mindfulness; he was endowed with the four reliances, with a retinue of those endowed with the four reliances; he had eliminated partial truths, with a retinue of those who had eliminated partial truths; he had abandoned seeking, with a retinue of those who had abandoned seeking.

He had reached the end, with a retinue of those who had reached the end; he had arrived at the ultimate goal, with a retinue of those who had arrived at the ultimate goal; he had cultivated himself, with a retinue of those who had cultivated themselves; he was free of the turbidity of conceptualizing, with a retinue of those free of the turbidity of conceptualizing; his conceptual thought was completely cut off, with a retinue of those whose conceptual thought was completely cut off; his bodily formations were fully stilled, with a retinue of those whose bodily formations were fully stilled; he was intent upon the unshakable, with a retinue of those intent upon the unshakable; his mind was fully liberated, with a retinue of those whose minds were fully liberated; his wisdom was fully liberated, with a retinue of those whose wisdom was fully liberated. He was a noble one, with a retinue of noble ones, free of the faults of branches, leaves, petals, shoots, and bark, pure, established in the heartwood.

He was dwelling in the land of the Śākyas, at Kapilavastu, in the Nyagrodha Park, together with a great Saṅgha of twelve hundred and fifty monks, among them the group of five, Yaśas, Udāyin, Uruvilvā Kāśyapa, Gayā Kāśyapa, Nadī Kāśyapa, Mahākāśyapa, Śāriputra, and Maudgalyāyana. All of them were arhats. Their defilements were exhausted, they were without afflictions, and they had gained mastery. Their minds were fully liberated and their wisdom was fully liberated. They were thoroughbreds, great elephants; they had done what was to be done, accomplished their task, and laid down the burden. They had attained their own goal, and their fetters to existence were utterly exhausted. Their minds were fully liberated through perfect knowledge, and they had reached the supreme perfection of mastery over all minds. He was with these, every one of this kind.

At that time, at the break of dawn, the Blessed One was seated in the open air, surrounded by the Saṅgha of monks, and with the Saṅgha of monks gazing at him from in front. Then the Blessed One, remaining silent, looked upon the entire Saṅgha of monks, who were silent. Having looked upon the entire Saṅgha of monks, he said to the monks, "Monks, find some monk of such a kind that he can bring King Śuddhodana to faith."

Then the venerable Ājñāta Kauṇḍinya rose from his seat, draped his upper robe over one shoulder, and placed his right knee on the ground. Bowing with palms joined toward the Blessed One, he said to the Blessed One, "Blessed One, I shall bring King Śuddhodana to faith."

When he had said this, the Blessed One said to the venerable Ājñāta Kauṇḍinya, "Kauṇḍinya, you are one who should be endowed with the qualities of one held in the very highest esteem, so remain seated."

Likewise the venerable Vāṣpa, Mahānāman, Yaśas, Uruvilvā Kāśyapa, Nadī Kāśyapa, Gayā Kāśyapa, Mahākāśyapa, the venerable Śāriputra, and Mahāmaudgalyāyana also said to the Blessed One, "Blessed One, I shall bring King Śuddhodana to faith."

When he had said this, the Blessed One said to the venerable Mahāmaudgalyāyana, "Maudgalyāyana, you too are one who should be endowed with the qualities of one held in the very highest esteem, so remain seated."

Then the venerable Mahāmaudgalyāyana thought, "Which monk does the Blessed One wish to bring King Śuddhodana to faith?" Then the venerable Mahāmaudgalyāyana entered into meditative equipoise in a samādhi of that kind. With his mind in equipoise in this way, he saw that the Blessed One's intention rested on the venerable Udāyin, and that his intention was, "This monk Udāyin will bring King Śuddhodana to faith." It was like this. Suppose that in a multistoried house, or in the courtyard of a multistoried house whose door faces north, the rays of the sun came through an eastern window and shone upon the western wall. In just the same way, at that time the venerable Mahāmaudgalyāyana saw the Blessed One's intention shining upon the venerable Udāyin, and saw that his intention was, "This monk Udāyin will bring King Śuddhodana to faith."

Then the venerable Mahāmaudgalyāyana rose from that samādhi, went to where the venerable Udāyin was, and said to the venerable Udāyin, "Venerable Udāyin, the Blessed One intends and wishes this concerning you: 'The monk Udāyin is able to bring King Śuddhodana to faith.' Venerable Udāyin, since that is so, arouse your confidence to bring King Śuddhodana to faith."

When he had said this, the venerable Udāyin said to the venerable Mahāmaudgalyāyana, "Venerable Mahāmaudgalyāyana, families are extremely hard to bring to faith, and royal families are harder still. Why is that? Because kings, kṣatriyas anointed on the crown of the head, are hard to please. Venerable Mahāmaudgalyāyana, it is like this. Suppose there were ten loads of firewood, or twenty, or thirty, or forty, or fifty, or even a hundred loads of firewood, without any moisture, dry, and many years old, and someone set fire to it. A great mass of fire would blaze up. If someone then poured butter or sesame oil on it, that mass of fire would blaze far greater than before, and to go on top of it or into its immediate surroundings would be extremely hard. Venerable Mahāmaudgalyāyana, in just the same way, kings, kṣatriyas anointed on the crown of the head, are hard to please. Venerable Mahāmaudgalyāyana, it is like this. Just as it is extremely hard to go between the teeth of a tusked elephant whose mouth is gaping open, venerable Mahāmaudgalyāyana, in just the same way, kings, kṣatriyas anointed on the crown of the head, are hard to please."

When he had said this, the venerable Mahāmaudgalyāyana said to the venerable Udāyin, "Venerable Udāyin, that is indeed so. Nevertheless, the Blessed One intends and wishes this concerning you: 'This monk Udāyin is able to bring King Śuddhodana to faith.'"

He said, "Venerable Mahāmaudgalyāyana, does the Blessed One intend me to bring King Śuddhodana to faith?"

He said, "Venerable Udāyin, it is so. The Blessed One intends, 'The monk Udāyin will bring King Śuddhodana to faith.'"

Then the Blessed One himself said to the venerable Udāyin, "Udāyin, bring King Śuddhodana to faith. Why is that? Udāyin, King Śuddhodana is to be brought to faith either by me or else by you. Udāyin, among all my śrāvakas, however many there are, you are in this way the foremost of those who bring families to faith."

Then at that time the Blessed One spoke to the venerable Udāyin in verse:

"Good Udāyin, listen to me.
Since you are the very foremost at bringing families to faith,
Swiftly bring to faith the noblest of kings,
Who gives joy to the Śākya clans.
If that supreme king is brought to faith,
Son of good family, for gods and for humans
Good will come that has no end and no middle;
So swiftly bring the noblest of men to faith.
At present that lord of the Śākyas
Is stirred up and undone by thoughts of overweening pride;
Like a boulder rolling from a mountainside,
He remains, having cast off all diligence and striving.
Because he keeps recalling his lost sovereignty,
His mind is wounded, and he loses heart and mind.
Just as merchants parted from their wealth
Keep recalling it and dwell in torment,
And as gods, dying and passing from the abodes of the gods,
Keep recalling the divine riches they desire,
So, not truly knowing the teaching of the Victor,
The king, his happiness lost, utters pitiful laments.
Recalling his seven kinds of precious things,
The four human excellences,
And his enjoyments,
That lord of the earth utters pitiful laments.
Just as when a man is overpowered
By a mighty rākṣasa who steals the vital luster,
He knows neither himself nor others,
So too is that lord of the earth.
Just as people hidden behind Kailāsa, the mountain of snow,
Do not see what is to be seen,
So, being hidden by great pride,
The king does not see what should be seen.
A man fallen asleep or crazed by a death
Does not know even his own mind;
So, being intoxicated by great pride,
That king too does not know what is virtuous."

Just as a woman, maddened by her husband's death,
Cries out in pitiful laments,
So too, intoxicated by his great pride,
That king also cries out in pitiful laments.
Therefore, Udāyin, you who are skilled in means,
You must bring the king to faith here.
Bring down the banner of pride
And raise the sublime banner of the Dharma.
Udāyin, apart from you, among the beings in existence
There is no one who shares your fortune and your conduct,
So there is no monk at all
Who could bring the king's mind to faith.

Udāyin, long ago, in times past,
There lived a lord of the earth
Whose name was Truth Increaser,
A renowned protector of the land.
He was a Dharma king, endowed with Dharma,
Honored by koṭis upon koṭis of living beings.
This earth, reaching to the ocean's shores,
Was ruled by that righteous king.
Its villages and towns stood close together,
Filled with fruit trees rich in fruit and flowers.
It was filled with noble people
And with humans free of wrongdoing.
It was full of many hundreds of gandharvas;
No thorny stalks, no noxious grass or trees were found.
It was soft, adorned with clusters of ascetics' dwellings,
And beautified by rivers with easy fords.
Hundreds of bands of sages roamed there,
And everywhere the people practiced bright deeds.
The evil paths to the lower realms were cut off,
And so all beings longed for the abodes of the gods.

That king had a son
Who had seen the buddhas who appeared in the past;
He had developed roots of virtue
And possessed an intelligence imbued with many qualities.
His name was Matigarbha,
Honored by koṭis upon koṭis of living beings.
He saw the faults in sense pleasures,
And though he lived in the house of the lord of men, he delighted in solitude.

The king called Truth Increaser
Set bands of young women all around him,
And said, "In this splendid house, like a palace of the gods,
Enjoy the fine pleasures of the senses.
Excellent son, whatever you wished,
What have I not done for you?
Why is your mind not drawn to pleasures?
Tell me quickly, my son.
Become skilled in the delights of worldly existence;
Son, take joy in this precious house.
Keep company with a retinue of precious women;
Delight in the objects of the senses and grow fond of them.
Even sages who dwell in forests of austerity
Leave them and come back again to these things;
They give up their austerities and enjoy the delights of pleasure.
These pleasures are laid out for you, my only son;
Therefore enjoy the delights of pleasure.
I and the whole kingdom are at one with you.
In this house adorned with celestial mansions,
Like the abode of Vaiśravaṇa, take your pleasure.
These women too are of supreme beauty,
Like goddesses, perfected in the arts,
Skilled in the ways of music and in song,
Accomplished in attendance, their charms captivating the mind.
Their eyes are like lotus petals,
Their lips lovely and charming, the color of the bimba fruit.
Their teeth and complexions are flawless and fine,
Their foreheads and brows are lovely and their necks graceful.
Their thighs are like an elephant's trunk,
Their shoulders full, their upper arms shapely.
The palms of their hands and the soles of their feet are copper-colored,
Their fingers and calves slender and fine.
Their tongues, copper-hued, are thin and clean,
And their way of speaking is measured and gentle.
Their teeth are well set, without any gaps,
Their eyeteeth brilliant white, the finest adornment.
Their bellies are smooth, their navels lovely,
Their hips broad and their waists like a vajra.
Their calves are like elephant tusks,
Like the well-grown pith of a reed.
Their gait is like that of a proud elephant.
Thinking of no one else, they gaze at you with all their hearts.
They long to play and are in the bloom of youth.
Young prince, look on them with compassion and show them love.
Dear son, the fine complexions and the youthful bloom
Of you and of these women
Will fall like flowers from the treetop.
So give up your pride and indulge in pleasure."

Then Matigarbha spoke thus
To his lamenting father and to all the retinue:
"Father, what you say is sincere;
Truly, your words bring no reproach upon your good name.
Yet hear my words, O king, inclined toward true qualities,
Pondered and well settled.
Hear how, even in my dreams,
My mind takes no delight in pleasures and is not drawn to them.
These things the lord of the earth has said
Do not appeal to the wise.
Foolish people think them right,
But the wise always disparage them.
For what reason would one who is not blind
Take a wrong road at the word of the blind?
Who, standing on dry land, would ever
Long to be helplessly swept away by water?
How would one who is free
Enter that prison at the word of one in chains?
How would one standing on level ground, unconfused,
Long for a perilous place?
Lord of the earth, one attached to pleasures,
Swept away by the waters of sensual enjoyment,
I regard as like the blind, like one swept away by water,
Like one in prison, like one in a perilous place.
Lord of men, you are like the blind;
I have eyes and see the difference.
You are swept away by the river of desire;
I utterly reject all pleasures.
Pleasures are like poisonous leaves;
Like the head of a snake, they bring suffering;
They are like a trident, a sword, a short spear,
Like a mass of fire blazing fiercely."

Thus Matigarbha spoke
To his lamenting father and the retinue,
And paying no heed to the seven precious things,
He renounced the kingdom and went forth.
As a snake casts off its old skin,
He cast it away like discarded mucus and ashes;
Matigarbha, without attachment,
Free of all wrongdoing, went forth.
When the prince had thus gone forth,
A minister's son followed him into homelessness;
His name was Candradatta,
And he went forth after Matigarbha.
When the prince had thus gone forth,
He developed the four abodes of Brahmā (brahmavihāras),
And together with his companion
He brought forth the five higher knowledges, complete and entire.
Because he saw the faults in pleasures,
That lion among men passed beyond the desire realm.
In order to attain the true state of Brahmā,
He turned the wheel of Brahmā.
When the prince had gone forth in this way,
The father lost faith in his son,
And Candradatta went to him and

and delighted him with well-spoken words.
Udāyin, what do you think of that?
Do you think that Essence of Intellect at that time was someone else?
At that time I myself was he,
who did not pursue the enjoyments of sensual pleasure.
Udāyin, what do you think of that?
Do you think that Increase of Truth was someone else?
This very lord of men, Śuddhodana, at that time
was the king called Increase of Truth.
Udāyin, what do you think of that?
Do you think that Candradatta, who went forth after him, was someone else?
You yourself, at that time, on that occasion,
were the one who brought Increase of Truth to faith.
Therefore, Udāyin, you must here
awaken faith in King Śuddhodana.
If this protector of the earth comes to faith,
great happiness and prosperity will come about.

Then the venerable Udāyin assented to the Blessed One by remaining silent. When that night had passed, in the morning, the venerable Udāyin put on his robe and lower garment, took up his alms bowl, and went to the great city of Kapilavastu.

At that time King Śuddhodana was seated before the assembly of his queens, gathered together with about a thousand Śākyas on a certain matter of business. King Śuddhodana had heard that the Blessed One had come to Kapilavastu and was staying in Kapilavastu itself, in the Nyagrodha Park, and he thought: "Because that prince went forth, I have been deprived of great sovereign wealth. Had that prince remained in the household, he would have become a wheel-turning king, victorious over the four quarters, righteous, a Dharma king possessing the seven precious things. These are his seven precious things: the precious wheel, the precious elephant, the precious horse, the precious jewel, the precious woman, the precious householder, and the precious minister; these are the seven. He would have had a full thousand sons, heroic, valiant, endowed with the finest bodily form, crushers of opposing armies. He would have subdued this world system of four great continents without punishment and without driving it by weapons, evenly and without unevenness, in harmony with the Dharma, and would have protected it well for a full eon. Had that come to pass, we too would have held sovereign power in the realm of a wheel-turning king; we would have been served, honored, respected and worshiped, and we would have gained great sovereign power as well. But now, because that prince went forth, we have been parted from such great power and wealth."

Having thought this, he commanded the Śākyas: "No one is to go to see my son or to wait upon him. If anyone goes to see my son or to wait upon him, he will be punished with bodily harm."

At that time a Śākya youth called Joyful Face rose from that assembly and went out. When the Śākya youth Joyful Face saw the venerable Udāyin coming from far off, he went to where the venerable Udāyin was, greeted the venerable Udāyin warmly, and said: "Come here, venerable Udāyin! Welcome, venerable Udāyin! It is a long time since you came, venerable Udāyin. Venerable Udāyin, is the Blessed One free from illness? Is he strong? Does he dwell at ease? Is he free from discomfort? Is nothing troubling him? Did the Blessed One travel in comfort? Is he not too weary? Is he not worn out by the alms round?"

When he had said this, the venerable Udāyin said to the Śākya youth Joyful Face: "Joyful Face, it is so. The Blessed One is free from illness. He is strong. He dwells at ease. He is free from discomfort. Nothing troubles him. He traveled in comfort. He is not weary. He is not worn out by the alms round."

The Śākya youth Well Awakened, too, seeing the Śākya youth Joyful Face seated in a secluded place with the venerable Udāyin, rose from that assembly, went to where the venerable Udāyin was, greeted the venerable Udāyin warmly, and said: "Come here, venerable Udāyin! Welcome, venerable Udāyin! It is a long time since you came, venerable Udāyin. Venerable Udāyin, is the Blessed One free from illness? Is he strong? Does he dwell at ease? Is he free from discomfort? Is nothing troubling him? Did the Blessed One travel in comfort? Is he not too weary? Is he not worn out by the alms round?"

When he had said this, the venerable Udāyin said to the Śākya youth Well Awakened: "Well Awakened, it is so. The Blessed One is free from illness. He is strong. He dwells at ease. He is free from discomfort. Nothing troubles him. He traveled in comfort. He is not weary. He is not worn out by the alms round."

The Śākya youths Aśoka and Vigataśoka, too, seeing the venerable Udāyin, the Śākya youth Joyful Face and the Śākya youth Well Awakened seated in a secluded place, went to where the venerable Udāyin was, greeted the venerable Udāyin warmly, and said: "Come here, venerable Udāyin! Welcome, venerable Udāyin! It is a long time since you came, venerable Udāyin. Venerable Udāyin, is the Blessed One free from illness? Is he strong? Does he dwell at ease? Is he free from discomfort? Is nothing troubling him? Did the Blessed One travel in comfort? Is he not too weary? Is he not worn out by the alms round?"

When they had said this, the venerable Udāyin said to the Śākya youths Aśoka and Vigataśoka: "Aśoka and Vigataśoka, the Blessed One is free from illness. He is strong. He dwells at ease. He is free from discomfort. Nothing troubles him. He traveled in comfort. He is not weary. He is not worn out by the alms round. And why is that? Because the Four Great Kings, Śakra (Indra) the lord of the gods, and Brahmā the lord of the Sahā world strive to make offerings to the Blessed One."

When he had said this, those Śākya youths said to the venerable Udāyin, "Reverend Udāyin, although we wish to see the Blessed One (the Buddha) and attend upon him, we are not able to do so. Why is that? Because King Śuddhodana has decreed, 'Let no one go to see my son or to attend upon him. If anyone goes to see him or attend upon him, he will be punished with bodily harm.' Therefore, noble Udāyin, out of fear of the king we cannot go to see the Blessed One or to attend upon him."

Then the venerable Udāyin thought, "Alas, this King Śuddhodana is utterly overcome by pride. How shall I go into his presence?" Then he thought this: "What if, by miraculous power, I were to rise into the sky to the height of seven palm trees, sit cross-legged in the air above, and go to King Śuddhodana in that way?" Then the venerable Udāyin entered into a meditative absorption of that kind, and with his mind so composed he rose into the sky to the height of seven palm trees, sat cross-legged in the air, and went to where King Śuddhodana was.

King Śuddhodana, seeing from far off the venerable Udāyin coming through the sky by miraculous power, risen to the height of seven palm trees and seated cross-legged in the air, joined his palms toward him and spoke these verses:

"You who wear the dyed robe, from where have you come?
Tell me quickly what it is you need.
Whatever you need, that too I shall quickly give.
One perfected in ascetic discipline is very hard to see."

When he had said this, the venerable Udāyin replied to King Śuddhodana in verse:

"O king, I am a son of your son.
It is the Dharma I need, O king, wherever it abides.
I am one who carries the Tathāgata's food;
I bear the food of the Sugata, the Protector.

Lord of the realm, possessed of great splendor,
You have a son, supreme among men, who has won renown.
With his majesty he blazes in every direction,
Like the noonday light of the autumn sun.

Just as the sun in a cloudless sky
Blazes forth, shedding light in every direction,
So, O elephant among kings, your son,
Possessed of wisdom, shines in every direction.

Just as, when the autumn sun has risen,
It outshines the fireflies and shines forth,
So, O elephant among kings, your son
Has vanquished all the other non-Buddhists (tīrthikas) and dwells resplendent.

Just as the stars, outshone by the sun,
In daytime, empty of light, cannot glitter,
So, O elephant among kings, outshone
By your son, the other tīrthikas do not appear.

Just as the moon, outshone by the sun,
Empty of light, does not appear in the expanse of the sky,
So the tīrthikas, outshone by your son,
Have no brilliance, like the moon outshone by the sun.

When small creatures hear the lion's roar,
They are stupefied by its force and, each as best it can,
Scatter into the ravines of the mountains and cower there;
The tiger's roar is hard for others to bear.

Just so, O king, the speech of the great sage:
When the other tīrthikas hear the roar of selflessness,
Though, of meager wisdom, they have reached the Peak of Existence,
On hearing his speech they all become afraid.

To the blind world together with its gods
He who has eyes has granted sight.
He makes light and clears away the dust;
The maṇḍala of wisdom's light has dawned.

He has fully shown the two paths, of happiness and of unhappiness;
The Victor has shown the path and the path that strays.
He brings those who have lost the way onto the path,
And draws those sunk in the mire out onto the plain.

Just as, on parched ground, a cloud
Satisfies with rain both the heights and the hollows,
So, O elephant among kings, your son
Satisfies beings, gods included, with the Dharma.

Just as ravines and forests satisfied by rain,
With grasses and shrubs, groves and trees,
Vines and trunks and all kinds of healing herbs,
And many kinds of flowers, adorn the earth,

So the king's son, who possesses the ten powers and the fearlessnesses,
On the trunk of the eighteen unshared qualities,
Adorned with the flowers and fruits
Of omniscient wisdom, is beautiful."

Just as Mount Meru in the midst of the ocean,
Abode of the gods, unmoving, shines in splendor,
So too, O elephant among kings, your son
Shines in splendor in the ocean of ascetics.

Just as Śakra (Indra), lord of the gods, husband of Śacī,
Looks splendid when seated upon his divine throne,
So too, O elephant among kings, your son
Shines in splendor amid the assembly of ascetics.

Mighty with loving-kindness, sounding deep thunder,
Wreathed in garlands of lightning, his great compassion,
Massing dense clouds, the streaming rain of the applications of mindfulness,
That Victor, supreme nāga, lets fall the rain of Dharma.

His perfectly pure disk, its rays the excellent moral discipline,
His absorption utterly crushing the asuras of affliction,
His rays of wisdom drying up the craving for existence,
The great sun of the Victor shines in splendor upon beings.

Absorption and moral discipline are its rafts and great ships,
The applications of mindfulness its hoard of precious jewels,
The factors of awakening its mine of gems, meditative concentration its waves:
Those who want liberation should resort to the ocean of the Buddha.

Its roots, pure moral discipline, firmly planted,
Absorption its leaves, mindfulness its branches and foliage,
The factors of awakening its flowers, emptiness its trunk:
Rely on the Victor's tree, which gives the shade of selflessness.

Great in strength, dwelling at the forest's edge of moral discipline,
Abiding in the mountain cave of peaceful absorption,
His domain emptiness, signlessness, and wishlessness,
Your son, the Victor, is a mighty lion.

The Sage teaches the abiding that is non-abiding.
As the Victor, a lordly bull, walks with majestic gait,
Childish ordinary beings and the other tīrthikas are struck with fear,
And your son lets out the bellow of the bull.

Adorned with the treasure of moral discipline and the riches of good qualities,
Protected by the calm abiding of serene meditative concentration,
Guarded by wisdom, his domain liberation,
The Victor, the chief caravan leader, bestows wealth.

Having destroyed evil by embracing virtue,
Pacifying afflictions, and commanding the practice of virtue,
In the world with its gods and asuras,
O king, your supreme son shines in splendor.

Supreme clouds of moral discipline flashing the lightning of intelligence,
Soaking the earth with the moisture of the great rain of the eightfold path,
The Sage bestows the fruit of a precious harvest:
Your son, a great nāga, sounds the thunder.

The king said:
Has that supreme person come in comfort?
Is the Guide not wearied by wandering for alms?
Is his body not exhausted with fatigue?
Has he not withered like an utpala lotus lying on open ground?

Udāyin said:
Possessing the power of miracles, his domain meditative concentration,
His limbs full and flourishing with delight and ease,
Since the Sage abides in the attainment of peace,
The Victor, like a golden lotus, does not wither.

The king said:
In the past he was roused by the sound of clay drums and kettledrums,
Roused by the sweet melody of flutes,
Awakened again and again by the singing of women.
Now, when he wakes, does he not suffer?

Udāyin said:
Contemplating in meditative concentration, his domain the peace of absorption,
Skilled in the ways of accomplishing absorption,
Whether he sleeps or rises, walks or sits,
The Victor has no suffering; he is ever joyful.

The king said:
He used to lie upon a supreme bed, laid out as befits greatness,
Surrounded on every side by rows of lamps,
Encircled by a throng of women as he lay.
Now, lying in darkness, how can he not be bewildered?

Udāyin said:
When the noble Great Sage lies upon his bed,
He spreads the seat of loving-kindness and rises at ease through compassion;
Abiding in joy, he is filled with delight;
For one beyond existence, possessed of equanimity, there is no bewilderment.

The king said:
The heroes of the Śākyas always attended upon him,
And he played and took delight with those learned in the treatises,
Taking his pleasure in a house like a celestial palace.
How can my son be happy at the forest's edge?

Udāyin said:
Those born of the Dharma, endowed with peaceful minds,
Always attend upon him whose mind is at peace.
Since he cultivates peace in the peaceful wilderness,
The fearless Teacher delights there.

The king said:
In the past, when the prince lived at home,
His limbs anointed with sweet-scented oil,
He was bathed as befits a king.
At the forest's edge, who bathes the Sage?

Udāyin said:
Pure discipline is the bathing steps, the Dharma the water of the pool;
Bathed in it, the Sage bathes others too.
His body unwet, he comes forth together with his heirs;
Having crossed over himself, he ferries a multitude of people across.

The king said:
Adorned with golden threads and garlands of jewels,
He wore excellent, fine garments befitting a king,
And leaf patterns were beautifully painted on him with fragrant sandalwood.
Amid that great forest, who adorns the Sage?

Udāyin said:
Anointed with the perfume of moral discipline, adorned with the garland of good qualities,
Conscience his clothing, absorption and meditative concentration his ornaments,
His body adorned with the superknowledges, the knowledges, and the deliverances,
Radiant with liberation, the Sage shines in the forest.

The king said:
Heroes come from battle, bearing two-pointed weapons and swords,
Guarded his person,
And he was always sheltered by canopies and parasols.
Now that he stays alone in the forest, who guards him?

Udāyin said:
The Sage is wholly protected by the power of loving-kindness;
Wisdom is his army, the śrāvakas his attendants and servants.
Certain in his mass of good qualities, he has no fear;
He possesses the joy of the ten powers and has attained the fearlessnesses.

The king said:
You have spoken of my son's good qualities,
And I have heard you tell how he practices the Dharma well and is unwearied.
You too, take this food, honoring it as alms,
And I too shall go, as befits a lord of men.

When he had said this, the venerable Udāyin spoke thus to King Śuddhodana: "Great king, that Blessed One is the master of the assembly, the foremost of the assembly, the one who gathers the community of ascetics, a great sage. When he sits amid the community of ascetics, that king of ascetics is radiant, brilliant, and resplendent. Great king, it is like this: for example, when on the fifteenth day of the waxing fortnight the moon is full and complete, surrounded on every side by the host of stars, it is radiant, brilliant, and resplendent. Likewise that Blessed One too, when he sits amid the community of ascetics, is radiant, brilliant, and resplendent. Great king, it is like this: for example, the sun in the autumn season is exceedingly radiant, brilliant, and resplendent. Likewise that Blessed One too, when he sits amid the community of ascetics, is exceedingly radiant, brilliant, and resplendent. It is like this: for example, Mount Meru, king of mountains, standing in the midst of the ocean, is radiant, brilliant, and resplendent.

In the same way, that Blessed One (the Buddha), seated in the midst of the assembly of renunciants, is radiant, resplendent, and glorious. It is like this: just as Śakra (Indra), lord of the gods, is radiant, resplendent, and glorious when he dwells in Sudharmā, the assembly hall of the gods, so too that Blessed One, seated in the midst of the assembly of renunciants, is radiant, resplendent, and glorious. It is like this: just as the god Suyāma, the god Santuṣita, the god Sunirmita, and the god Vaśavartin are each radiant, resplendent, and glorious when they dwell in the midst of their assemblies of gods, so too the Blessed One, seated in the midst of the assembly of renunciants, is radiant, resplendent, and glorious. It is like this: just as Brahmā, lord of the Sahā world, is radiant, resplendent, and glorious when he is surrounded by a thousand koṭis of Brahmās, so too that Blessed One, seated in the midst of the assembly of renunciants, is radiant, resplendent, and glorious.

Then, when King Śuddhodana had heard this account of the Blessed One's qualities, he thought: "If this one, who is merely a śrāvaka of that Blessed One, is renowned for such great miraculous power, such great might, and such great mastery, then what need is there to speak of the Blessed One himself? At the very moment of his birth this great earth shook in six ways. It shook, shook strongly, and shook strongly all over. It moved, moved strongly, and moved strongly all over. It rocked, rocked strongly, and rocked strongly all over. It quaked, quaked strongly, and quaked strongly all over. It roared, roared strongly, and roared strongly all over. It surged, surged strongly, and surged strongly all over, and an immeasurable light appeared. What need is there to speak of that? As soon as he was born, unsupported by anyone, he took seven steps upon the ground; from the sky above came two streams of water, one cold and one warm, and with these two he was bathed; a fine divine throne with a center made of gold appeared for him; and from the sky above appeared a divine parasol together with yak-tail whisks. What need is there to speak of that? As soon as he was born all the gods bowed to him, and from the time he was born until the time he went forth he was never mistaken, always steadfast in his commitments, acting just as he had spoken, always and without interruption firm in diligence, speaking truly, free from deceit. What need is there to speak of that? And when he awakened fully to unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening, he declared, 'Having crossed over, I shall bring others across.' What need is there to speak of that?"

Thinking thus, and recalling the pledge the Blessed One had made when he was a bodhisattva, the king spoke these verses:

He who, possessed of supreme intelligence, as soon as he was born
Uttered speech that was free from error,
He who possesses words of truth:
What wise person would not trust him?

He who, at the very moment he was born,
Declared, "I shall become the chief of the world,"
The Protector who spoke words beyond compare:
What wise person would not trust him?

He who, even at the time when dreams are dreamt,
Never once spoke a false word,
Who did just as he had said:
What wise person would not trust him?

He whom heaps of gold as large as snow mountains
Could not cause to become attached,
He who has passed wholly beyond attachment:
What wise person would not trust him?

He whom harsh words like sharpened weapons
Could not cause to become angry,
He who has gone far beyond the very stuff of aversion:
What wise person would not trust him?

He whom what breeds attachment, aversion, and delusion
Was never able to overcome,
He who is endowed with the king of wisdom:
What wise person would not trust him?

He whom the many joys and pleasures of desire
And all the ways of fancying enjoyments
Were never able to bind:
What wise person would not trust him?

He whom the eight kinds of marvels
Could never make cling to amazement,
Endowed with dense splendor and never dejected:
What wise person would not trust him?

He whom the sweet words spoken by the learned,
With many and various kinds of phrases,
Were never able to bind:
What wise person would not trust him?

He whom those striving in the ways of meaning,
And words that fix on faith in the Dharma and on longing,
Were never able to bind:
Who would not trust his words?

He who, stainless and possessed of infinite strength,
When he went forth from the supreme city,
Could not be stopped by armies:
Who would not trust his words?

He who, longing for supreme awakening,
Abandoned the pleasures of desire
And, for the sake of the deathless, lived the life of a wild deer:
Who would not trust his words?

The being free of clinging who, longing for supreme awakening,
Practiced fierce austerities for six years:
Who would not trust his words?

He who, for the benefit and happiness of the world,
In his quest for supreme awakening,
Took broth at the end of six full years:
Who would not trust his words?

He whom Māra, for six years,
Constantly followed, seeking an opening,
Yet in whom he found no opening at all:
Who would not trust his words?

He who is free of attachment and aversion,
Who asks nothing at all of anyone,
And who has worked for the benefit of the world:
Who would not trust his words?

He who, without hearing it from another,
Awakened fully to unsurpassed awakening,
Hard to realize and utterly peaceful:
Who would not trust his words?

When, at the entreaty of Brahmā himself,
The Blessed One heeded his words
And himself expounded the Dharma:
Who would not trust his words?

When he, out of loving concern for me,
Has come home to the city of the Śākyas
And brings liberation to the assembly of Śākyas:
Who would not trust his words?

When the Blessed One, who has crossed over,
Recalls the pledge he made long ago
And delivers us from the stream of existence:
Who would not trust his words?

At this time the moment has come; knowing the time in this way,
The Blessed One knew that it was the time,
The Guide who shows compassion at the proper time.

who would not believe his words?
"To behold the supreme body
Of the Leader of humankind, I must go."
So the king reflected
Within his own mind.

Then King Śuddhodana, having reflected in this way for a long while, said to the venerable Udāyin, "Monk, what do you need?" When he had said this, the venerable Udāyin answered King Śuddhodana in verse:

"Thinking of your welfare, O King,
I have come here to inspire faith in you.
If they give rise to faith in the one with the ten powers,
Men and women alike will go to the higher realms.

He has the ten powers and boundless qualities,
The great sage who brings joy to the Śākyas.
He has come here. King, give rise to faith,
And your fame will spread without measure in the world.

Your son is the supreme lord of men,
With the ten powers, compassion and great love.
Like a lotus in the water, free of attachment,
The Ten-Powered One acts with a mind unattached.

He has crossed the four rivers of existence
And ferries many beings across the great flood.
Since the chief of two-footed beings bestows fearlessness,
Give rise to faith in the Leader, supreme among humans.

He has drawn out all four thorns of existence;
As chief of physicians he pulls out the thorns of beings.
He is the most excellent of skilled healers.
Give rise to faith in the Leader, supreme among humans.

He defeated the measureless great hosts of Māra
And utterly crushed the dark side with all its armies.
Since he has attained the supremely peaceful awakening,
Give rise to faith in the Leader, supreme among humans.

'Free beings, teach the Dharma,
And explain to humans the deathless peace':
Since the lord of gods made this entreaty to the lord of men,
Give rise to faith in the Leader, supreme among humans.

He overpowered the hosts of opponents;
The Teacher set turning the measureless wheel.
Since he tames countless millions of beings,
Give rise to faith in the Leader, supreme among humans.

He whose eyes are pure, for beings
Whose eyes are veiled by darkness and thick cataracts,
Cleanses the eye, removes the cataract and brings clarity.
Give rise to faith in the Leader, supreme among humans.

To those tormented by the fear of aging and death
He teaches the supreme state free of aging and death,
And he leads beings to the deathless abode.
Give rise to faith in the Leader, supreme among humans.

On this world scorched by the three great fires,
Like a great cloud's rain on fire-burnt ground,
He pours the rain of the excellent cloud of the four and the two.
Give rise to faith in the Leader, supreme among humans.

He has cast off the three stains and cleared away all faults;
He frees people from the three stains,
And the Ten-Powered One brings forth illumination.
Give rise to faith in the Leader, supreme among humans.

Loving all beings as a father does,
The compassionate Ten-Powered One regards them with loving-kindness.
Since with compassion and love he sets beings free,
Give rise to faith in the Leader, supreme among humans.

The Blessed One tames beings who are untamed;
The compassionate one brings peace to those without peace,
And calms the afflictions even of the agitated.
Give rise to faith in the Leader, supreme among humans.

He dries up the ocean of existence for beings;
Just as a son of the gods rescues from the ocean,
The Ten-Powered One with great compassion ferries them across.
Give rise to faith in the Leader, supreme among humans.

Adorned with hosts of boundless qualities,
With great compassion he acts from a mind of clear faith.
Since he brings faith to people long troubled,
Give rise to faith in the Leader, supreme among humans.

Like a precious jewel that clears the water,
The Victor acts to make people clear,
And he fully clears the strife and turbidity of humans.
Give rise to faith in the Leader, supreme among humans.

A precious jewel, by nature wholly pure,
Gives rise to full delight in the wise.
Just so the Victor, his mind pure with every fault removed,
Gives rise to full delight in the wise.

When they gain faith in the Lord of the World,
Gods and humans cast off even the burden of suffering;
Abandoning the stream of existence, they long for supreme peace.
Give rise to faith in the Leader, supreme among humans.

When I speak of but a portion of his qualities,
It is like a garuḍa tracing a path in the expanse of the sky;
I have not reached the end of the Victor's qualities.
Give rise to faith in the Leader, supreme among humans."

Then King Śuddhodana heard this fine discourse from the venerable Udāyin, and he himself had directly seen such qualities as the Blessed One had displayed while a bodhisattva. He also recalled the promise that had been made: "Having first crossed over myself, I shall ferry you across as well, O bull among men." At that moment he gained faith in the Blessed One. With a mind that had gained faith in this way, he spoke these words to the venerable Udāyin: "Monk, since you are a son born of my son's Dharma, eat your meal, then take the Blessed One's food and go. I too shall come afterward to see the Blessed One."

Then the venerable Udāyin, having thus brought King Śuddhodana to clear faith, ate his meal, took the Blessed One's food, and departed. At that time the Blessed One said to the monks, "Monks, King Śuddhodana has been brought to faith by the monk Udāyin. Today this will be for the benefit of a great many people."

Then the venerable Udāyin, carrying the Blessed One's food, went to where the Blessed One was and reported to the Blessed One all that he had done. The Blessed One then gave his approval to the venerable Udāyin, saying, "Excellent, Udāyin, excellent! Today, by bringing King Śuddhodana to faith, you have generated a great deal of merit."

At that time the Blessed One said to the monks, "Monks, as for the heap of merit that the monk Udāyin has generated today by bringing King Śuddhodana to clear faith, if it had any form, it would not fit even into world systems as numerous as the sands of the river Ganges in the world systems of the ten directions. So immeasurable is that heap of merit."

The second fascicle.

Then the Blessed One, having eaten his meal, withdrew his hand, washed his alms bowl, and displayed a sign of the following kind.

When the Blessed One (the Buddha) had made such a sign, the Great King Vaiśravaṇa, surrounded and preceded by a hundred thousand koṭis of niyutas of yakṣas, vanished from his own abode. In the time it takes a strong man to stretch out his bent arm or to bend his outstretched arm, he appeared before the Blessed One. He bowed his head at the Blessed One's feet and took up his place on the northern side. There, in the sky above, surrounded by his host of yakṣas, he remained with palms joined, making offerings to the Blessed One and to the Saṅgha of monks.

The Great King Dhṛtarāṣṭra, too, surrounded and preceded by many hundreds of thousands of koṭis of niyutas of gandharvas, appeared before the Blessed One just as before. He bowed his head at the Blessed One's feet and took up his place on the eastern side. There, in the sky above, surrounded by his host of gandharvas, he remained with palms joined, making offerings to the Blessed One and to the Saṅgha of monks.

The Great King Virūḍhaka, too, surrounded and preceded by many hundreds of thousands of koṭis of niyutas of kumbhāṇḍas, appeared before the Blessed One just as before. He bowed his head at the Blessed One's feet and took up his place on the southern side. There, in the sky above, surrounded by his host of kumbhāṇḍas, he remained with palms joined, making offerings to the Blessed One and to the Saṅgha of monks.

The Great King Virūpākṣa, too, surrounded and preceded by many hundreds of thousands of koṭis of niyutas of nāgas, appeared before the Blessed One just as before. He bowed his head at the Blessed One's feet and took up his place on the western side. There, in the sky above, surrounded by his host of nāgas, he remained with palms joined, making offerings to the Blessed One and to the Saṅgha of monks.

Śakra (Indra), lord of the gods, too, surrounded and preceded by many hundreds of thousands of koṭis of niyutas of gods of the Heaven of the Thirty-Three, remained just as before in the sky above with palms joined, making offerings to the Blessed One and to the Saṅgha of monks.

In the same way, Suyāma, king of the gods, Santuṣita, Sunirmita, Vaśavartin, king of the gods, Brahmā, lord of the Sahā world, the gods of Ābhāsvara, Śubhakṛtsna and Bṛhatphala, the king of the gods, and the gods of the Pure Abodes all vanished from their divine realms. They appeared before the Blessed One and bowed their heads at his feet. Then, in the sky above, each of them together with many hundreds of thousands of koṭis of niyutas of attendant gods remained making offerings to the Blessed One and to the Saṅgha of monks.

Vemacitrin, lord of the asuras, together with sixty niyutas of asuras, also vanished from the abode of the asuras, in the time it takes a strong man to stretch out his bent arm or to bend his outstretched arm. He appeared before the Blessed One, bowed his head at his feet, and remained making offerings to the Blessed One and to the Saṅgha of monks.

Eighty-six thousand garuḍa kings, too, vanished from their own abodes. They appeared before the Blessed One, bowed their heads at his feet, and remained making offerings to the Blessed One and to the Saṅgha of monks.

Eighty koṭis of other non-Buddhist wandering ascetics (parivrājakas), who had come from various kingdoms, also gathered there.

On this it is said:

In the center of the expanse of the utterly pure sky,
the full moon, complete without remainder,
outshines the hosts of stars
and shines in beauty in the sky as the moon.

He whose face is like the stainless disk of the moon
dispels darkness and dimness of sight and destroys the three stains;
outshining the hosts of logicians,
he is beautiful like the lord of stars in the sky.

The sun, bearing a body of a hundred rays,
dispels the darkness in autumn and shines in beauty;
outshining the hosts of asuras,
that sun also opens the mouths of the lotuses.

He whose body is like the sun's hundred rays
outshines the three thousand worlds;
the sun among humans shines in beauty
and opens the supreme lotus grove of the śrāvakas.

Śakra (Indra), king of the gods, their lord,
in his assembly hall encircled by hosts of gods,
outshining the divine retinue,
the Bountiful One, with Śacī and his other consorts, shines in beauty.

The lord of bipeds, dense with glory, with the ten powers,
honored at the head of the hosts of renunciants in the three thousand worlds,
outshining the hosts of logicians,
shines in beauty amid the hosts of renunciants.

The lord of gods who rules the Yāma gods,
Suyāma, worshiped and revered by the gods,
Suyāma, having entered among the hosts of gods,
dwells in beauty among the Yāmas.

He of boundless splendor, who has the ten powers,
outshining those who go by evil paths to evil destinies,
fully teaches the eightfold path
and shines in beauty with the qualities of the ten powers.

Santuṣita, king of the gods,
encircled by hosts of gods in the abode of Tuṣita,
outshining many hosts of gods,
is beautiful through his former roots of virtue.

Likewise the teacher of gods and asuras,
whom the hosts of gods and asuras have made their teacher,
outshining the eight assemblies,
the sublime lion among men graces the world.

Sunirmita, lord and sovereign of the gods,
outshining many hosts of gods,
dwells in the divine abode by the power of his own merit,
and dwelling there he is utterly beautiful.

Likewise the one fully liberated from the afflictions,
possessed of the ten powers, free of the afflictions,
the king of discipline who tames the untamed,
outshines all beings and shines in beauty.

Just as the king of the gods who wields control,
dwelling in the divine abode and outshining the hosts of gods,
through the splendor of his former roots of virtue
has come to shine in beauty,

so the Victor, having long ago practiced
the conduct of the ten powers, outshines gods and humans;
through the splendor of his former roots of virtue
that king is utterly beautiful.

In the Brahmā world, Brahmā too
outshines the retinue of Brahmā by himself;
he speaks words most melodious, endowed with eight qualities,
and is manifestly beautiful.

Just so the Victor, who has become like Brahmā,
king of renunciants, honored at the head by the hosts of renunciants,
speaks words of truth
and is manifestly beautiful in the three worlds.

Knowing that gods and humans eager to listen
and the great mahoragas have gathered,
the bull among men, with speech endowed with eight qualities,
teaches them thoroughly.

It may be possible to measure the sky, the path of birds;
it may be possible to measure the ocean with a vase;
it may be possible to weigh Mount Meru by hundreds of weights;
but the qualities of the Buddha cannot be fathomed to their end.

This is the first chapter, on the king being inspired to faith.

Then, not long after the venerable Udāyin had gone, King Śuddhodana addressed the Śākyas: "Friends, it is like this. The harbinger of the sun's rising is the red glow of dawn. When the red glow has appeared, one knows that the sun will rise. Friends, in the same way, the monk Udāyin is the harbinger of the Blessed One, the Omniscient One. Whatever qualities the monk Udāyin has described are certainly the qualities of an omniscient one. Therefore, friends, make ready a thousand of the finest vehicles. And why? Because this very day we shall go to see the Blessed One, who is supremely beautiful to behold."

Saying, "Excellent, Your Majesty," the Śākyas obeyed King Śuddhodana and quickly harnessed the finest conveyances. At that time King Śuddhodana had the whole way between Kapilavastu and the Nyagrodha Park, the route of the king's procession, made level and strewn with clean sand. It was made delightful, filled with scattered flowers, and adorned with silk pennants and many garlands, and at intervals there were stationed dancers, singers, wrestlers, players of pot drums, cymbals, musical instruments, and accompaniments for song, and people carrying every kind of provision.

Then King Śuddhodana, together with those who had set out with the finest vehicles, himself mounted the finest vehicle, came forth from the great city of Kapilavastu, and went to where the Nyagrodha Park was. At that time the finest vehicles of some of the Śākyas were of this kind. The chariots were all blue and drawn by blue horses. Their ornaments too were blue, adorned with nets of bells and little bells, and encircled with blue rim ornaments. They were furnished with parasols, swords, helmets, jeweled yak-tail whisks, and blue foot ornaments. Attendants in blue cloth with uncut fringes and wearing blue ornaments waited upon them, carrying whips with blue ornaments, and there were many guides. Every one of them raised blue banners, and each chariot was surrounded by a hundred men wearing blue ornaments. The vehicles of other Śākyas were of this kind: the chariots were entirely yellow, and likewise entirely red, entirely white, and entirely multicolored, all as before, up to "each chariot was surrounded by a hundred men wearing multicolored ornaments."

Behind each of those chariots were arrayed eighty thousand elephants, every one adorned with golden ornaments, bearing golden banners and covered with golden nets. On those eighty thousand elephants were placed eighty thousand multistoried houses of the seven precious substances, bright and beautiful to behold, and so they proceeded. Behind those eighty thousand elephants were arrayed eighty thousand horses adorned with golden ornaments, hung with strings of golden bells and little bells, and decked with ornaments of the seven precious substances, and so they proceeded.

Then the Blessed One (the Buddha) saw King Śuddhodana and the Śākyas coming from far away, and he said to the monks, "Monks, those of you who have never seen the gods of the Thirty-Three going to their pleasure grove should look at these Śākyas coming from Kapilavastu. Why is that? Monks, these Śākyas who have come out of Kapilavastu are like the gods of the Thirty-Three going to their pleasure grove."

On this it is said:

From the excellent city known as Kapila,
King Śuddhodana, that bull among men,
Has come together with his retinue.
I shall tell how it was; listen to it!

With a hundred thousand excellent chariots,
Well adorned and beautifully decorated,
From the excellent city of Kapilavastu
The excellent lord of men came forth.

The chariots were blue, their ornaments blue,
The horses blue, with a retinue in blue,
The five royal insignia were also blue,
And likewise the fine garments were blue.
The whips were blue, the reins were blue as well,
Together with their nets of bells and little bells;
The people too were clad in blue garments,
Made beautiful by the blue banners they raised.

The chariots were yellow, their ornaments yellow,
The horses yellow, with a retinue in yellow,
The five royal insignia were also yellow,
And likewise the fine garments were yellow.
The whips were yellow, the reins were yellow as well,
Together with their nets of bells and little bells;
The people too were clad in yellow garments,
Made beautiful by the yellow banners they raised.

The chariots were red, their ornaments red,
The horses red, with a retinue in red,
The five royal insignia were also red,
And likewise the fine garments were red.
The whips were red, the reins were red as well,
Together with their nets of bells and little bells;
The people too were clad in red garments,
Made beautiful by the red banners they raised.

The chariots were white, their ornaments white,
The horses white, with a retinue in white,
The five royal insignia were also white,
And likewise the fine garments were white.
The whips were white, the reins were white as well,
Together with their nets of bells and little bells;
The people too were clad in white garments,
Made beautiful by the white banners they raised.

The chariots were multicolored, their ornaments multicolored,
The horses multicolored, with a retinue in many colors,
The five royal insignia were also multicolored,
And likewise the fine garments were multicolored.
The whips were multicolored, the reins multicolored as well,
Together with their nets of bells and little bells;
The people too were clad in multicolored garments,
Made beautiful by the multicolored banners they raised.

Behind all of those chariots
A full eighty thousand elephants were arrayed,
Handsomely adorned with golden banners;
The great elephants were adorned with plates of gold.
On top of them were placed
Multistoried houses of the seven precious substances,
And, adorned with ornaments of every kind,
They were arrayed to follow behind the chariots.

Likewise eighty thousand excellent horses
Were delightfully adorned with ornaments of the seven precious substances;
With royal trappings and a royal gait,
They followed behind those elephants.

Loose flowers of five colors, most fragrant,
Were strewn along the royal road.
Silken pendants were hung all about,
And it was handsomely adorned with vessels of incense.
Singers and dancers, and wrestlers,
And those bearing pot-drums made it beautiful.
And those who played music of every kind,
Overjoyed, followed behind the king.

This was the second chapter, on the king's departure.

Then King Śuddhodana and the Śākyas rode in their vehicles as far as the place where horses are dismounted. Having arrived there, they got down from their vehicles and, surrounded by a retinue on foot that was arrayed before them, went on to the Nyagrodha Park.

At that time the Blessed One, knowing that King Śuddhodana was very proud, rose into the sky, a little more than a man's height above the ground, and walked and remained there in order to inspire faith in King Śuddhodana. As the Blessed One walked thus, on his right Brahmā, lord of the Sahā world, walked in attendance, and on his left Śakra (Indra), lord of the gods, walked in attendance. Attending the Blessed One as he walked, the god kings Suyāma, Santuṣita, Sunirmita, and Vaśavartin held parasols and waved fans in order to honor the Blessed One. The Great Kings Vaiśravaṇa and Dhṛtarāṣṭra, at the near end of the eastern side of the Blessed One's walkway, draped their upper robes over one shoulder, set their right knees on the ground, bowed toward the Blessed One with palms joined, paid homage to the Blessed One, and remained there. The Great Kings Virūḍhaka and Virūpākṣa, at the near end of the western side of the Blessed One's walkway, draped their upper robes over one shoulder, set their right knees on the ground, bowed toward the Blessed One with palms joined, paid homage to the Blessed One, and remained there.

At that time the young gods of the realm of the Four Great Kings, and the young gods of the Thirty-Three, of Yāma, of Tuṣita, of Nirmāṇarati, and of Paranirmitavaśavartin, in order to honor the Blessed One, stood in the sky above and scattered divine flowers: blue lotuses, red lotuses, white water lilies, and white lotuses, divine mandārava flowers and great mandārava flowers, and divine powdered incense.

To make offerings to the Blessed One (the Buddha), divine music, cymbals and songs resounded from the sky above. Melodies of song beyond the human also came forth from the sky above. From the clouds fell a gentle, drizzling rain of perfume. A rain of divine utpala flowers and of divine sandalwood powder fell as well.

Then the Blessed One displayed a miracle of such a kind that gods and humans could see one another.

Then King Śuddhodana saw those gods making offerings to the Blessed One, and he saw the sky filled with tiered mansions made of the seven divine precious substances. He thought: "How wonderful it is, how well the Blessed One spoke! Long ago, when the Blessed One was a youth, he said, 'For me to hold sovereignty over the four great continents would be no great wonder. In the great trichiliocosm, among gods and humans, I shall hold sovereignty as lord of the Dharma.' This, it seems, is the Blessed One's sovereignty as lord of the Dharma. My retinue is only of humans, while the Blessed One has a retinue of gods and humans."

Then King Śuddhodana arranged his upper robe over one shoulder, set his right knee on the ground, bowed his head at the feet of the Blessed One, and spoke these words: "As soon as the Blessed One was born, he walked seven steps upon the great earth unsupported by anyone. Looking to the four directions, he declared, 'I am supreme in the world. I am chief in the world. I am foremost in the world. I shall put an end to birth, aging, sickness and death.' At that time too I touched my head to the Blessed One's feet and paid homage. When the Blessed One went to the farmers' village and sat in the cool shade of the jambu tree, even when the sun had moved on, the jambu tree's shade did not leave the Blessed One's body, and six young sons of the gods remained there, palms joined, paying homage. At that time too I paid homage to the Blessed One. And now, with this, I bow to the Blessed One for the third time."

Having said this, he then also spoke these verses:

"When you, chief of two-footed beings, were born,
You walked seven steps upon this great earth
And declared, 'I am supreme in the world.'
Then, wise one, I bowed to you.
When, great sage, you went to the farmers' village,
The jambu's shade did not leave you as you sat,
And six young sons of the gods bowed down.
Then, worthy of offerings, I bowed to you.
And now, with this, for the third time,
I bow, O sage, at your feet.
Greatly compassionate one, chief honored by gods and humans,
There is none like you in the world, let alone one greater.
That your name was Siddhārtha, as was said before,
Your father and mother gave you after reflection.
Since your aims are fulfilled and you have found the deathless nectar,
Only now do I understand that you are Siddhārtha."

Then the gods of the desire realm arranged a lion throne for the Blessed One, spread with divine wish-fulfilling fine cloths variously interwoven. The entire Nyagrodha Park, too, they carpeted with divine wish-fulfilling fine cloths, and above it they stretched a canopy of divine wish-fulfilling fine cloth.

Then the Blessed One descended from where he stood in the sky and sat upon the throne that had been arranged. King Śuddhodana and his retinue bowed their heads at the Blessed One's feet and sat to one side. The gods of the desire realm and the gods of the form realm also bowed their heads at the Blessed One's feet and remained in the sky.

In this regard, this is the nature of things, and of it this is said:

In the excellent city called Kapilavastu,
in the delightful Nyagrodha Grove,
at the time when King Śuddhodana
met the Protector of the World,
a great multitude of beings gathered there.
Asuras and the lords of the garuḍas,
hosts of nāgas and likewise kumbhāṇḍas,
and the kings of the gandharvas also gathered.
Likewise the lords of the secret ones, the yakṣas, gathered,
with hosts of kinnaras and with human beings,
and likewise the gods who dwell in the realms of desire,
filled with great joy, gathered as well.
Likewise, of those belonging to the form realm,
the Brahmās and the gods of Brahmā's retinue,
the Ābhāsvara gods and the Śubhakṛtsna gods,
those of Bṛhatphala and of the Pure Abodes gathered.
Śramaṇas and likewise brahmins,
carakas and wandering ascetics,
naked ascetics and many other non-Buddhists (tīrthikas),
followers of wrong paths, also gathered.
Through the sublime splendor of the Buddha himself,
for the sake of bringing to full maturity
the mind of the great King Śuddhodana,
at that time a great assembly came about.

Before this, the Blessed One with his well-spoken words
had brought the assemblies to complete satisfaction.
Now the Victor wished to fully display
the might of a Victor to King Śuddhodana.
For King Śuddhodana had once entreated the Blessed One:
"Young prince, since the wise have prophesied
that you will become a king, a great lord of the earth,
do not go forth from your home!
You will be a wheel-turning monarch ruling the four continents,
honored by countless myriads of living beings.
You will possess the seven precious things,
and you will also possess the four kinds of prosperity.
Abiding in the Dharma and protecting the earth,
a great kṣatriya king ruling the four continents,
you will be a lord of men, honored by men,
and greatly honored by the mahoragas." So he had pleaded.

Then at that time the bodhisattva said:
"For immeasurable, countless myriads of eons
I have always spoken words of truth.
O king, listen to my words.
Since in this way you wish to beguile me
with the kingship of the four continents,
great king, in a course such as that
place not the slightest hope in me.
Great king, long ago it happened
that a wave of the ocean flung up a turtle.
When the ocean wave drew back again,
that turtle fell down into a well.
Then the turtle living in the well
said, 'Turtle of the ocean, from where have you come here?
To cut through the doubt that has arisen in me,
tell me quickly!' This is what it said.
To that very turtle in the well
the great-minded turtle of the ocean replied:
'I was carried along and along by the ocean's waves,
and today I have been dropped into this well.
That great ocean wave has drawn back and gone,
and I am not able to go back again.
Since I have been parted from the waves,
turtle, I shall stay here in your well.'
Then the turtle in the well
said, 'Is that ocean only the size of this well,
or is it something larger than this well?
Answer my question quickly!'
The ocean turtle of vast mind
spoke these words to the turtle of the well:
'Turtle of the well, you are childish,
narrow of mind and very feeble in understanding.
I was born in those salty waters,
and though I have been born there again and again for ages,
if I have not been able to know the ocean's shore,
how could I possibly know what its middle is like?'
In the same way, you too, lord of men,
have not understood my might,
and with the honors of the four continents
you wish to beguile me.
I shall become a righteous king, a king of Dharma.
Without punishing by penalties, without driving by weapons,
I shall protect this trichiliocosm world system,
and I shall obtain honor beyond the world."

The Blessed One, whose very nature is the cultivated power of miracles,
wishing to make good his former words,
therefore turned his mind
toward those great assemblies and toward beings.
In order to delight the mind of King Śuddhodana,
he urged them on with his mind,
and the three worlds, entirely and without remainder,
at that time gathered there.

This is the third chapter, on inspiring the king's aspiration.

Then at that time the Blessed One produced such a sign that the thought of making offerings to the Blessed One first arose in Vemacitrin, lord of the asuras. Then Vemacitrin, lord of the asuras, together with six trillion asuras and six trillion asura maidens, was the first to make offerings to the Blessed One. Then all the jewels in the great ocean, as many as there were, both those that had a price and those beyond price, they presented in order to make offerings to the Blessed One. They also magically emanated other jewels not found in that great ocean, and presented them as offerings. They also emanated six trillion chariots made of the seven precious substances. For each of those chariots they also emanated six trillion thoroughbred horses. Those horses all bore nets of bells and small bells of the seven precious substances, had leg ornaments made of gold, were adorned with the seven precious substances, had plumes made of the seven precious substances, and were decked with golden ornaments. To serve as adornment for those thoroughbreds, they also emanated canopies of the seven precious substances. Those chariots too all had golden banners and were decked with golden ornaments. Those canopies of wish-fulfilling jewels, moreover, followed behind the chariots in the sky above: when the chariots moved on, they moved after them, and when the chariots stopped, they all stopped. To beautify those canopies of wish-fulfilling jewels as well, they arranged hanging fringes of garlands of the seven precious substances. Those canopies of wish-fulfilling jewels were also emanated with nets of bells and small bells of the seven precious substances.

When the wind stirred those chariot ornaments, those horse ornaments, and the ornaments of the canopies of wish-fulfilling jewels, nothing but sweet, delightful sounds arose, pleasant to hear. Just as music of instruments and cymbals with eight parts, played by a skilled musician, gives forth sweet and delightful sounds that are pleasant to hear, so too, when the wind stirred those chariot ornaments, those horse ornaments, and the ornaments of the canopies of wish-fulfilling jewels, nothing but sweet, delightful sounds arose, pleasant to hear.

The height of those chariots covered sixty yojanas of the sky above. Behind those chariots followed all kinds of music and cymbals. In every one of those chariots sat asura maidens adorned with every kind of ornament. To make offerings to the Blessed One, some played music, some struck cymbals, some sang songs, and some danced. Some scattered powdered sandalwood and utpala, some scattered powdered aloeswood and tamāla leaves, some scattered the powder of the divine substances of the asuras, and some scattered gold and silver powder. Some emanated mandārava flowers, great mandārava flowers, mañjūṣaka flowers, great mañjūṣaka flowers, paruṣaka flowers, great paruṣaka flowers, white-hued flowers, great white-hued flowers, pale-hued flowers, great pale-hued flowers, variegated pale-hued flowers, and greatly variegated pale-hued flowers, and scattered them over the Blessed One. Some scattered flowers of gold, flowers of silver, flowers of crystal, flowers of beryl, flowers of agate, flowers of red pearl, and flowers of emerald. Some filled their hands with the seven precious substances and with treasures and scattered them over the Blessed One. Some offered garments and ornaments. Some scattered girdles, anklets, bracelets, finger rings, wristlets, paired wristlets, three-pointed head ornaments, armlets, diadems, golden threads, pearl necklaces, jewel necklaces, long necklaces, and many-stranded necklaces, and all kinds of neck ornaments.

Some held strings of tinkling ornaments, some head ornaments, some earrings and rings, some golden garlands studded with jewels, some jeweled garlands studded with gold, some golden nets, and some garlands of gold, garlands of jewels, and garlands of pearls. Some held parasols of gold, parasols of silver, parasols of beryl, parasols of crystal, parasols of agate, parasols of emerald, parasols of red pearl, and parasols of the seven precious substances, all manner of different jeweled victory banners, and many flags of every jewel in various hues and of many colors. Some remained there sending down a rain of scented water. Some praised the Blessed One with these verses:

Homage to you, supreme among humans!
Homage to you, most excellent of humans!
Homage to the lord of two-legged beings!
You have no equal whatsoever.
Homage to the light of the world!
Homage to the ocean of wisdom!
Homage to the glorious one!
Homage to the light of the Victors!
Homage to the one perfect in qualities!
Homage to the dispeller of afflictions!
Homage to you whose conduct is pure!
Homage to you whose mind is pure!
You have no stagnation and no discouragement,
You have no laziness and no agitation.
Having taken up diligence, steadfast,
Homage to the one of certain realization!

They also emanated sixty niyutas of jewel palace compounds, lovely to behold and beautiful, made of the seven precious substances. In each of those jewel palace compounds they also emanated sixty niyutas of residences. In each of those residences they emanated six thousand bright turrets, lovely to behold, made of the seven precious substances. On each of those turrets they also emanated sixty niyutas of bright multi-storied mansions, lovely to behold, made of the seven precious substances. In each of those multi-storied mansions they also emanated sixty niyutas of bright courtyards, lovely to behold, made of the seven precious substances.

Of the golden courtyards, the beams and rafters were of silver, the lattices and door panels of beryl, the thresholds and lintels of crystal, the pillars of agate, the capitals of red pearl, and the floors entirely of emerald. Of the silver courtyards, the beams and rafters were of gold, the lattices and door panels of beryl, the thresholds and lintels of crystal, the pillars of agate, the capitals of red pearl, and the floors entirely of emerald. Of the beryl courtyards, the beams and rafters were of crystal, the lattices and door panels of gold, the thresholds and lintels of silver, the pillars of agate, the capitals of red pearl, and the floors entirely of emerald. Of the crystal courtyards, the beams and rafters were of beryl, the lattices and door panels of agate, the thresholds and lintels of emerald, the pillars of gold, the capitals of silver, and the floors entirely of red pearl. Of the agate courtyards, the beams and rafters were of emerald, the lattices and door panels of red pearl, the thresholds and lintels of gold, the pillars of silver, the capitals of crystal, and the floors entirely of beryl. Of the red pearl courtyards, the beams and rafters were of agate, the lattices and door panels of emerald, the thresholds and lintels of silver, the pillars of gold, the capitals of beryl, and the floors entirely of chrysoberyl. Of the emerald courtyards, the beams and rafters were of red pearl, the lattices and door panels of gold, the thresholds and lintels of silver, the pillars of beryl, the capitals of chrysoberyl, and the floors entirely of crystal.

In those courtyards, too, they emanated sixty niyutas of lion thrones made of the seven precious substances, spread with the various divine fabrics of the asuras, covered with kācalindika cloth, with fine cushions arranged at both ends.

On the footstools of those lion thrones, made of the seven precious substances, were spread sixty koṭis each of many kinds of cloth: very fine cloth made of cotton, very fine cloth made of silk, very fine cloth made of flax, very fine cloth made of bark fiber, and very fine cloth from Kāśi. On both sides of each of those lion thrones there also appeared two asura maidens adorned with every ornament, holding fans made of the seven precious substances, and they fanned the young asura maidens seated on the lion thrones.

In each of those courtyards there also appeared sixty hundred billion parasols made of the seven precious substances. In each of those courtyards there also appeared sixty hundred billion victory banners of the seven precious substances. In the golden courtyards were raised blue banners on yellow poles adorned with crystal. In the silver courtyards were raised yellow banners on red poles adorned with gold. In the beryl courtyards were raised red banners on yellow poles adorned with crystal. In the crystal courtyards were raised white banners on beryl poles adorned with gold. In the courtyards of various jewels were raised multicolored banners on golden poles adorned with beryl. The blue banners had yellow finials, the yellow banners had blue finials, the red banners had white finials, the white banners had red finials, and the banners of various jewels all had finials of the seven precious substances.

Those jeweled palace grounds floated in the sky and circled the Blessed One three times. It was just as Airāvaṇa, the elephant of the gods of the Heaven of the Thirty-Three, travels through the sky and carries the gods of the Thirty-Three to the pleasure grove of the Pārijāta tree. In the same way, those palace grounds floated in the sky and circled the Blessed One three times.

From those jeweled palace grounds, those halls, those dwellings, those multistoried mansions, those courtyards, those lion thrones, those parasols, those victory banners, and those pennants, there fell a rain of the powder of divine sandalwood, utpala, and aloeswood. There also fell a rain of flowers: mandārava and great mandārava, mañjūṣaka and great mañjūṣaka, paruṣaka and great paruṣaka, white-hued flowers and great white-hued flowers, pale flowers and great pale flowers, variegated pale flowers and greatly variegated pale flowers. There also fell a rain of gold, silver, beryl, crystal, conch, emerald, red pearl, flowers of every kind of jewel, wealth, gold, and wish-fulfilling cloth. By the power of those canopies of wish-fulfilling jewels, whatever jewels those asura youths wished to rain down fell from them.

At that time, around the great city of Kapilavastu, for sixty thousand leagues in circumference and sixty thousand leagues in height, everything was completely filled with such offerings of the asuras. Those jeweled palace grounds circled the Blessed One three times and came to rest before the Blessed One. The great city of Kapilavastu was filled with mandārava flowers to the depth of about seven times a man's height, and it became a mire of scented water.
