Through the power of the Buddha, the great trichiliocosm, the world system of a billion worlds, was pervaded by the fragrance of that mire of scented water. All the sentient beings in this great trichiliocosm who smelled the fragrance of that mire of scented water became irreversible from unexcelled, perfect, complete awakening, to say nothing of those who had first generated the mind set on awakening.

Then Vemacitrin, the lord of the asuras, mounted a chariot of the seven precious substances, circled three times, scattered sandalwood powder over the Blessed One, and praised him in these verses:

"For the sake of seeking supreme awakening,
I go for refuge to you, supreme refuge,
Utterly unshakable, bestower of fearlessness
On the world together with its gods.

Whoever goes to him for refuge with a joyful mind
Does not fall into the lower realms;
I go to him for refuge
For the sake of seeking incomparable awakening.

You who have shattered the sufferings of saṃsāra,
Who have crossed into nirvāṇa, purified of the afflictions,
A guide who relies on no other,
To you I go for refuge.

Blessed One, guide of gods and humans
Who are tormented by aging and death,
Whoever takes refuge in you is not born again;
To you I go for refuge."

Then, as Vemacitrin, the lord of the asuras, circled the Blessed One three times, those ornaments of the horses, those ornaments of the chariots, those ornaments of the asuras, those ornaments of parasols, victory banners, and pennants, and those ornaments of canopies, bells, and little bells, stirred by the wind, gave forth sounds sweet, delightful, and joyful to hear, just like an eightfold ensemble of music played by a skilled musician. Thus, as Vemacitrin, the lord of the asuras, circled the Blessed One three times, there fell from the sky above a rain of the powder of divine sandalwood, utpala, aloeswood, and tamāla leaves. There fell a rain of many kinds of asura incense powder.

There fell a rain of divine flowers: māndārava and great māndārava, mañjūṣaka and great mañjūṣaka, paruṣaka and great paruṣaka, white flowers and great white flowers, pale flowers and great pale flowers, variegated pale flowers and great bright pale flowers, together with a rain of all kinds of emanated flowers. From the sky above there also fell a rain of flowers made of gold, silver, beryl, crystal, musāragalva, emerald, red pearl, and every kind of jewel. A rain of the scented water of the gods and asuras fell as well. As that rain of scented water fell, the land for sixty leagues all around the great city of Kapilavastu became a mire of scented water. The fragrance of that mire of scented water filled the whole of the great trichiliocosm, the thousandfold world system of a thousand million worlds. All the sentient beings who smelled that fragrance became irreversible from unsurpassed, perfect, complete awakening.

When the asura lord Vemacitrin was thus circling the Blessed One three times, the sounds of the divine cymbals of the asuras rang out from the sky. The sound of those cymbals resounded throughout the great trichiliocosm. All the sentient beings who had rightly entered the Great Vehicle and who heard that sound became irreversible from unsurpassed, perfect, complete awakening.

When the asura lord Vemacitrin had thus circled the Blessed One three times, hundreds of thousands of asura maidens, in order to make offerings to the Blessed One and to serve as the retinue of the asura lord Vemacitrin, did the following. Some sang songs. Some played cymbals. Some danced. Some made graceful movements of the body. Some scattered powdered sandalwood, blue lotus, aloeswood, and tamāla leaf. Some scattered divine flowers, māndārava and great māndārava, mañjūṣaka and great mañjūṣaka, paruṣaka and great paruṣaka, white flowers and great white flowers, pale flowers and great pale flowers, variegated pale flowers and great bright pale flowers, and emanated flowers, as well as flowers made of gold, silver, beryl, crystal, musāragalva, emerald, red pearl, and every kind of jewel, strewing them over the Blessed One.

Some scattered garments, anklets, belts, hand ornaments, finger rings, bracelets, heavy bracelets, three-pointed head ornaments, armlets, diadems, gold threads, pearl garlands, jeweled necklaces, double necklaces, chest chains, various neck ornaments, earrings, hoop earrings, strings of little bells, head ornaments, tassels of garlands, tassels of incense, and tassels of jewels; and they held up canopies of flowers, canopies of incense, and canopies of jewels. Some sent down a rain of scented water. Some sent down a rain of fragrant powder. Some filled their cupped hands with red pearls and strewed them over the Blessed One. Some filled their cupped hands with jewels, and some filled their cupped hands with gold, and strewed them over the Blessed One. They also spoke this verse:

Relying on whom, and dwelling with whom,
one comes to make offerings to the Supreme Person,
with such a friend as that
may we always and continually meet.

Just as the asura lord Vemacitrin had done, so too the asura lord Fully Soothed made offerings to the Blessed One, mounted a chariot made of the seven precious substances, circled the Blessed One three times, and praised the Blessed One with these verses:

Homage to you of the ten powers, diligent and true!
Homage to you, fearless one, free from all fear!
Certain in the qualities that are unshared,
Guide of beings, homage to you!

Homage to you who cut the bonds of the fetters!
Homage to you who do not remain in the stream of saṃsāra!
Homage to you who have reached the end and stand firm!
Homage to you who rescue beings who are sinking!

Though you keep company with beings, you have no agitation;
your mind is free in the midst of all beings,
like a lotus unstained by water.
Homage to you of profound mind, beyond conception!

Buddha, Sage, you know emptiness;
in every way you have destroyed all signs;
you have no wishing for anything at all.
Homage to you who abide nowhere, equal to space!

Just as the asura lord Fully Soothed had done, so too the asura lord Excellent Chariot made offerings to the Blessed One, mounted a chariot made of the seven precious substances, circled the Blessed One three times, scattered gold dust, and praised the Blessed One with verses:

On the earth, in the sky, and in the realm of the gods,
there is none equal to you, Sugata, let alone one greater.
Beings who grasp at signs and so fall into error
you know to be like a mirage.

Here there is no agent, no feeler, no sentient being;
there is no person, and nothing to be claimed as mine.
All phenomena are empty and void of any agent;
you teach that they lack intrinsic nature, are unmoving and at peace.

Whoever has faith in this teaching of the Sugata
and follows the truth that all phenomena are unmade
becomes here a child of the Guide,
and practices just as was taught, like the father.

Whatever merit has arisen from my praising you, Guide,
just as the Victor knows it with his wisdom,
all those roots of virtue, entirely and without remainder,
I give here to beings: may all attain buddhahood!

The Third Fascicle.

Just as the asura lord Suratha had done, the asura lord Bali also made offerings to the Blessed One (the Buddha). Mounted on a chariot made of the seven precious substances, he circled the Blessed One three times and scattered silver flowers. Then he praised the Blessed One in these verses:

You have crossed the waters of the ocean of saṃsāra to the far shore;
Having crossed, you ferry over the people who follow the current,
And, Sugata, you set them on the far shore, at ease and free from fear.
Sugata of beautiful eyes, I touch my head to your feet.

Supreme intellect, you know all beings completely.
Those whose minds cling fiercely to the thicket of inverted views,
Who do not know delusion to be like a mirage,
You bring to understanding, moved by incomparable compassion.

Supreme among humans, you have taught that all phenomena are like illusions,
That they resemble mirages and are like tricks of the eye.
Excellent Sage, you have given birth to supreme heirs of the Dharma,
Who practice a conduct unattached to the three realms of existence.

Lord of humans, you know that things are false objects,
Devoid of intrinsic nature and empty of the nature of others.
Seeing the minds of deluded fools as like the joys of a dream,
Lord of humans, you and your heirs pursue your conduct in the grove.

Just as the asura lord Bali had done, the asura lord Rāhu also made offerings to the Blessed One. Mounted on a chariot made of the seven precious substances, he circled the Blessed One three times and scattered flowers of pale and variegated hues. Then he praised the Blessed One in these verses:

Supreme among humans, your body
Outshines this world with its gods,
As Mount Meru outshines a mustard seed,
And the ocean the water in a cow's hoofprint.

Your beautiful form is adorned with the major marks.
By your form you outshine the most excellent of forms
And all forms whatsoever,
As the sun outshines a firefly.

Mass of splendor, by your splendor
You outshine all splendid beings;
Those you outshine no longer glow,
As the sun eclipses the firefly.

Ocean of wisdom, by your wisdom
You outshine all the three worlds;
Those you outshine no longer glow,
As the sun eclipses the light of the moon.

Just as the asura lord Rāhu had done, the asura lord Śambara also made offerings to the Blessed One. Mounted on a chariot made of the seven precious substances, he circled the Blessed One three times and scattered flowers of pale and variegated hues. Then he praised the Blessed One in these verses:

Guide whose intellect is peaceful,
Delighting in calm abiding, you settle
The dust of beings' desire, hatred, and delusion,
As rain settles the dust.

Blazing, you make the way of the Dharma blaze,
Like a fire fed with poured oblations.
As a forest fire let loose burns the trees of the forest,
So you burn up the net of afflictions.

For immeasurable billions upon billions of eons
You have upheld the roots of virtue.
You vanquish all afflictions,
As nectar smeared on the body overcomes poison.

You dispel darkness and gloom,
Like a lamp blazing in a dark cave.
You also reveal the way of the Dharma,
As a jeweler reveals the qualities of gems.

Just as the asura lord Śambara had done, the asura lord Delighting in Battle also made offerings to the Blessed One. Mounted on a chariot made of the seven precious substances, he circled the Blessed One three times and scattered jewel flowers. Then he praised the Blessed One in these verses:

You have tamed the mind, fierce and hard to tame,
Like a hero brave in battle at the front line of war.
Subduing it utterly, you brought it under your own control.
Tamed in mind and fearless one, I bow to you.

You have tamed the senses and conquered the foe;
Fearless and unshaken anywhere, you have found bliss.
You are not stirred even by the afflictions;
In every way you have destroyed the poisons without remainder.

Mighty one, you have cultivated loving-kindness well;
Your mind is the same toward enemy and friend.
Sage, you have overturned the notions of beings;
You do not go into any of the destinies of beings.

Liberated through wisdom, you have dried up the craving for existence;
Your wisdom is pure, and your mind is pure as well.
Your mind is unattached to any phenomenon at all.
Supreme Sage who outshines all, there is none like you.

Just as the asura lord Delighting in Battle had done, the asura lord Sunetra also made offerings to the Blessed One. Mounted on a chariot made of the seven precious substances, he circled the Blessed One three times, presented a great treasure to the Blessed One, and then praised the Blessed One in these verses:

Great hero, you
Know the true characteristic of phenomena.
Even what is spoken of as "dharma"
Is nothing but a conventional designation, a word.

Phenomena, by the essence of phenomena,
Are not apprehended in any way at all.
Even what is spoken of as "dharma"
Is nothing but a name, so you have taught.

A name is void of name,
And so too are characteristics.
Phenomena are without characteristics,
And there is no name either, so you have explained.

Even what is called "explaining fully"
Involves no explaining whatsoever.
Nothing is explained by anyone,
And nothing is explained to anyone.

Your heirs, supreme among humans,
Having understood phenomena in this way,
Conduct themselves without attachment
Even to phenomena, great Sage.

Just as the asura lord Sunetra had done, the asura lord Subduer of the Three Worlds also made offerings to the Blessed One. Mounted on a chariot made of the seven precious substances, he circled the Blessed One three times, presented a pearl necklace to the Blessed One, and then praised the Blessed One in these verses:

Matchless one without a rival, I praise you.
In moral discipline, in form, and in the power of wisdom
You have no equal in the three worlds;
Therefore, vanquisher of foes, you are unequaled.

Since your form was produced by the Dharma,
By the power of your form you outshine the three realms.
Through the power of generosity your major marks are perfect;
Your minor marks are perfect and your form is pure.
Through the power of moral discipline you are unshakable.

even the learned cannot, each for themselves, conceive of it.
You are endowed with moral discipline, and your mind is pure as well.
Lion among men, you have accomplished every deed.
In the power of wisdom there is no one at all your equal.
Undaunted there, having subdued the three worlds,
Roaring like a lion in the assembly,
You overwhelm the intellects of the hosts of sophists.

Just as the asura lord Subjugator of the Three Worlds had done, so too the asura lord Vairocana made offerings to the Blessed One (the Buddha). He mounted a chariot of the seven kinds of precious substances, circled the Blessed One three times, filled his cupped hands with jewels and scattered them, and then praised the Blessed One in verse:

Great Sage, this gathering of people
Gazes upon your face with a mind of faith;
Each of them sees the Victor seated before them.
This is the unique mark of the Victor, the Buddha.

When the Blessed One utters a single speech,
People each understand it according to their own inclinations.
Sage, your speech arises in just that way.
This is the unique mark of the Victor, the Buddha.

When the Blessed One utters a single speech,
The assembly understands it as many different speeches,
And beings understand it according to their own aims.
This is the unique mark of the Victor, the Buddha.

By uttering a single speech,
You establish one thing and refute another.
Guide, you bring the untrained to understanding.
This is the unique mark of the Victor, the Buddha.

Just as the asura lord Vairocana had done, so too the asura lord Release-and-Hold made offerings to the Blessed One. He mounted a chariot of the seven kinds of precious substances, circled the Blessed One three times, filled his cupped hands with red pearls and scattered them, and then praised the Blessed One in verse:

Those given to intoxication, pride, and sinful views,
When, joyful and deeply faithful, they delight
In the mind of the Sugata, lord of men, become heedful;
Those who render him peerless service are the Teacher's sons.

These phenomena do not exist by way of an essential nature;
They are like all the joys of a dream, which are not valid.
Though the enjoyments of a dream do not exist, perception is there.
In this way the Victor sees all phenomena.

Like autumn clouds and like the moon in water,
They delude the minds of those of childish intellect.
But those who are always heedful, who possess the Sugata's understanding,
The learned, are not deluded by this.

Those who are always heedful in your teaching, O Victor,
Those supreme and excellent among humans, will not fall into unfavorable states.
Having examined all phenomena as being like a dream,
Those who see correctly attain samādhi.

Then those six hundred million asura lords, Vemacitrin and the others, having made such offerings, with minds intent upon awakening, were gratified, satisfied, and delighted, overjoyed, and filled with joy and happiness. Joining their palms, they bowed to the Blessed One himself and sat down before the Blessed One.

Then the Blessed One looked upon the offerings of those asura lords, and, understanding the superior intention of those asuras, at that moment he smiled. It is the nature of reality for the buddhas, the blessed ones, that whenever they smile, rays of light of various colors, of many colors, namely blue, yellow, red, white, crimson, crystal, and silver, issue from the Blessed One's mouth. Those rays filled the entire world with radiance, then returned, circled the Blessed One three times, and vanished into the crown of the Blessed One's head.

Then the venerable Aśvajit rose from his seat, draped his upper robe over one shoulder, and placed his right knee on the ground. He bowed with palms joined toward where the Blessed One (the Buddha) was, and asked the Blessed One in verse the meaning of his smile:

"Without a cause, O you who wish to benefit the world,
The lion among men does not smile.
Great Sage, for what reason have you smiled?
The supreme being also tells the cause of his smile.
Having seen the vast offering of the asuras,
If you who wish to benefit the world now smile,
Please declare a prophecy for the asuras.
When we hear it, we shall rejoice.
Who today, free of doubt, has truly given rise
To a vast mind here in this assembly?
Knowing whose intention has the Tathāgata,
The lion among men, smiled?
Who today, with a fully purified mind,
Has given rise to faith in the supreme among men?
Knowing whose intention has the Tathāgata
Smiled this delightful smile?
Seeing that smile upon the Sugata's face,
This assembly today has come to wonder,
'This smile the Sugata has smiled in this way:
Whose is it that today has vanquished Māra with his army?'
Great Sage, Victor victorious over foes,
Please cut off the uncertainty of those who wonder.
Supreme among men, today, for beings,
Please swiftly and thoroughly cut off their doubt."

Then the Blessed One answered in verse the question the venerable Aśvajit had asked, and spoke again to the venerable Aśvajit:

"Aśvajit, you have asked your question well.
The smile that I smiled for the benefit of the world,
By it I shall show the result in full.
Listen to that teaching with a one-pointed mind.
Wishing for unsurpassed awakening,
The asuras made this offering to me.
Their minds are attached to nothing at all,
Like a palm that clings nowhere in the expanse of the sky.
The hosts of asuras made their minds full of faith,
And because they made this offering to me,
Those intelligent ones will leave the asura class behind,
Go to the higher realms, and rejoice there for long.
Later, having served and honored victors, lions among men,
As many as ten million times the sands of the Ganges River,
In the Delightful Eon,
All of them will become victors sharing a single name.
At that time those sixty trillion sugatas,
Lamps of the world, renowned in every direction,
Will teach the Dharma of non-abiding
And make it clear to the world with its gods.
The fields of those who know the world
Will be very vast and free of degeneration.
At that time the victors, a full sixty trillion,
Will purify the trichiliocosm.
They will dispel all the lower realms,
And beings will live in joy in those fields.
Those steadfast ones will purify all unfavorable states
And teach the unsurpassed vehicle.
As for the measure of their lives,
For a full sixty trillion eons
Those guides will live,
And they will teach the Dharma of non-attachment.
When those sages have passed into nirvāṇa,
When the knowers of the world have entered perfect peace,
For a full sixty trillion eons
The true Dharma of those self-arisen ones will remain.
Those sugatas will tame the hosts of beings,
People as many as the Ganges' sands.
They will share one name, Victor Victorious over Foes,
And their fields, their lifespans, and the duration of their Dharma will be alike.
Those sixty trillion sugatas
Will bring men and women to full maturity
And lead them by the unsurpassed vehicle.
Those wise ones will also uphold the lineage of the victors."

This prophecy that the Victor gave the asuras
The Great Sage spoke for the benefit of the world.
When the world with its gods heard it,
They rejoiced, and strength arose in them as well.

This is the fourth chapter, the Prophecy to the Asuras.

Then the venerable Mahākāśyapa, having seen this kind of offering by the asuras, was amazed and thought, "What sort of roots of virtue did the Tathāgata accumulate long ago, when he engaged in the conduct of a bodhisattva, that such a maturation has come about?" At that moment, settling his mind in such a way that he could recollect even incalculable eons, he entered a meditative absorption of such a kind that he would recollect all of them: the roots of virtue the Blessed One accumulated as he died, transmigrated, and took birth in this and that continuum of existence; the roots of virtue by which he accomplished unsurpassed, perfect, and complete awakening; and the roots of virtue by which he attained irreversibility.

Then the venerable Mahākāśyapa, having recollected the Blessed One's great roots of virtue, thought, "Since the Blessed One has accumulated such great roots of virtue, then even for a single one of his generations of the mind, suppose all the beings dwelling in world systems as numerous as the sands of the Ganges River were to obtain human bodies, and each of them made offerings equal to all the offerings of the asuras for eons as numerous as the sands of the Ganges River. Even so, they could not repay even a single one of his generations of the mind."

Then the venerable Mahākāśyapa arose from that samādhi. Filled with wonder and faith, he praised the Blessed One (the Buddha) at that time in verses:

O Sage, set on awakening,
even beside a single one of your resolves,
this offering of the asuras
does not amount to the smallest part, O Lion among Men.

You who have perfected such qualities,
honored by gods and humans,
Supreme among Humans, you are worthy to be honored
with sandalwood powder heaped as high as Mount Meru.

O Lion among Men, you are worthy
to be honored with scented water
filling oceans to the brim,
oceans more numerous than the sands of the Ganges.

You who possess great qualities are worthy to be honored
with heaps of flowers more numerous than the Ganges' sands,
equal in size to the supreme mountain
and to the great ring of the world's encircling mountains.

O Victor, self-arisen lamp,
you are worthy to be honored with lamps
whose wicks are the size of Mount Meru,
with sesame oil as plentiful as the waters of the ocean.

You who possess great might,
who have thus gone to the far shore of qualities,
are worthy to be honored with flower stupas
more numerous than the sands of the Ganges.

Compassionate one, honored by lords of men,
Supreme among Beings, you are worthy to be honored
with great jeweled parasols
for myriads upon myriads of eons.

You who possess boundless qualities
are worthy to be honored for inconceivable eons
with jeweled victory banners
filling the world realms of the ten directions.

O Lion among Men, you are worthy
to be honored for immeasurable myriads of eons
with multitudes of pennants
more numerous than the sands of the Ganges.

Great Sage, tiger among speakers,
gods and humans,
and whatever other beings are gathered here,
please listen to an illustration I propose.

Suppose all the beings
in the world realms of the ten directions,
as many as the sands of the Ganges,
were buddhas, supreme humans endowed with the ten powers,
and each of them had heads
as many as the Ganges' sands,
and each of those heads
had just as many mouths,
and each of those mouths
had tongues equal in number to the mouths;
even if they were to praise you
for eons as many as the sands of the Ganges River,
those Victors, unflagging in eloquence,
would not reach the end of your qualities.
O Victor, you have gone
to the far shore of such qualities.

Then the Blessed One said to the monks, "Excellent, monks, excellent! My śrāvakas, every one of them, abide as upright, as endowed with wisdom, as having become Dharma, as having gained mastery, as having become reliable authorities. Every one of them enters into the collection of my qualities. Why is that? Monks, the Tathāgata possesses immeasurable qualities; he possesses inconceivable qualities. Monks, the qualities of the Tathāgata, the perfection of the Tathāgata's qualities, and the collection of the Tathāgata's qualities are inconceivable.

"Monks, if the Tathāgata's qualities had form, the collection of qualities of even a single resolve would not fit into world realms as numerous as the sands of the Ganges River. Even if tathāgatas as numerous as the sands of the Ganges River were to try, for eons as numerous as the sands of the Ganges River, to state the collection of qualities of even a single resolve as a measure of number, they could not state it; even if they were to think about it, they could not conceive it. Why is that? When the Tathāgata formerly practiced the conduct of a bodhisattva, there was not a single resolve he generated that was not generated for the benefit and happiness of all beings, however many there are that are counted among beings. Since the realm of beings is immeasurable, merit as vast as the realm of beings arises from each single resolve of the Tathāgata. The realm of beings is inexhaustible; just as the realm of beings is inexhaustible, so inexhaustible merit arises from each single resolve of the Tathāgata. Since that mind arose for the benefit of all beings, even if all beings were to make offerings, they could not repay it. Why is that? Worldly offerings are bound up with material things, whereas the mind of the bodhisattva mahāsattva, which arises for the benefit of all beings, thinking, 'How can these beings who are bent on saṃsāra come to be bent on nirvāṇa?', is free of anything material. When the Tathāgata formerly practiced the conduct of a bodhisattva, he generated for the sake of beings immeasurable resolves free of anything material.

"Monks, long ago, in the past, immeasurable, incalculable, inconceivable, incomparable, unfathomable, inexpressible eons ago, at that time, on that occasion, there appeared in the world a tathāgata, an arhat, a perfectly complete buddha, endowed with knowledge and conduct, a sugata, a knower of the world, a guide who tames beings, unsurpassed, a teacher of gods and humans, a buddha, a blessed one called Indraketu. Monks, with each single resolve, that Tathāgata Indraketu intended to benefit the beings of world realms as numerous as the sands of the Ganges River, and he also accomplished their welfare. The buddha field of the Tathāgata Indraketu, moreover, comprised world realms as numerous as the sands of the Ganges River as a single buddha field. It was completely pure and entirely free of all lower realms and unfavorable states. In that buddha field of the Tathāgata Indraketu there was no birth whatsoever of beings fixed in wrongness. There were no kinds of views whatsoever. There was no one at all with overweening pride. There were no beings at all whose actions of body, speech, and mind were not completely pure. There was no arising whatsoever of the afflictions and karma that become causes of the lower realms. Why is that? Because for a long time that Tathāgata had placed beings in virtuous qualities, established them in them, instructed them, and trained them. Through the Tathāgata, the arhat, the perfectly complete buddha Indraketu, the beings dwelling in that buddha field also attained five kinds of happiness. Thus some came to possess the happiness of sense pleasures.

Some had the happiness of going forth from the household life. Some had the happiness of meditative concentration. Some had the happiness of meditative absorption. Some had the happiness of unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening. They enjoyed that happiness, yet they had no clinging to those kinds of happiness. A bee, for example, lives on the nectar of flowers, yet it has no clinging to it. In the same way, those beings fully enjoyed that happiness, yet they had no clinging to it. A bird that flies through the sky, for example, moves through the sky, yet it has no attachment to it. In the same way, those beings fully enjoyed that happiness, yet they had no clinging to it.

For the beings who dwelt in that buddhafield of the Tathāgata Indraketu, there was no displeasure that made the mind unhappy, and nothing that made them bewildered. Instead, they enjoyed nothing but the joy that makes the mind happy. This was because they had formerly cultivated such roots of virtue. Why was that? When the Tathāgata Indraketu was formerly practicing the conduct of a bodhisattva, he turned those beings away from nonvirtue and set them in virtue. They therefore had no nonvirtuous karma at all of the kind whose ripening comes about helplessly, through which painful feeling is experienced. Because they had not accumulated it, no ripening arose that brought painful feeling. Nor, even with a merely deluded thought, did they create any karma of the kind through which, for the deluded, a feeling that is neither painful nor pleasant arises. Because they had not accumulated it, that deluded feeling which is neither painful nor pleasant did not arise.

In that buddhafield of the Tathāgata Indraketu, the cause by which those beings would suffer from the change of seasons did not occur. So no harsh sun rose there, no harsh wind blew, and no harsh rain fell. When the Tathāgata Indraketu was formerly practicing the conduct of a bodhisattva, he taught and explained to those beings that all actions of body, speech and mind should be preceded by knowledge and should follow upon knowledge. Those beings heard that teaching from the bodhisattva, and they carried out all their actions of body, speech and mind preceded by knowledge and following upon knowledge. Through the ripening of that karma, they had no bewilderment and no unhappiness of mind. They had none of the causes by which, for the deluded, a feeling that is neither pleasant nor painful arises. So in that buddhafield those beings had no painful feeling, and no feeling that is neither pleasant nor painful.

The beings born in that buddhafield, whom that Tathāgata had gathered, lived devoted to the Dharma with great reverence. When they walked, they walked with their thoughts on the nature of reality, with minds intent on the Dharma through their devotion to it. For those who walked with minds devoted to and intent on the Dharma, no painful feeling arose. Likewise, when standing, sitting, lying down or waking, and in every posture, no suffering arose.

Among the beings of that buddhafield there was nothing dreadful left unabandoned. Therefore the suffering of what is dreadful and unabandoned did not arise. Because they had right abandonment, they had no weariness. Therefore they also had no suffering that would need to be rightly abandoned. Those beings had no suffering of change either, because they had no clinging to any phenomena. They had no suffering of meeting with what is unpleasant, because a mind of equality toward all beings was present in them. They had no suffering of separation from what is dear. Why is that? Because they had no clinging to any phenomena. The suffering of separation from what is dear arises from what is dear, and therefore they had no suffering of separation from what is dear. Those beings had no suffering of suffering either, because no painful feeling occurred there. Apart from this there was only the suffering of formations, namely the suffering of impermanence itself.

That Tathāgata taught the ultimate Dharma. Through all the time the Tathāgata Indraketu remained, for eons as many as the sands of the Ganges River, no being ever appeared in that buddhafield who contradicted what he taught, because he had formerly brought those beings fully to maturity.

"Monks, what do you think? With roots of virtue that are weak, low, timid and small, brought about through suffering, mixed with suffering, accompanied by suffering and gripped by suffering, ill-intended and ill-performed, could one act for the benefit of so many beings, or intend their welfare, or bring about their happiness, or remove their suffering, or purify so vast a buddhafield, or bring so many beings to maturity?"

They replied, "No, Blessed One, one could not."

He said, "Monks, what do you think? With a mind that is weak, a mind that is low, and so on up to a mind that is ill-performed, could one act for the benefit of so many beings, or intend their welfare, or bring about their happiness, or remove their suffering, or purify so vast a buddhafield, or bring so many beings to maturity?"

They replied, "No, Blessed One, one could not."

The Blessed One said, "Monks, what do you think? With faith ranging from weak faith to poorly accomplished faith, with moral discipline ranging from weak discipline to poorly accomplished discipline, with learning ranging from weak learning to poorly accomplished learning, with diligence ranging from weak diligence to poorly accomplished diligence, with mindfulness ranging from weak mindfulness to poorly accomplished mindfulness, with meditative absorption ranging from weak absorption to poorly accomplished absorption, and with wisdom ranging from weak wisdom to poorly accomplished wisdom, could one act for the benefit of so many sentient beings, or intend to help them, or bring about their happiness, or remove their suffering, or purify so vast a buddhafield, or bring so many sentient beings to maturity?"

They replied, "No, Blessed One, one could not."

The Blessed One said, "Excellent, monks, excellent! With weak roots of virtue and a weak mind; with weak faith, moral discipline, learning, diligence, mindfulness, meditative absorption, and wisdom; with a wisdom that is inferior, faint-hearted, and small, that is wholly shaped by suffering, mixed with suffering, accompanied by suffering, and in the grip of suffering, a wisdom that is ill-conceived and poorly accomplished, one cannot act for the benefit of so many sentient beings, or intend to help them, or bring about their happiness, or remove their suffering, or purify so vast a buddhafield, or bring so many sentient beings to maturity.

"Monks, what do you think? Who do you suppose was the Tathāgata called Indraketu who appeared at that time, on that occasion?"

When he had said this, the monks sat there without saying anything.

At that time, in the eastern direction, beyond as many world systems as there are grains of sand in the river Ganges, there is a world system called Beautifully Adorned with Moonbeams. There the Tathāgata called King of Exalted Splendor is presently dwelling, living, and abiding, and he teaches the Dharma. Mañjuśrī the youthful, who had gathered there and was seated among the assembly, knew with his mind what the Blessed One intended, and he said this to the Blessed One, the Tathāgata King of Exalted Splendor: "Blessed One, the Tathāgata Śākyamuni is teaching the Dharma in the Sahā world system. I too shall go there in order to serve and honor that Blessed One Śākyamuni."

When he had said this, the Blessed One, the Tathāgata King of Exalted Splendor, spoke these words to Mañjuśrī the youthful: "Mañjuśrī, if you know that the time has come, then go."

Then Mañjuśrī the youthful rose from his seat, bowed his head at the feet of the Blessed One, the Tathāgata King of Exalted Splendor, and circumambulated him three times. Then, in the time it takes a strong man to stretch out his bent arm or to bend his outstretched arm, Mañjuśrī the youthful vanished from that world system Beautifully Adorned with Moonbeams and came to be here, in the presence of the Blessed One Śākyamuni. He bowed his head at the feet of the Blessed One and sat down to one side. Seated to one side, Mañjuśrī the youthful said this to the Blessed One:

"Blessed One, when the Blessed One said, 'Monks, what do you think? Who do you suppose was the Tathāgata called Indraketu who appeared at that time, on that occasion?', Blessed One, it was the Blessed One himself who at that time, on that occasion, was the Tathāgata called Indraketu. And why is that? Because the Blessed One possesses inconceivable skill in means. He is never satisfied in bringing sentient beings to maturity; he never ceases purifying buddhafields; he is never satisfied in establishing sentient beings in the qualities of a bodhisattva. If anyone, speaking truly, were to speak truly and say 'the chief Victor, the holy Victor, the supreme Victor, the most excellent Victor,' then, saying this of the Blessed One himself, they would be speaking truly."

Having said this, Mañjuśrī the youthful then spoke these verses:

The heroes are skilled in means
And loving toward sentient beings;
To bring sentient beings to full maturity,
The Great Hero works his magical displays.
Though in eight hundred million worlds you, the Victor,
Have shown yourself as a buddha,
You still dispel the thought that this is enough,
And your mind still turns to supreme awakening.
Since you purify the buddha fields
Of sixty-one great trichiliocosms,
All know you, Victor,
As the Sage skilled in means.
Even now, here and there,
You show the first arising of the mind of awakening;
And even now, O Guide,
You make many buddhas appear.
Through forms of various kinds
You carry out the conduct of the supreme person.
Unattached to the realm of phenomena,
You know whatever there is.
In the guise of Śakra (Indra) or of Brahmā,
And sometimes in the guise of Māra,
You work for the benefit of sentient beings,
In ways the worlds cannot fathom.
You act in the guise of a woman,
And dwell even in the births of animals;
Yet you are not stained by worldly concerns,
Just as a lotus is untouched by water.
O Guide, you conform yourself
To this world of many dispositions;
Therefore these beings
Proclaim your qualities day and night.
In some places you display your birth,
In some places you display nirvāṇa,
In some you turn the wheel of Dharma,
In some you take up the conduct of practice.
For the sake of taming sentient beings,
You rejoice, sing songs, and play,
And you even shed tears;
No sentient being whatever fully knows you.
Free of attachment, you take on the manner of attachment;
Free of fear, you seem afraid;
In order to tame sentient beings,
The Guide works his magical displays.
Without delusion, you show the manner of the deluded;
No child, you take on the manner of a child;
In order to tame sentient beings,
The Guide works his magical displays.
Not mad, you take on the manner of the mad;
Not lame, you seem to be lame;
Through magical displays of every kind
You tame sentient beings.
Thus, inconceivable Protector,
Your sublime Dharma too is beyond conception.
Great Hero, I bow down to you:
May sentient beings become like the Great Sage!

This concludes the fifth chapter, Past Events, Requested by Mahākāśyapa.

Then the eighty-six thousand garuḍa kings, having seen the offerings of those asura lords and heard the prophecy, felt immeasurable faith in the Blessed One (the Buddha). Eager to make offerings to the Blessed One, they magically emanated, as an offering to him, eighty-six thousand multistoried palaces of the seven precious substances, brilliantly colorful and beautiful to behold. The seven divine precious substances are these: gold, silver, beryl, crystal, agate, red pearl, and emerald.

Those multistoried palaces were surrounded on all sides by seven tiers of latticed railings of the four precious substances, brightly patterned and beautiful to behold. The four precious substances are these: gold, silver, beryl, and crystal. The posts and rails of the golden railings were of gold, and their slats of silver. The posts and rails of the silver railings were of silver, and their slats of gold. The posts and rails of the beryl railings were of beryl, and their slats of crystal. The posts and rails of the crystal railings were of crystal, and their slats were made of silver.

Those multistoried palaces were surrounded on all sides by seven tiers of nets of bells and small chimes, brightly colorful and beautiful to behold. These too were made entirely of the seven precious substances, namely gold, silver, beryl, crystal, agate, red pearl, and emerald. Those multistoried palaces were entirely covered with nets of the seven precious substances, brightly colorful and beautiful to behold: nets of gold, nets of silver, nets of beryl, nets of crystal, nets of red pearl, nets of agate, and nets of emerald.

They also emanated eighty-six thousand parasols, brightly colorful and beautiful to behold, all made of the seven precious substances, from gold up to emerald. They emanated eighty-six thousand victory banners as well, brightly colorful and beautiful to behold. Those banners too were all made from the four precious substances, gold, silver, beryl, and crystal. All the banners they emanated were such that the blue banners had yellow crests, the yellow banners had blue crests, and the red banners had white crests. They emanated eighty-six thousand pennants of many colors as well, brightly colorful and beautiful to behold. They emanated eighty-six thousand canopies as well, brightly colorful and beautiful to behold. Those canopies too, canopies of gold thread, canopies of silver thread, canopies of beryl, canopies of crystal, canopies of red pearl, canopies of agate, and canopies of emerald, were all made from the seven precious substances.

Then those garuḍa kings offered to the Blessed One those eighty-six thousand multistoried palaces, those eighty-six thousand parasols, those eighty-six thousand victory banners, those eighty-six thousand pennants, and those eighty-six thousand canopies, and all of them, floating in the sky above, circled the Blessed One three times. Just as the son of the elephant king Airāvaṇa carries the gods of the Heaven of the Thirty-Three on his head and goes through the sky to the Pārijāta tree, so those multistoried palaces, those parasols, those victory banners, those pennants, and those canopies floated in the expanse of the sky and circled the Blessed One three times.

Then those eighty-six thousand garuḍas made offerings to the Blessed One, circled him three times, and sat down to one side. Joining their palms and bowing, they praised the Blessed One with these verses:

Homage to you, who are free from aging and death!
Homage to you, who are liberated from aging and death!
Homage to the guide of countless sentient beings!
There is none equal to you, nor any who surpasses you.

"May we too possess the body of Nārāyaṇa,
Our limbs adorned with the thirty-two major marks,
Made utterly beautiful by the eighty excellent minor marks;
May we become buddhas, guides, just like you.
May we have the brilliance of a fathom-wide aura of light,
Beautiful with a sublime and exalted form;
May we be endowed with supreme deportment,
Purifying beings and dispelling their sorrow.
May we be fully endowed with moral discipline and concentration,
And through wisdom become supreme;
Thus, just like you, O guide,
May we expound to people and accomplish buddhahood.
Just like you, the guide wise in the ways of the world,
May we become guides endowed with the ten powers,
Perfect in the eighteen unshared qualities,
Wise in the ways of the world, and omniscient.
May we all teach that beings are without substance,
Like dreams, like illusions, without any abiding,
Like echoes and like space,
Just as you, O guide, teach."

Then the Blessed One (the Buddha), knowing the superior intention of those kings of birds, the Suparṇas, smiled. Then the venerable Aśvajit addressed the Blessed One in verse:

"Victors, guides, chief ones, buddhas
Do not smile without a cause;
What is the cause and condition of this smile?
Benefactor of the world, compassionate one, please tell us.
The Suparṇas have worshipped the Victor
And displayed a canopy filling the vast sky,
Arousing wonder in this world with its gods;
Supreme among two-legged beings, please prophesy for them.
All beings, their palms joined together,
Sit with joyful minds and delighted minds,
Wishing to hear what will become of the garuḍa beings;
Supreme among two-legged beings, please prophesy for them.
Their coming to you, best of humans,
Frees all beings from doubt,
And being free of doubt, the assembly becomes fearless;
By this, it is said, the Buddha is pleased.
In this assembly, fearless, they will greatly rejoice.
Fully purified, their faults thoroughly removed,
Those prophesied by the Victor will rejoice,
As when a teacher instructs a supreme disciple.
Please cut the doubts of those who have doubts;
Bring them great joy, and make an aspiration for them.
Since this assembly has gathered with joyful minds,
Self-Arisen One, tell what will become of the garuḍa beings."

Then the Blessed One again addressed the venerable Aśvajit in verse:

The one who overpowers all, of ten powers and true diligence,
The Victor, lord of sages, the Tathāgata,
The guide, with a voice of eight qualities,
Spoke to Aśvajit about the Suparṇa beings:
"Aśvajit, you have asked your question well.
The smile that I displayed for the benefit of the world,
Hear its result with a joyful mind;
Dispel your doubts and give rise to great joy.
Seeking unsurpassed and peaceful awakening,
And in order to seek the powers and the four fearlessnesses
Through which a guide becomes fearless,
The lords of birds made fine offerings to me.
In order to seek thoroughly the very firm body of Nārāyaṇa
Of one who proclaims the eighteen unshared qualities,
And the thirty-two marks and the excellent minor marks,
They made offerings to me.
Seeking the moral discipline and concentration of the Victor,
And also wisdom, the domain of the buddhas,
Perfect fields, and the liberation of beings,
For the sake of seeking all these, they made offerings.
Because they have made offerings to me with joy,
When they die and pass from the state of animals,
They will go where their conduct accords with that of the gods;
This is their last existence in the lower realms.
For ten million eons, as many as the sands of the Ganges,
They will dwell among gods and humans,
And having worshipped endless multitudes of guides,
They will become victors who have tamed their senses.
In their fields no lower realms will arise;
No unfavorable states will arise, and none will lack the marks.
The names of those victors will be wholly beautiful;
In the eon Banner of Sumeru they will become self-arisen ones.
For eight hundred and forty million years
Those guides will remain for the benefit of the world.
Because the guides rest in meditative equipoise for the world's benefit,
They will work impartially for the welfare of embodied beings.
Those protectors, who have become free of stain,
Will have eighty assemblies of those who have abandoned pride.
At that time, of those stainless protectors,
Eight hundred million with thoroughly tamed minds will arise.
Those guides, in order to remove the pride of beings,
Will fully turn the wheel of Dharma.
Friends, it is thus that these had great pride
And took on garuḍa bodies of great strength.
At a certain time in the past,
These were a company of sages of fierce austerity,
Fully eighty-six thousand in number;
They practiced austerities and sought perfect miraculous powers.
Having gained miraculous powers, through their pride
They delighted in possessing fierce austerities.
Their moral discipline unbroken, in the forest groves
Their lives ran out, they died and passed on, and their bodies perished.
Because of that pride they became garuḍas flying in the sky;
Through their minds set on miraculous power, they were born with miraculous powers.
Because of their pure moral discipline, they have faith in the Victor's teaching;
And through pride they have also given rise to the mind of awakening."

This is the sixth chapter, the Prophecy to the Garuḍas.

The fourth fascicle.

Then nine hundred and sixty million nāga maidens saw the offerings of those lords of the asuras and of those lords of the garuḍas, and heard the prophecies as well. A supreme and great joy arose in them; they were delighted, pleased, and overjoyed, and full of joy and gladness, they set about making offerings to the Blessed One. They emanated nine hundred and sixty million parasols of the seven precious substances, with spread nets of beryl, encircled by nets of red pearls, with golden handles, a hundred thousand spread ribs of beryl, and tassels of refined jewels. They emanated nine hundred and sixty million blue horses, blue in color, appearing blue, emitting blue light, adorned with blue ornaments, and bridled with bridles of beryl. In the sky above they emanated a canopy of star-colored jewels, sixty yojanas in circumference, which covered that entire assembly.

On it they magically produced nine hundred sixty million hanging pendants of the seven precious substances, bright, beautiful to the eye and lovely to behold, and hung them there. All around that canopy they magically produced nets of bells and small bells of the seven precious substances and hung them. They magically produced crane-foot ornaments of the seven precious substances. They magically produced nine hundred sixty million nāga musical instruments.

Then those nāga maidens mounted those blue horses and held parasols of the seven precious substances in the sky above. Moving through the sky, they played the music of those nine hundred sixty million instruments and circled the Blessed One three times. They scattered powdered divine sandalwood over the Blessed One. Over the Blessed One they also scattered divine aloeswood, tamāla leaves, powdered utpala and various flowers. They also scattered utpala flowers and garlands of campaka, and garlands of many different kinds of flowers. They also presented garlands of various incenses, garments and ornaments. Just as the asuras had done, they made vast offerings.

Then the sound of those nine hundred sixty million musical instruments they played entirely filled the great trichiliocosm, and all the beings who heard that music became irreversible from unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening. When the canopies of star-colored jewels, the pendants of the seven precious substances, the nets of bells and the crane-foot ornaments were stirred by the wind, there arose a sound as melodious, as pleasing and as delightful to hear as that of an instrument of eight parts played by a skilled musician. That sound too entirely filled the great trichiliocosm, and all the beings who heard the sounds of those ornaments became irreversible from unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening.

Then those nāga maidens sent down a rain of many kinds of flowers and a rain of many kinds of scented water. As the rain of flowers fell, a sweet fragrance arose and drifted to and fro. As the rain of scented water fell, the ground for sixty yojanas all around the great city of Kapilavastu became a mire of scented water, and that fragrance entirely filled the great trichiliocosm. All the beings who smelled that fragrance became irreversible from unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening.

Then those nāga maidens, having made offerings to the Blessed One with those nine hundred sixty million parasols, nine hundred sixty million horses and nine hundred sixty million musical instruments, remained in the sky, adorned with every ornament. They bowed their heads at the Blessed One's feet and circled the Blessed One three times. With palms joined, they paid homage to the Blessed One and settled to one side. At that time they also spoke these verses:

Those wise nāga maidens, with joyful, delighted and contented minds,
Seeking peaceful awakening, there made a great offering to Śākyamuni.
They magically produced nine hundred sixty million parasols, beautifully adorned with ornaments,
And worshiped the Sugata, free of attachment, with thoroughly tamed minds.
They magically produced nine hundred sixty million horses, beautifully adorned with ornaments,
Horses blue in color, with blue trappings, adorned with blue banners.
Moving all together through the sky, they made offering to the Sage with the horses.
Those nāga maidens, with minds of faith, bowed their heads to the Sage.
To make offering to the Supreme One, from the abode of the nāgas, with nāga instruments,
They there made offering to the Guide Śākyamuni, who is worthy of offering.
The sound of those instruments offered to the Sugata resounded through the three realms.
Multitudes of beings who heard that sound truly entered upon awakening.
What they had magically produced there in the sky became a single great canopy,
Spreading sixty yojanas in the ten directions and covering all beings.
The splendor of precious star-colored jewels blazed and shone in every direction.
Joyfully, for the sake of beings, the nāga maidens made offering to the Victor.
Joyfully generating measureless resolve and seeking peaceful awakening,
May we attain buddhahood as sugatas who benefit the world, wise in the ways of the world!
Just as you who possess the ten powers free sentient beings from suffering,
So may we too teach the Dharma and free the multitudes of beings obscured by affliction and suffering!
Phenomena are like illusions and mirages, and frail like foam;
Like bubbles on water struck by streaming rain, they perish, and they have no owner.
Having examined all beings as like reflections in a mirror,
We shall teach beings the true reality of the nature of phenomena.
Like an illusory display, nonexistent and false, they delude the childish
And make unskilled people, who do not know things as they are, commit great wrongs.
You, Sinless One, see well these phenomena that delude the childish just as they are,
And just as you see them, so you teach them to sentient beings.
Just as when clouds gather over this great earth they appear like mountains,
Yet in truth no mountain exists there by its essential nature,
So all beings, lacking intrinsic nature, delude the senses;
But the wise, examining with knowledge, are not deluded.
Because you teach that in truth all beings lack intrinsic nature,
The wise become inseparable from you, Guide and Victor.
Your heirs, fully satisfied by the Dharma, are like bridges over a mire.
This is not the domain of childish people; these beings follow after mere sounds.
That being so, Sinless One, true Teacher, protector of childish people,
Refuge, ally, abode, support and mainstay, in you we take refuge.
To seek that immeasurable awakening, we make offering to you, the Leader.

"Having become buddhas, may we, who realize and are free of evil, accomplish the welfare of the world."

Then the Blessed One (the Buddha), understanding the superior intention of those nāga maidens, smiled. Then the venerable Aśvajit addressed the Blessed One in verse:

"In the world, O wise one of the world, supreme guide, you have smiled.
I have never seen you, so rich in glory, smile without a cause.
Sublime lord of men, truly declare the cause of your smile.
On hearing it, the hosts of gods, humans and asuras, the nāga maidens, the guhyakas,
And the gandharvas too, hearing the protector's intent, will rejoice in mind.
In the worlds, O guide of the world, you know every cause.
There is no phenomenon at all that the Victor does not know in its causes and its aspects.
Therefore, Sugata, Victor, declare the cause of your smile as you have understood it.
Hearing it, all beings will be delighted and their doubts will be cut off.
In the supreme teaching of the Sage, the Sugata, they will also attain the goal.
These assemblies, having experienced its meaning, will become deeply peaceful in mind.
They will not fall under the sway of conceptual thought, and with minds set on awakening they will listen to the conduct of the Victor.
Hearing the meaning of your smile, Sugata, they will surely go forth as self-arisen ones.
This assembly, seeing the smile upon your face, O Victor, has fallen into doubt.
Those anxious with suffering, unhappy, and uncertain about the Dharma:
For them, Sage and Teacher, please dispel the doubt and slay the uncertainty.
O sage, quickly declare why you showed a smile, and crush the doubt.
Which victor today has purified the mind and has vanquished and tamed Māra?
Who, with mind fully satisfied, has made a sublime offering to the Blessed One?
Whose power is this? Teacher, declare it before these beings.
Having heard it, we will be delighted, supreme joy will arise, and we will be happy.
These beings gathered at your feet are in doubt.
Declare the cause of the smile that brings forth joy; Sage, please dispel the doubt.
Hearing it, these beings together with the gods will be free of doubt.
Having truly heard the sound of the Victor's speech, their minds will rejoice."

Then the Blessed One replied once more to the venerable Aśvajit in verse:

"Excellent, Aśvajit! You have asked well about this smile, the Victor's intent.
When the nāga maidens made offerings, I showed a smile for the benefit of the world.
I shall show its fruit; listen with a mind that has abandoned doubt and hesitation.
Listen to my words about the reason for which I smiled here.
These nāga maidens, with minds free of attachment, have truly entered upon awakening.
Having analyzed all beings as empty, these wise ones abide well, free of doubt.
Here there is no agent, no experiencer, no person, and no one who is born.
These phenomena are false, like a mirage, like a reflection in a mirror.
I know that these, who have made offerings, are grateful and of true understanding.
They see well that the Victor's nature of reality and beings are empty and without an owner.
These offerings, so varied and so well arranged, are like illusions.
The immeasurable awakening for which they made their aspiration is likewise empty.
Because they made that immeasurable offering to me and know beings to be empty,
When they die and pass from the nāga realm, they will go to the abode of the gods, the abode of Śakra (Indra).
Having gone to the Heaven of the Thirty-Three and completed their lives in joy among the gods,
They will go to the divine abode of unblemished fame, the abode of the Yāma gods.
Dwelling among the Yāma gods, in that divine abode they will enjoy divine pleasures.
Having become gods, those children of the Sugata will remain there for the measure of their lives.
The Victor's children, having enjoyed the pleasures of Yāma, when their lifespan is exhausted,
Will go to the abode of Tuṣita, the city of the gods, and become like the gods in their way of life.
There, surrounded by goddesses, they will enjoy divine pleasures.
Like a lotus unstained by water, those sugata ones of nonabiding mind will dwell there.
Realizing all beings to be empty, they will never fall away from awareness and understanding.
The mindfulness of these protectors, like something carved in stone, will never decline.
Having dwelt in that abode of Tuṣita and enjoyed divine pleasures,
When their lifespan is entirely exhausted, they will then go to the Nirmāṇarati gods.
There too those sugata ones of unblemished fame will be honored by hosts of goddesses,
And, possessed of nonabiding understanding, they will remain for as long as their lifespan lasts.
Having become Nirmāṇarati gods in that abode and enjoyed divine pleasures,
The wise and understanding ones, when their lives are spent, having reached the culmination of the Dharma,
Guides of pure intention, with unattached minds, whose thoughts are set on the Dharma,
Those endowed with the Dharma will go to the assembly of the gods in the abode of the Paranirmitavaśavartin.
They will dwell in that divine abode and enjoy divine pleasures.
Those sugata ones, undeluded and understanding, will remain for their own lifespan.
Like nirvāṇa, they realize the divine pleasures of the senses to be sameness and peace.
Through meditative concentration they will go to the abode of Brahmā, of unblemished fame.
In the abode of Brahmā, skilled in meditative concentration, having enjoyed the fruits of concentration,
Dwelling there they will become incomparable, and with minds set on awakening they will remain for an eon.
For an eon they will dwell in harmony with the abode of Brahmā, seeking the aim of wisdom, of excellent discipline.
Those sages, wise and skilled in means, will accomplish boundless benefit for the world.
Those wise sages there, in the domain of meditative concentration, will be unattached to concentration.
Knowing the bliss of concentration as like sameness, their faculties will be stilled for the sake of awakening.
With minds wholly liberated, those sages, for the sake of awakening, will possess the mind of awakening.
Like nirvāṇa, their concentration will be single-pointed and bear the characteristic of peace.
Sugata ones and children of the Blessed One, seeking the peace of awakening,
With minds like Brahmā's, working for the welfare of the world, they will dwell in that abode of Brahmā.
All of them, possessed of Brahmā's understanding, perfected, wholly for the sake of awakening,
Will teach the deliverance of the self-arisen intent that passes beyond the path of the three realms.
Generating respect for Brahmā, beings will come to seek awakening.
Having understood Brahmā's intention, the self-arisen intention, the ultimate deliverance,
Those diligent in the supreme teaching of the Sugata will teach the Dharma to beings
In such a way that they swiftly come to know the fruit of the Dharma's way.
Dwelling in that abode of Brahmā, for the sake of the worlds' awakening,
Having benefited them, they will establish many tens of millions and trillions of living beings in awakening.
To make offerings to all the victors, they will go to many trillions of world systems.
Those of stilled faculties will become victors in the eon called Tārakopama, Like the Stars."

Gods, humans, nāgas, garuḍas, and asuras,
gandharvas, kumbhāṇḍas, hosts of kinnaras, and yakṣas, with all beings, were joyful and delighted.
The Blessed One, through the Dharma perfectly purified, has gained power in awakening.
All of them rejoiced, joined their palms, and bowed their heads to the Sugata (the Buddha).

This concludes the seventh chapter, the Prophecy of the Nāga Maidens.

Then Nanda and Upananda, kings of the nāgas, and nine hundred million nāgas with them, saw the offerings of those nāga maidens, heard the prophecy, and understood it completely. They were filled with wonder and thought: "That Tathāgata knows the thoughts of all sentient beings. There is nothing the Blessed One does not know, has not seen, has not heard, or has not directly realized. The Dharma of the Blessed One is well spoken, rightly seen, free of fever, timeless, leading onward, meaningful to see here and now, and to be known by the wise for themselves. How wonderful and marvelous! That Tathāgata possesses great miraculous power and great might. Even those born as women, who are restless, unsteady and inconstant, fully understand the Dharma the Blessed One teaches. Likewise the Saṅgha of the Blessed One's disciples has entered well, entered uprightly, entered in accord with reason, and entered harmoniously. How very wonderful that even those born as women, possessed of fierce attachment, aversion and delusion, enter into the Blessed One's teaching!"

Filled with wonder, in order to make offerings to the Blessed One, they covered the whole of Jambudvīpa, as far as the mountains at the edge of the ocean, with great clouds of incense, and they filled this whole world with the fragrance of incense. That fragrance in turn filled all the buddhafields completely, and all the sentient beings who smelled it became irreversible from unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening.

To make offerings to the Blessed One, they also filled the whole great city of Kapilavastu, for sixty yojanas around, knee-deep with red pearls. They heaped the Nyagrodha Park with priceless precious jewels. They conjured up assembly halls of nāga flowers, sixty yojanas wide, with pillars of the seven precious substances. They also conjured up countless nāga instruments and cymbals and made them resound. In those assembly halls of nāga flowers they hung nine hundred million garlands of many kinds of flowers. The pillars of the seven precious substances were beautifully adorned with nets of beryl. The floor of the assembly hall was covered with priceless precious jewels. That floor was smooth and yielding, just like the stone slabs of the gods of the Heaven of the Thirty-Three: smooth as polished stone, beryl in color, pleasant to the touch like kācilindika cloth, delightful, a solace to the eyes of all beings who saw it, and pleasing to the minds of humans and gods.

Among those many precious jewels, from some there came fire, and from some came only water. Of these, from some came blue water, from some yellow water, from some red water, from some white water, and from some water of many colors. From some jewels came a pleasant, cool breeze. From some jewels came, just as one wished, other requisites besides these. Some did the work of oil, and some the work of a mirror, so that the whole assembly appeared within those precious jewels. All the beings moving about in Kapilavastu, however many there were, also appeared within those precious jewels. And the whole assembly saw the Blessed One, who had produced those miraculous displays of emanation, together with the Saṅgha of śrāvakas, seated within those precious jewels.

From beneath the floor of the assembly hall, by the power of the nāgas, parasols of many kinds emerged. Victory banners of many kinds, pennants of many kinds, flower garlands of many kinds, incense garlands of many kinds, jewel garlands of many kinds, pearl strands of many kinds, garlands of layered flowers of many kinds, horses of many kinds, and nets of bells and tinkling bells of many kinds emerged as well.

Then Nanda and Upananda, kings of the nāgas, and the nine hundred million nāgas led those horses before them and, going on foot, circumambulated the Blessed One three times. Then with precious jewels, pleasant to the touch like kācilindika cloth, they scattered, strewed and showered the Blessed One. All the jewels that had emerged from the floor of the assembly hall fell like rain upon the Blessed One and the Saṅgha of śrāvakas. Those countless nāga instruments and cymbals resounded from the sky above.

Then those nine hundred million nāgas circumambulated the Blessed One three times, bowed their heads at the Blessed One's feet, and joined their palms. Without a word, they recollected the Blessed One in every aspect and gazed at him without blinking. Thus, longing for the qualities of a buddha, they set out truly for awakening with superior intention.

Then they remained silent for a moment and recollected the qualities of the Blessed One (the Buddha). Each of them draped his upper robe over one shoulder, set his right knee on the ground, and bowed with joined palms toward where the Blessed One was. They prostrated to the Blessed One with the five limbs and praised him in these verses:

You whose hundred marks are complete, who kept your vows well,
Delighting in austerity, stainless and compassionate,
Abandoned the land that held a source of jewels.
You, Sugata, went forth from the city.
For six years you performed hard austerities,
And though you, Victor, did not yet find the deathless nectar,
Your heart, Sugata, never lost courage.
Sage, you practiced with the mind set on awakening.
How you, Guide, teacher of gods and humans,
Wishing to benefit beings, practiced austerities,
How the Protector of the world underwent suffering:
Even to hear of that leaves us unhappy.
That the Sage gave away his head and his eyes,
And how he brought the mind of awakening to fulfillment:
When we hear of the Sugata's hardships,
Even to hear of that, we are not at ease.
When the king of Kaliṅga cut off
The feet, the hands and the ears of Kṣāntivādin,
And likewise cut off his nose, the Victor remained unshaken.
Even to hear of that leaves us unhappy.
When you, Guide, would not give up the dove that had come to you for refuge,
And were weighed upon the scales,
Even to hear that of the Victor, we are not at ease.
The Sugata's conduct toward awakening was great suffering.
Should you ask why we find no pleasure in it:
Because beings who do harm to the Sugata
Fall into the darkness of the lower realms,
We are unhappy when we recall the Sugata's conduct of suffering.
Guide of the world, of exalted mind,
How may the Sugata's conduct become a mind of non-harming,
So that while one practices it no harm befalls it?
Guide of the world, please declare the easy conduct.
Sage of infinite understanding, in the Sugata's conduct
These hosts of nāgas strive with all their might.
So that they may practice in accord with your word,
Sugata, please teach the path to awakening.
Longing to attain the state where there is no aging and death,
These hosts of nāgas, O Sage, remain athirst.
Sugata, please declare the easy practice of discipline
By which these beings may be easily tamed.

Then the Blessed One looked upon those offerings of the nāgas, and, understanding the aspirations they had made with superior intention, at that moment he smiled. It is the nature of things that whenever the buddhas, the blessed ones, smile, rays of light of various colors, of many colors, issue from the Blessed One's mouth, rise up as far as the world of Brahmā, return, and vanish into the crown of the Blessed One's head. Then the venerable Aśvajit at that time addressed the Blessed One in verse:

Since the victors, sages among ascetics of excellent discipline,
Do not smile without a cause,
Guide, Sugata, possessed of great compassion,
Peerless Blessed One, please tell the cause of your smile.
Teacher of the assembly, these people
Long to hear the cause of the smile and gaze upon your face.
Their minds are downcast and they are held by doubt.
Wise one, please dispel their doubt.
Wise one of the world, Teacher Śākyamuni,
Who is it today whose mind is filled with joy?
Who has seen the lord of the māras,
Confounded him, and left him unhappy?
Teacher of the assembly, Guide, who is it
That has honored and served the Sugata, the father, the nature of reality itself,
And made a sublime offering to the Sugata?
Lion of the Śākyas, speak, and settle our doubt.
With their ten fingers joined in reverence and placed upon their heads,
These beings gaze upon the Sage's face.
Teacher of the assembly, please dispel their doubt,
And, Guide, tell also the cause of your smile.
Hearing it, these people will rejoice,
And they will understand the Victor's intent.
If the Sugata's retinue is satisfied,
They will not think otherwise and will abide in diligence.

Then the Blessed One again spoke to the venerable Aśvajit in verse:

As the wise Sage, the Guide, the teacher of the assembly,
Speaks in a voice like Brahmā's, endowed with eight qualities,
Aśvajit, have faith with a flawless mind,
And listen to the cause of the smile.
These lords of the nāgas, having gained faith,
Outshone all beings and made offerings.
In order to ripen people with ease,
They seek this awakening of a buddha.
Seeing with great compassion beings who have no guide,
Why would they not take suffering upon themselves?
That beings may attain vast awakening,
Guides never grow weary.
Delighting in calm abiding, strong with the powers of wisdom,
Having fully cultivated the three gates of liberation,
Signlessness, emptiness, and likewise wishlessness,
Their minds utterly pure, they will go to bliss.
Realizing the sameness of all beings,
When they hear from the Victor the marks of wisdom,
They will gain an even mind, love and compassion.
Generating great compassion for beings,
Loving and supremely compassionate,
They will become unsurpassed knowers of the world.
Those victors, having liberated these beings equally,
Will become arhats and guides.
Always listening to the words of selflessness,
And never entering into the stream of worldly talk,
They will attain the deathlessness of the sugatas.
There, there will be no māras and no hosts of non-Buddhists (tīrthikas).
To those victors, the compassionate guides and sugatas,
All beings will be devoted.
They will be devoted to all the sugatas' teachings and words,
And to the intent of the sugatas.
Within existence they will practice the conduct of the sugatas,
And ripen beings with the greatest ease.
Attaining the peerless, easily and swiftly
They will fully awaken and attain deathlessness.
When they have attained it, beings too will be devoted,
And all people will be endowed with supreme form.
All beings will directly understand
The words of those sugatas' intent.
Those animals, birds, yakṣas and humans
Will not do the least thing of their own accord that is not their intent.
At all times they will realize the Dharma and possess mindfulness,
And they will attain the deathlessness of the sugatas.
Of the beings who hear their words,
There will be none at all displeased with that teaching.
Delighting in the speech of those sugatas,

Beings will truly attain the deathless.
At that time beings will rightly understand the Victor's intent.
From old age and sickness and death,
from sorrow and from lamentation, they will be wholly freed.
Hearing those words, they will be free of clinging to "I."
The nāgas' intention was fully laid before the Lion of the Śākyas,
and how they would attain immeasurable awakening
and enter the wisdom of the sages was taught thus to the son.
Hearing the Victor's words, the delightful prophecy of the nāgas,
the assembly rejoiced at heart.
Their minds became utterly peaceful in it,
and with joyful minds they went for refuge to the Victor.

This is the eighth chapter, the Prophecy of the Nāgas.

Then the one hundred eighty million kumbhāṇḍas saw the offerings of the asuras, the garuḍas, the nāga maidens and the nāgas, and also heard the prophecy. They were satisfied, pleased and delighted, overjoyed, joyful and happy, and they were filled with wonder, thinking: "So great is the Tathāgata's mastery of wisdom that even though such vast offerings have been made to the Blessed One, he does not regard them as anything wondrous. It is like the great ocean, which is vast and full, and so neither rises nor falls. In the same way the Blessed One has no doubt about any dharma, has perfected every quality, and possesses the vast wisdom of omniscience. So even when he accepts such offerings he is neither elated nor deflated, and does not regard them as wondrous. That is wondrous indeed!"

Then, in order to honor the Blessed One with an offering of their own, those one hundred eighty million kumbhāṇḍas emanated one hundred eighty million canopies of the seven precious substances: canopies of gold thread, canopies of silver thread, canopies of beryl, canopies of crystal, canopies of red pearl, canopies of mother-of-pearl, and canopies of emerald. On those jeweled canopies they also emanated pendants of garlands of various flowers. On the canopies of gold thread hung pendants of garlands of silver flowers. On the canopies of silver thread hung pendants of garlands of golden flowers. On the canopies of beryl hung pendants of garlands of crystal flowers. On the canopies of crystal hung pendants of garlands of beryl flowers. On the canopies of red pearl hung pendants of garlands of mother-of-pearl flowers. On the canopies of mother-of-pearl hung pendants of garlands of red pearl flowers. And on the canopies of emerald hung pendants of garlands of crystal flowers. All these they emanated.

They also emanated one hundred eighty million splendid chariots, beautiful to behold, made of everything from gold to emerald. Above those chariots they emanated one hundred eighty million parasols of the seven precious substances, each with a hundred ribs, with handles of gold, handles of beryl, and handles of crystal. On those parasols too they emanated pendants of flowers of various kinds of jewels: pendants of golden flowers, pendants of silver flowers, pendants of beryl flowers, pendants of crystal flowers, pendants of red pearl flowers, and pendants of flowers of nāga jewels. They also emanated nets of red pearl, which held together those parasols and those flower garlands. They emanated the cymbals of kumbhāṇḍa music as well. And each of those chariots was followed by one of those canopies.

Then those kumbhāṇḍas mounted those chariots of the seven precious substances and honored the Blessed One by scattering over him those parasols, canopies and flowers of the seven precious substances, and they circled the Blessed One three times in the sky above. Having circled him, they descended from those chariots, bowed their heads at the Blessed One's feet, sat down to one side, and praised the Blessed One in verses:

"Fearless in every way, possessed of mastery,
Great Sage, neither elated nor deflated,
like Meru, king of mountains:
please accept these vast offerings.

Sage, you know all beings as mere name,
mere sign and mere convention, and you have no attachment.
Guide, having accomplished your peaceful samādhi,
you who possess wisdom have no arrogance at all.

Victor, you know beings to be like illusions,
empty of intrinsic nature, like joy in a dream,
like the moon in water, like a mirage in the sky:
so the Sugata sees these beings.

However a city of gandharvas may appear,
that city exists nowhere in all the ten directions;
that city is otherwise merely a name:
just so the Sugata sees these beings.

All these offerings are just like echoes:
chariots, parasols, canopies and cymbals,
flowers, banners, and the joining of our palms.
Like echoes, so are they.

Guide, may we who have made these offerings
swiftly become buddhas, supreme among humans.
Having realized beings to be like dreams,
we shall teach as you, Victor, teach.

Seeing people tormented by sickness, old age and death,
we too will swiftly attain awakening
and teach to beings the state free of aging and death,
hearing which they will be freed.

Having awakened to the supreme path of awakening,
we shall teach it to beings who have lost their way.
Sage who teaches the undisturbed dharmas,
we shall teach beings the supreme nirvāṇa."

Then the Blessed One, knowing the superior intention of those kumbhāṇḍas, smiled.

Then the venerable Aśvajit addressed the Blessed One in verse:

"The Teacher does not smile without a cause;
The Guide, wise in the ways of the world, has nothing that astonishes him.
When the Victor, who is never moved to wonder, smiles,
Please explain well the cause of that smile; I wish to hear it.
Having seen a smile break upon the Victor's face,
These beings are caught in doubt and hesitation.
Dispel the doubt of those who are in doubt;
Lord of humans, tell us the cause of your smile.
Who today has set their mind upon the Dharma?
Who today has also seen the King of Dharma?
Who now has made offerings in praise of the Buddha?
Best of humans, I wish to hear.
Who today, having understood the suffering of the conditioned,
Has overcome the single mind that has turned into formations?
Who abides unwavering in the true limit?
You whose mind is utterly pure, I wish to hear.
Who today has vanquished the māras with their hosts?
Who now has pleased the Sage, the Teacher?
What guide today has found the very depth?
I have doubts, and now I wish to hear of this.
In order to hear the meaning of that smile,
All beings stand with their palms joined.
Guide, remove the doubt of those in doubt;
Supreme among two-legged beings, please give the explanation."
