Then the Blessed One again addressed the venerable Aśvajit in verse:

"Aśvajit, you have asked your question well.
Because you have fully asked the cause of this smile,
You have also accomplished the benefit of beings.
I shall explain its meaning; Aśvajit, listen.
The hosts of kumbhāṇḍas have made offerings to me,
And they have set their minds upon the Dharma of peace.
Having seen me, who am beyond wonder, they were satisfied,
And they have set their minds upon the Dharma of peace.
Today they have made offerings to me,
And have turned their understanding fully to the Dharma of peace.
Feeling tender love for people who have lost the path,
From the depths of their hearts they have given rise to great compassion.
Out of compassion they have made offerings to me,
And have thought with loving-kindness of those who have utterly lost the path.
Because those wise ones have done that deed,
When they die and pass from the kumbhāṇḍa state of birth, they, of excellent conduct,
Will go into the presence of Śakra (Indra), lord of the gods,
And will also go into the presence of the Protector, who acts out of compassion and love.
There the teaching will be shown to them a little, again and again,
And they will further become free of doubt about the Dharma.
For eons as numerous as the sands of the Ganges
They will roam from field to field,
Making offerings to the Dharma of the great-souled ones.
With minds that have fully donned the great armor,
Without weariness they will move through
Endless, teeming world systems.
Acting for the benefit of many people, they will become guides,
And will become victors called Beyond Wonder.
Wherever the fields may be to which they go and travel,
There beings will know that a victor has appeared.
Through skillful means, in times to come,
Beyond wonder, they will bring people to liberation.
The victors, Beyond Wonder, purifying fields,
Seek the unsurpassed peace of perfect awakening.
Yet they do not dwell even in that seeking,
For in this way they abide in unsurpassed awakening."

Thus the Victor explained the cause of his smile,
And the Sugata dispelled the misgivings of the world.
Having heard of that fruit of the kumbhāṇḍas' offering,
Beings, their doubts dispelled, were filled with joy.

This is the ninth chapter, the Prophecy concerning the Kumbhāṇḍas.

Then, at that time, three hundred sixty million gandharvas saw the offerings made by the asuras, the garuḍas, the nāga maidens, the nāgas and the kumbhāṇḍas, and heard the prophecies as well. They were satisfied, delighted and elated; supreme joy, happiness and gladness arose in them, and they were filled with amazement, thinking: "It is wondrous and marvelous how the Tathāgata has truly declared again and again that the realm of phenomena is unchanging, has also truly taught the deeds to be done, has taught the increase of the roots of virtue, and has truly taught again and again that there is no prophecy regarding any phenomenon whatsoever." They came to understand such a Dharma, and a vast resolve arose in them to serve the Tathāgata. They became devoted to unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening, and with minds filled with supreme joy they set about making offerings to the Blessed One.

They emanated three hundred sixty million Airāvaṇas, kings of elephants. Each of those Airāvaṇa elephants had three hundred sixty million heads, and each head had three hundred sixty million tusks. On each tusk there were seven ponds, in each pond seven lotuses, and on each lotus ten billion petals. On each petal there were seven goddesses, and they emanated each goddess surrounded by seven more, adorned with every ornament and holding divine incense, seated at the very edges of the lotus leaves.

On each head of those Airāvaṇa elephants they also emanated three hundred sixty million parasols of the seven precious substances, held in place with nets of the seven precious substances, the spaces between them hung with tassels of the seven precious substances. Over all the elephants' heads they also emanated canopies of divine incense, every one of them hung with tassels of many kinds.

Then those gandharvas mounted the three hundred sixty million Airāvaṇa elephants and, playing divine music and cymbals, moved through the sky. They circled the Blessed One thirty-six times and then scattered divine sandalwood powder over the Blessed One; they strewed it, and they strewed it all about. With divine aguru powder, divine tamāla leaves, utpala powder, divine gold dust, and divine flowers, mandārava and great mandārava, mañjūṣaka and great mañjūṣaka, paruṣaka and great paruṣaka, white-hued flowers and great white-hued flowers, they scattered over the Blessed One; they strewed them, and they strewed them all about. With flowers of gold, silver, beryl, crystal, agate, red pearl and emerald, and with emanated flowers, they scattered over the Blessed One; they strewed them, and they strewed them all about. From those canopies of incense a fragrance arose, spreading out and drifting back again. And a rain of many kinds of divine scented water fell from the sky above.

As that rain of perfumed water fell, the great city of Kapilavastu, for sixty yojanas all around, turned into a mire of perfumed water. The scent of that perfumed mire spread until it filled the entire great trichiliocosm world system, and every sentient being who smelled that fragrance and heard the sound of those musical instruments became irreversible from unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening.

In the same way, each gandharva, in order to make offerings to the Blessed One (the Buddha), made offerings with thirty-six koṭis of elephants, sons of Earth Guardian, on whose heads sat divine maidens who sang and danced, and they filled all the people with longing. Some of those divine maidens changed the postures of their bodies. Some scattered powders of sandalwood, aloewood and tamāla leaves. Some scattered powders of the asuras' divine substances and powders of many kinds of incense. Some scattered flowers ranging from māndārava and great māndārava flowers to multicolored skyasnar flowers and great brilliant skyasnar flowers, and conjured flowers as well. Some took up in handfuls flowers ranging from golden flowers to emerald flowers, along with precious metal, gold and jewels, and scattered them over the Blessed One. Some scattered garments. Some offered things ranging from anklets and girdles to golden garlands adorned with jewels. Some held golden nets. Some held banners of every kind of jewel, and jewel flags of many colors and many hues. Some rained down divine perfumed water, and so on as before, up to the asura maidens praising the Blessed One with verses.

Then those gandharvas, mounted on those royal elephants, the sons of Earth Guardian, circled the Blessed One again and again. Then they dismounted from the elephants, the sons of Earth Guardian, circled the Blessed One three more times, bowed their heads at his feet, joined their palms, sat down to one side, and praised the Blessed One in verses:

Blessed One, Sugata, who brings existence to its end,
You free beings from the bonds of existence.
Nowhere in the three realms of existence is there one like you.
Supreme bull, you possess the finest form and marks.

Supreme among humans, upon this great earth
You, of boundless fame, have shown a wonder.
Though there is no phenomenon whatever that causes change,
You establish beings in the accumulation of virtue.

Sugata, changeless suchness
Is mere expression; there is nothing else at all.
There is no designating of conventions, nor any who designates;
And yet, Sugata, you purify beings.

The Sugata has taught that there is no going and no coming,
No abiding, emptiness of intrinsic nature,
And that these phenomena are without intrinsic nature;
And the supreme Victor has also taught their functions.

No phenomenon whatever performs an action,
Yet the Lord of the World has fully taught action too.
Not thinking of the qualities of self or other,
The Blessed One has taught every deed.

The many and various parts of a chariot,
Though they do not think of the qualities of self or other,
Still carry out the work of a chariot.
Just so has the Sugata spoken of all phenomena.

A phenomenon does not urge on a phenomenon;
A phenomenon does not recollect a phenomenon;
Phenomena do not rely upon phenomena;
No phenomenon whatever is born or dies.

You have declared these beings to be conventional,
Yet that convention too is not found there.
"All these beings are just as convention is":
So you, teacher of the assembly, have proclaimed in perfect purity.

The intrinsic nature of the eye exists nowhere,
Yet the Sugata has set forth its function.
Because you have fully taught such a way of Dharma,
The Knower of the World has done a thing most wondrous.

So may we too become Sugatas ourselves,
And through the Dharma lead erring beings, who have lost their minds,
To take hold of the way of the Dharma,
And bring them to attain the peaceful, deathless state.

Just as you are, Guide,
Guide, Sugata of boundless fame,
Compassionate, wishing to benefit, Knower of the World,
May we possess the marks born of a hundred merits.

Just as you do, Sugata, so may we
Free beings who suffer, veiled by pride,
Following the stream of sensual pleasures,
Delighting in them, beset by the eight sufferings.

Just as you do, Sugata, so may we
Free beings who undergo the harms of suffering,
Caught in the noose of deceit, who see no end,
Utterly bewildered, restless as monkeys.

Just as you do, Sugata, so may we
Free beings who are unskilled and bereft of sight,
The hosts of gods, utterly weary and sunk in sleep,
Who squander the fruit of their own karma.

Then the Blessed One, perceiving the superior intention of those thirty-six koṭis of gandharvas, smiled.

Then the venerable Aśvajit addressed the Blessed One in verse:

"The Sugata, who sees the infinite, has smiled.
Since the Guide does not smile without a cause,
Sugata, tell us swiftly the cause of your smile,
And dispel the doubts of those troubled by hesitation.
Having seen the smile upon your face, O Sugata,
These people have come to doubt.
They long to hear the meaning of the smile.
Great Sage, please dispel the doubts of beings.
Who has entered the teaching of the Sugata,
And become free of doubt, with uncertainty removed?
Knowing whose thought has the Blessed One,
The Supreme among humans, displayed a smile?
Who, just as the Sugata has taught,
Has realized it with understanding and come to abide in accord with the Dharma?
Knowing whose understanding, mindfulness, realization and insight
Has the Sugata smiled amid beings?
These beings, having wholly set aside all other thoughts,
Are eager to hear from your own lips the meaning of the Sugata's smile.
With palms joined, all ten fingers placed upon their heads,
With joyful minds they gaze upon the Teacher's face.
Free of afflictions, having wholly abandoned sorrow,
Great Sage of joyful heart, eye of the world,
Please dispel the doubts and hesitation in the minds of beings.
Compassionate Victor, tell us the cause of the smile."

Then the Blessed One replied to the venerable Aśvajit in verse:

"The smile I made was for the sake of beings.
Your question about its meaning is fitting and good.
Through the explanation of that smile's meaning,
The welfare of a great multitude of people will grow today.
Aśvajit, listen to the cause for which I smiled.
This smile that the Guide has made
I shall explain without error.
Listen to that teaching with a joyful mind.
These gandharvas, following the Dharma,
Have made these offerings to the Sugata.
The nature of reality has never come into being,
And the nature of reality will never be exhausted.
The Victor abides seeing conceptual thought as an illusion;
Those who do not understand this fall into delusion.
Whatever phenomenon one may express,
That very thing is empty, the Sugata has said.
Having understood these words of mine,
They made offerings with horses and the finest elephants.
Seeking awakening, free of aging and free of death,
The kings of the gandharvas have honored me.
Having given rise to compassion for beings who have gone astray,
They have set their aspiration on omniscience.
Because they have made this offering to me,
They will leave the gandharva realm with joyful minds,
And, of excellent understanding, dwelling in the abode and realm of the gods,
They will go to the assembly of the gods of Śakra (Indra).
They will make offerings to many koṭis of trillions
Of stainless buddhas, the sugatas.
Following them from field to field,
The wise will also hear the Dharma from them.
They will perfect the conduct of the sugatas,
And their own fields too will always be utterly pure.
Holders of the Victors' lineage,
They will teach awakening to many trillions of sentient beings.
Knowing these beings to be empty of intrinsic nature,
As sugatas they will teach beings.
As victors, omniscient and all-seeing,
Stainless, they will remain for trillions of eons.
As buddhas they will bear the name Infinite Realization,
And each buddha, stainless, will remain
For eons numbering many trillions,
Teaching in turn this path to omniscience."

Thus at that time the Victor, the Sage, destroyer of afflictions,
Prophesied the gandharvas who had made offerings.
Aśvajit asked the meaning of the smile,
And he gave the explanation, the prophecy of the gandharvas.
Hearing the prophecy of the gandharvas, which delighted the mind,
All beings rejoiced.
Having heard there the words of the Sugata,
All went for refuge to Śākyamuni.

This concludes the tenth chapter, the Prophecy of the Gandharvas.

The Noble Meeting of the Father and the Son, a Great Vehicle sūtra. Fifth section.

Then the eight hundred million yakṣas, having seen the offerings of all those from the asuras up to the gandharvas and having heard the prophecies, were delighted, pleased and glad. Supremely joyful, they gave rise to joy and happiness and developed great reverence for the Blessed One's way of wisdom. And because they had also heard of entry into the nature of reality, the perception of him as their Teacher arose in them. They also came to perceive the Blessed One as one whose understanding is inexhaustible, and recognizing him as the Blessed One of Inexhaustible Understanding, they conceived a longing for the qualities of the Buddha.

Having conceived a longing for the qualities of the Buddha, they became eager to make offerings to the Blessed One, and they praised the Blessed One in verses:

"Your power of higher knowledge is utterly boundless,
Like the ocean, which the clouds can never drain.
Therefore, peerless benefactor of the world,
Lion among men, we praise you thus.

One might perhaps weigh Mount Meru on a scale,
O one of great splendor, one might perhaps measure the sky,
But the power of the gnosis of the Sugata, who has supreme wisdom,
No one could ever grasp its extent or gauge it.

You comprehend the intentions of all beings,
Those who can be guided, those who cannot, and those not to be tamed.
You directly know the kinds of their karma.
Therefore, peerless one, you are utterly without limit.

To what kinds of elements they are devoted,
In what ways their mind-streams arise,
And what their inclinations are, you know without exception.
Therefore, peerless one, you are utterly without limit.

Those whose conduct is attachment, and likewise those whose conduct is aversion,
And people whose conduct is delusion, you know likewise;
The forms of jealousy too you know likewise.
Therefore, peerless one, you are utterly without limit.

Wherever these beings wander along the stream of paths,
You, the peerless one, fully know them.
Since gnosis flows forth for this unceasingly,
Therefore, peerless one, you are utterly without limit.

The Sugata fully sees all beings:
On what wasted streams of paths they have wandered,
And who have fallen away from the power of loving-kindness;
All of them without exception you fully see.

For how long beings remain in time,
What kinds of suffering befall them,
And what kinds of bodily form they bear,
All these, O Guide, you fully see.

What views there are, the Lion among men sees,
Through which they come to meet with kinds of suffering;
On what they rely in performing their actions,
These too the Guide fully sees.

They go forth into the teaching of the Victors;
Through which door of liberation,
Relying on what kind of path,
They attain what kind of peace, you know.

Those childish ones who go forth
Into the teaching of the Sugata, and through worldly cleverness
How they abandon the most subtle teaching,
These too the Guide fully sees.

Through contempt for the teaching that benefits the world,
For how long they remain in the great hells
And what kinds of suffering they experience,
These too the Guide fully sees.

Those who give rise to faith in the Victors,
Go forth and uphold the Victor's teaching,
Examine all phenomena as emptiness,
Demolish all signs,
Abandon all wishes for any destiny of beings,
Examine this body as like an illusion,
And realize the aggregates as like an echo,
Those who possess the awakening mind you fully see.

Whatever merit there is in praising you, O Protector,
Just as you, the peerless one, fully understand it,
By it may unexcelled buddhahood be accomplished,
And may all beings attain the Self-Arisen state!"

Then the Blessed One, at that time, perceiving the superior intention of those eight hundred million yakṣas, smiled. Then the venerable Aśvajit addressed the Blessed One in verse:

"The Lion among men has smiled this smile.
The Moon among men, who has become self-arisen,
Does not smile without a cause;
Please tell us the cause of this smile.

Having seen that smile on the Teacher's face,
Blessed One, these worlds are in doubt.
Protector, please dispel their doubts,
And these worlds will become joyful in mind.

Who today has given rise to faith in the Victor?
Who has fully realized the vast Dharma?
Whose mind has come to rest unmoving?
Victor, please reveal these to beings.

Today the assemblies are in great doubt,
And all sit gazing at the Victor's face.
So that all may become free of doubt,
O Hero, please dispel their doubts.

Whose miraculous display is this today?
Who has shown such excellent diligence here?
Who has had cause to meet with you here?
Please explain this well to beings.

Victor, who wish to benefit beings,
Please dispel well the uncertainty of those in doubt.
Hearing it, the worlds together with their gods
Will also attain joy and delight."

Then the Blessed One answered the venerable Aśvajit in verse:

"Aśvajit, you have asked a good question;
Today you have done great benefit for beings.
Since you asked the cause of the Self-Arisen's smile,
You have also clearly seen the Teacher's intent.
I shall reveal their prophecy in full.

Having seen the minds of the hosts of yakṣas,
I smiled for the sake of benefiting beings.
Aśvajit, listen with a faithful mind.

Having understood the peaceful awakening of the Victors,
The hosts of yakṣas gave rise to a mind of devotion,
Praised the Buddha amid the hosts of bhūtas,
And set their minds on the supreme Self-Arisen.

In order to know phenomena as emptiness
And to demolish all signs,
They abandoned all wishes for any destiny,
And set their minds on supreme awakening.

By the power of yoga they demolished the aggregates,
And knowing them to be merely conventional, they were unattached,
Like a lotus in water throughout all existence;
They set their minds on supreme awakening.

Whatever existence they might become attached to
They demolished as empty and realized everything well.
That itself is the unexcelled awakening of the Victors;
For the sake of awakening they know the discipline of solitude.

Birth, old age and death are wholly empty;
That is the unexcelled awakening of the Victors.
Having come to know the intrinsic nature of things as empty,
One attains peaceful, unexcelled awakening.

The intrinsic nature of the aggregates is void and empty;
The intrinsic nature of awakening is void and empty;
The one who practices is also empty by nature;
The wise know this, not the childish.

When they understand gnosis as empty by nature,
And understand the nature of the knowable as empty,
And realize it to be the same as the knower,
They are said to practice the path to awakening.

One should know the nature of emptiness to be empty;
Signlessness too is empty by its very nature;
Wishlessness too is empty by nature;

If they practice thus, they practice nothing other than this.
Hearing this, the worlds together with their gods
rejoiced, and they became endowed with strength.
They placed their faith in the self-arisen Dharma,
knew the state of peace, and set their minds on awakening.

"The hosts of yakṣas today have become devoted,
and because they have made such abundant offerings to me,
these great-minded ones, when they die and pass from the yakṣa lineage,
will go to the higher realms and rejoice for a long time.

"In times to come, by miraculous power,
they will go to buddhafields, and in a single instant
those great-minded ones will worship ten million Victors.
The Victors, knowing their forbearance, will give them instruction.

"By miraculous power, throughout the worlds of the ten directions,
they will move and travel without signs.
Knowing these worlds to be like emanations,
unattached to the world, the wise will practice the practice.

"Relying on this, having richly worshiped the Victors,
they will attain unsurpassed awakening.
Their fields will be limitless,
and they will set free limitless beings.

"As buddhas of limitless knowledge, renowned,
their name will be Wise in the World.
The lifespan of those Victors of peaceful mind
will be a full thousand eons.

"The Saṅgha of their śrāvakas that will arise,
and their many children of the Victors, as numerous as the hosts of stars,
will all attain their aspirations through bliss.
With those called Wise in the World there will be no suffering."

Hearing these words, the worlds together with their gods
sought awakening with minds full of joy.
Satisfied, they undertook diligence steadfastly,
and all became firm through the power of diligence.

This is the eleventh chapter, the Prophecy of the Yakṣas.

Then Druma, king of the kinnaras, together with eighty million kinnaras, saw those offerings of the asuras and the others, up to the offerings of the yakṣas, and heard the prophecies as well. He was filled with great amazement, and thought: "In this way you do not apprehend even a sentient being. There is no life, no one who is born, no human (manuja), no child of Manu (mānava), no person, no self, and nothing whatsoever belonging to a self. Even the aggregates, elements, and sense sources on which the designation 'sentient being' is imputed are not apprehended. Even the karma through whose existence maturation would be designated is not apprehended. Awakening is not apprehended, bodhisattvas are not apprehended, and buddhas are not apprehended. And yet the Blessed One makes prophecies concerning bodhisattvas. This is wondrous and marvelous!

"With what intention did the Blessed One prophesy that the bodhisattvas will become buddhas? With what intention did the Tathāgata make prophecies concerning the bodhisattva's genuine accomplishment, the greatness of the buddhas, the ripening of sentient beings, the arrangement of buddhafields, birth in them and their purity, the bodhisattvas' going from field to field, the worship of the tathāgatas, the power of that worship, and the establishing of sentient beings; prophesying 'one will become a buddha after so many eons,' 'the buddhas, the blessed ones, will remain for so many eons,' 'the assembly of śrāvakas of those buddhas, the blessed ones, will number so many,' and 'after they have passed into parinirvāṇa, the true Dharma will remain for so many eons'? With what intention did the Blessed One have in mind that sentient beings will pass into nirvāṇa through non-grasping?"

Then Druma, king of the kinnaras, these doubts having arisen in him, bowed with palms joined toward where the Blessed One was, and put his question to the Blessed One in a song of verses:

"Having heard the speech of the Victor,
O you of great wisdom, doubt has arisen in me.
Emptiness and prophecy:
Sugata, these I do not understand.

"O moon among speakers, how is it
that you say the dharmadhātu is unchanging, empty,
and without intrinsic nature?
What then is this worship, O supreme among persons?

"There is no birth at all; how then
can there be the generation of the mind for awakening?
That cryptic speech spoken by the Supreme Person
I do not understand.

"Lord of the rulers of men, how is it
that where there is no cessation you speak of death and transmigration?
Through a true explanation of this,
O Sage, my doubt will be laid to rest.

"Being like an illusion, yet born as a god:
how is it that you say this?
These cryptic sayings too,
Lion of the Śākyas, I do not understand.

"There is no abiding at all,
yet you say to rely on a spiritual friend; how is that?
These cryptic sayings too,
Bull among men, I do not understand.

"There is no movement at all, yet you speak of deeds:
how is that, Supreme among two-footed beings?
These cryptic sayings too,
Blessed One who has dispelled the foe, I do not understand.

"Intrinsic nature is empty, and emptiness too
is to be realized, the Protector says; how is that?
Doubts of this kind have arisen in me;
O you of incomparable wisdom, I do not understand.

"You declared that peace exists,
and said that all dharmas are peace; how is that?
O Unequaled One, these cryptic sayings,
please explain them to me.

"Though your body is beautiful,
you say that dharmas are like space; how is that?
If one utterly abandons the Dharma,
how is it that the unwise fall into hell?

"O Steadfast One, you speak of the heavens, the world,
and the other destinies as well;
there is no doer of that karma,
yet you speak of one's own karma; how is that?
This manner of your teaching as well,
O Invincible One, I do not understand.

"Since I have doubts about this, O Protector,
Sugata, please dispel my doubt.
There has been no accumulation of virtue,
yet you speak of the genuine accomplishment of awakening.
This manner of speech of the king of ascetics,
what you have declared thus, I do not understand.

"You say that dharmas are inexhaustible,
yet you teach the exhaustion of karma through abandonment.
About this too, O you of immeasurable intellect,
I am torn by doubt.

"You have declared the true limit,
yet you also teach unerring generosity.
This too, O you whose eyes are free of film,
who have abandoned evil, please explain.

"Apart from the All-Seeing One,
no other is able to dispel
this doubt of mine; therefore
I place my faith in the supreme Victor."

Then the Blessed One, having heard these doubts that Druma, king of the kinnaras, had asked about, replied in verse to Druma, king of the kinnaras, in order to answer him:

"You said, 'Emptiness and prophecy,
these I do not understand.'
If something were not empty,
the Victors would not prophesy it.

Why is that? If in this way
things abided in their own intrinsic nature,
they would be changeless and permanent,
and in them there would be no increase and no decrease.
Just as in a perfectly clear mirror
a reflection appears
that has no intrinsic nature,
so, Druma, you should understand phenomena.

"If the dharmadhātu is changeless,
which limb of offering changes?"
So you ask. Examine the limbs,
and, Druma, investigate this offering.
The wise understand the dharmadhātu
as changeless in every respect.
The childish are confused about this;
those whose minds are trained know it as nonexistent.

You said, "There is no arising, and yet the mind is generated;
this I do not understand."
This is the intentional speech of the one endowed with the ten powers;
do not take it otherwise, but listen.
The childish who delight in saṃsāra,
who take great delight in saṃsāra, their minds attached,
with wrong minds and wrong perceptions,
come to experience the suffering of saṃsāra.
If I were to teach them the mindless Dharma
that they have never heard before,
those of childish understanding
would be utterly bewildered by it.
But once they have heard of the mind inclined toward awakening
and of the benefits of awakening,
they will attain that vast, self-arisen mind
that nothing can overpower.

You said, "There is no cessation, and yet there is death and transmigration;
this too I do not understand."
Death and transmigration were rightly taught;
do not take it otherwise, but listen.
To those who cling to permanence,
saying, "In this there is nothing changeless or permanent;
these things are impermanent,"
the Sugata teaches death and transmigration.

You doubted, "If things are like illusions,
how is there birth among the gods?"
To learners who have not reached the end
and to ordinary people, heaven was taught.

As for what you said: "There is no abiding,
and yet one should rely on spiritual friends,"
in order to become free of abiding,
rely on the spiritual friend who does not abide.

You said, "There is no movement, and yet
I do not understand the teaching of activities."
Understand that the assembly of a chariot's parts
and the function of a chariot are different.
To people who assert that a self exists
and that the act has been done by that self,
the Sugata taught
that there is no action and no movement.

As for what I said: "By intrinsic nature things are empty,
and emptiness too is to be understood,"
it is to teach the childish of wrong understanding
who do not know that intrinsic nature is empty
that the Sugata proclaims,
"Intrinsic nature is empty."

As for what you said: "Produced by the conceptions of imagination,
bound by false conception,
yet there is peace, and all phenomena
are seen as peace,"
those who are ignorant and deluded
give rise to craving from imagination.
Just as at a mirage
thirst arises in the thirsty through imagining,
so that unskilled one wrongly imagines
a cause of conception that does not exist.
Although he sees it as water,
in the mirage there is no water.
In the same way, stirred up by imagination,
the childish who are full of desire
conceive the unlovely as lovely.
What seems possessed of loveliness is by its nature
without loveliness, like the water of a mirage:
in the mirage there is no water,
and likewise in form there is no loveliness.
Though that form is not lovely,
the childish with wrong perceptions
are bound by conceiving it as lovely.

You said, "Phenomena are like space,
and to reject them is to fall into hell."
The unskilled, who have not seen nonexistence,
become afraid.
If intrinsic nature is empty of essential nature,
those who cling to self and do not understand,
when they hear "empty,"
grow afraid, thinking, "I will become selfless."
Clinging firmly to a self,
they tie a knot in empty space,
and rejecting excellent emptiness,
the unskilled in mind fall into the great hell.

I taught the heavenly worlds
and the other streams of beings' destinies,
and I also taught karma,
yet I thoroughly refuted an agent.
Understand that in reality beings
are explained to be like a dream.
In no dream whatever is there a real thing,
yet those with wrong understanding cling to it.
I taught the modes of karma,
yet in the ten directions there is no agent.
It is just as when fire arises
from trees stirred by the wind:
neither wind nor tree has the thought,
"We shall produce this fire,"
and yet fire arises.
So too is the agent of karma.

As for the claim, "There is no accumulation of merit,
and yet there is true accomplishment of awakening
and there is good conduct,"
listen properly to how this is.
A human lifespan is a hundred years,
and one says that a person lives that long,
yet the years are not piled into a heap.
Accomplishment, too, is just like that.

As for what was said, "There is no exhaustion,"
while I said, "Karma is exhausted":
in the way of emptiness there is no exhaustion;
in the way of convention, exhaustion is taught.
I have explained the limit of reality;
even the inverted is the limit of reality.
The childish of wrong understanding
do not know the limit of reality.

All characteristics are one and the same.
Druma, listen to the certainty
Of those who have entered the path of wisdom
That knows there are no characteristics.

The awakening that is entered through a single letter
I have taught to the wise.
That all phenomena are unmade
Is taught as the dhāraṇī of the door of a.

To those who practice in this way
I have taught the infinity of characteristics.
To enter into a way such as this
Is taught as the dhāraṇī of the door of a.

That all phenomena are unceasing
Is taught as entering the Dharma through a.
This teaching of the dhāraṇī of the door of a,
Druma, you should understand as well.

That phenomena are free of conceptual thought
Is also declared to be entering the Dharma.
That all phenomena are unchanging, too,
Is taught by a, the door to all phenomena.

These dhāraṇīs of the door of a,
Druma, understand them in this way.

Of what is spoken of as inexhaustible and as exhausted,
All phenomena are to be called inexhaustible.
All those who possess the ten powers
Teach this as the dhāraṇī of the door of the inexhaustible.

All phenomena have no door;
Since they do not exist, no door arises.
This too is a door of dhāraṇī;
Relying on a, one should enter it.

"All phenomena are inconceivable":
So the Sugata has taught, just as it truly is.
This teaching of the dhāraṇī of the door of a,
Druma, you should understand as well.

"All phenomena are bodiless; that is awakening":
What I have taught to the wise in this way
Is also a door of dhāraṇī;
Relying on a, one should enter it.

Phenomena have no coming;
Since they do not exist, they have no coming either.
This too is a door of dhāraṇī;
Relying on a, one should enter it.

Phenomena cannot be designated;
Since they do not exist, they cannot be designated.
This too is a door of dhāraṇī;
Relying on a, one should enter it.

Phenomena have no intrinsic nature;
Since they do not exist, they have no intrinsic nature.
This too is a door of dhāraṇī;
The Sugata has taught it as dhāraṇī.

Hear from me the door of dhāraṇī:
All phenomena are beyond mental engagement.
This too is a door of dhāraṇī;
The Sugata has taught it as dhāraṇī.

"Since they do not exist, all phenomena are without obscuration":
This, which the Tathāgata has taught,
Is also a door of dhāraṇī;
Relying on a, one should understand it.

All phenomena are unobstructed;
That which could be obstructed is not found.
This too is a door of dhāraṇī;
Relying on a, one should enter it.

The wise should know all phenomena
As unborn, as of a single characteristic.
Whatever phenomena are unborn
Should be known as nameless.

Phenomena are not real;
All of them without exception lack intrinsic nature.
This too is a door of dhāraṇī;
Relying on a, one should understand it.

Whatever is unborn and has not come to be
Should be taught as unseen,
Because there is no seer,
And because those things too do not exist.

Therefore all phenomena are equal in their nonexistence,
Of a single characteristic, with no basis for characteristics.
They are like space;
No phenomenon exists.

Thus there is no excess and no deficiency,
No meditative equipoise and no duality,
No torment, no coolness, and no heat;
It is beyond the range of sight, because it does not exist.

Not straight and not crooked,
Not darkness and not light,
Unseen and unheard:
Because it does not exist, know it as dhāraṇī.

Without guile and without uprightness,
Phenomena are not clear, nor do they move;
Without agitation and without anger,
They do nothing, and are not pacified.

There is no falling and no remaining,
No going, no turning back, no thought of going,
No sleeping and no not sleeping;
Know them to be without waking.

Not sighted and not blind,
Not seeing and not veiled in darkness,
Not hidden and not displayed,
Not tamed and not untamed,

Not excited and not calm,
Not saṃsāra and not nirvāṇa,
Not true and not false: in this way
Know the domain of the Victors.

This is for the sake of taming the world;
About this there is no doubt whatsoever.
The doubt that is to be cut off
Has never arisen in the ultimate sense.

Then Druma, king of the kinnaras, completely satisfied by the Blessed One's answer to his questions and by the definitive teaching of the dhāraṇī, became eager to make offerings to the Blessed One. He magically emanated eighty million multistoried palaces, some resting on trees, some on lotuses, and some on mountains, made of the seven precious substances, beautified with jeweled parasols, beautified with the fringes of the parasols, adorned with victory banners and pennants, and adorned with canopies and fringes. He also emanated eighty million kinnara musical instruments and cymbals, and made offerings to the Blessed One with all kinds of flowers: flowers grown on mountains wafting with fragrance, flowers grown on the plains, and flowers grown in the water. Then, together with the host of kinnaras, seated in those eighty million multistoried palaces, he circumambulated the Blessed One three times, and he scattered, strewed, and showered those flowers grown on the plains and grown in the water upon the Blessed One. Then they descended from those palaces, went on foot, and circumambulated the Blessed One three times. They bowed their heads at the Blessed One's feet, joined their palms, and, gazing at the Blessed One without blinking and recollecting the qualities of the Buddha in every way, they sat down to one side.

Then the Blessed One, perceiving the superior intention of Druma, king of the kinnaras, and his retinue, smiled.

Then the venerable Aśvajit addressed the Blessed One in verse:

"Sugata, you whose eyes are free of stain,
You do not smile without a cause.
You who are truly honored by gods and humans,
Please declare the cause of your smile.
Seeing the Omniscient One smile,
The assembly has fallen into doubt.
As people gaze at the new moon,
They gaze upon the moonlike face of the Speaker.
Beautiful Sugata, who today
Has given rise to supreme knowledge of the nondual Dharma?
I have come to be in doubt;
Supreme and excellent among humans, dispel this doubt.
Sugata, who is it whose faith
In the nature of reality has made you,
Blessed One, smile in this way?
Supreme Speaker, remove my doubt.
This assembly, endowed with all,
Hearing the Blessed One's teaching, will rejoice in mind,
Be without doubt, its hesitation cut off,
And abide just as you have taught."

Thus the supreme among two-legged beings,
Out of love for beings,
And to cut off their doubt,
Spoke the prophecy of Druma and the kinnaras.

Then the Blessed One again spoke to the venerable Aśvajit in verse:

"Among the great multitudes of people,
This will make the Buddha's qualities shine forth.
You have asked about Druma's prophecy;
Aśvajit, you have asked well.
Toward the unobscured vision of wisdom,
The wondrous wisdom of the Sugata,
Make your mind clear with faith,
And listen to me with an untiring mind.
For the benefit and happiness of the world,
I shall declare the fruit
Of the doubt that Druma raised;
Hear from me how that doubt is cut.

Druma and the other kinnaras,
Eighty koṭis who have come here,
Having made offerings to me,
Will die, pass from here, and go to the higher realms.
For ninety koṭis of eons there,
They will circle among gods and humans.
Having cultivated the five superknowledges,
They will become powerful, masters of their minds.
Those lions among humans
Will send forth emanations to countless buddha fields,
While they themselves, dwelling in the abodes of the gods,
Taste the bliss and joy of meditative concentration.
For a further ninety koṭis of eons,
Having circled among gods and humans,
In their own various fields
They will fully awaken to enlightenment.

In the eon called Ever Shining,
Those lions among humans, protectors of the world,
Will have unattached wisdom and immeasurable vision,
The ten powers, and boundless splendor.
In their fields no being
Who is not fully ripened will be born.
All who arise there through the śrāvaka vehicle
Will be in their final existence.
In those fields of the lions among humans,
Whatever bodhisattvas are born
Will be bound to but one more life,
And will attain the awakening of the supreme among humans.

Those among them who were foremost
Set out with this intention:
'We shall purify
Immeasurable buddha fields.'
These magnanimous beings
Long ago made vast aspirations.
When they have died and passed from here,
They will purify their own fields through that intention.
The array of the fields
Of those who have crushed the afflictions
Will have enjoyments like those of Tuṣita,
And every lower realm will be entirely purified.
In their fields, in any way at all,
No unfavorable condition will ever arise.
Those who have purified their fields
Will lead beings, too, to happiness."

Thus the Protector of the World
Spoke the prophecy of the kinnaras.
Hearing that prophecy, those in the assembly
All rejoiced.

This was the twelfth chapter, the Prophecy of the Kinnaras.

Then the eighty thousand Mālādhara gods called the Elephant Bearers saw the offerings made by all, from the asuras up to the kinnaras, and heard their prophecies. Satisfied, pleased and delighted, overjoyed, full of joy and gladness, they became devoted to the qualities of the Buddha and set about making offerings to the Blessed One. They filled the whole surroundings of the great city of Kapilavastu, for up to sixty yojanas, knee-deep with māndārava flowers. Then they circumambulated the Blessed One three times and praised him in these verses:

"Lord of peace with smiling face,
Loving toward all living creatures,
Compassionate Sugata,
Lord of the world, tiger among humans,
For as long as this assembly,
Devoted to the Buddha's Dharma, remains here,
We bow our heads to the Compassionate One,
Whose supreme feet are beautiful and joyful.
Just as at the full moon in the sky
People pay homage to the moon,
So too these hosts of gods
Gaze upon the face of the Blessed One.
You who practiced immeasurable virtue,
Endowed with an immeasurable mass of wisdom,
Possessing an immeasurable mass of splendor,
Supreme in intellect, we bow our heads to you.
Possessor of the ten powers, dense with glory,
Fearless in the four truths, dispeller of the three stains,
Endowed with the eighteen qualities of a Victor,
Free of doubt, supreme Speaker,
Possessor of the thirty-two major marks,
Endowed with the eighty excellent minor marks,
Your mind a peerless banner of Indra,
Sugata, homage to you.
You have reached the perfection of the power of moral discipline,
Abide in the power of samādhi, unshakable,
Bear the weapon of the power of wisdom,
And are the Victor, the Sage who crushed the power of Māra.
Your supreme power of wisdom has grown full;
You are without stinginess, and have trained the śrāvakas;
You act with a peaceful mind
And tame the world with its gods.
Lion among men, Sugata,
You have found the Dharma that pacifies delusion;
May we too, here,
Attain the qualities of the ten powers.
Just as you, Sage, know the thoughts
Of beings and act accordingly,
So may we too, illuminating the world,
Do likewise for beings, gods and humans included.
Thus, for the sake of beings,
Taking up compassion,
We shall free beings sunk in the ocean of saṃsāra
From the stream of existence.
May we become guides and great guides
Of the world with its gods;
We shall release beings tormented by hundreds of sufferings
From the stream of existence."

Then the Blessed One, knowing the intentions of the Elephant Bearer gods, smiled at that time. Then the venerable Aśvajit addressed the Blessed One in verse:

"Sugata, Guide,

Without a cause you do not smile.
You of the ten powers, what is the cause
Of the smile you have shown? Please declare it.
Seeing the smile upon the Victor's face,
Beings and the whole assembly have come to doubt,
And we long to hear the meaning of the smile.
Great-purposed one, the meaning of your smile,
Declare it, declare it, supreme Guide!
You who are king of the ascetics,
Please declare it, so that our doubt is cut off.

Who has pleased the Victor?
Who today has done what is supreme and unsurpassed?
Who today has reached the depths?
Sugata, resolve my doubt.
Who today has made the abode of Māra
Shaken, distracted, and utterly thrown into turmoil?
Who today has filled all the abodes of the gods
With minds of the greatest joy?
Victor, Sage, explain this,
And quickly resolve the doubt of beings.
Hearing it, Sugata, all of your assembly
Will be filled with joy of mind.

Then the Blessed One replied to the venerable Aśvajit in verse:

Aśvajit, because you have asked
About the prophecy of the Garland-Bearing Elephant gods,
Great benefit for many beings
Will today indeed be accomplished.
Unless asked, the Victor of the ten powers
Does not prophesy the awakening of his supreme heirs.
So for the benefit of many beings
You have today put your question to the Victor.

The host of the Garland-Bearing Elephant gods,
Setting their minds on me and honoring me,
After countless eons have passed,
Will defeat the afflictions and become victors.
When they die and pass from here,
They will be born in harmony among humans,
And will honor immeasurable billions of victors
With the finest flowers.
Everywhere they will give rise to the mind of a sugata
And make it grow.
They will praise the self-arisen lion among humans
With verses.

In the future all of these
Will honor countless, immeasurable myriads of victors
With flowers, and with verses
Will praise those supreme among humans.
Having honored those sugatas
And praised the bodies of the mighty ones,
In the eon called Supreme Joy
They will appear in the world as guides.
These eighty thousand of unblemished fame,
Who wander in the forests, all of them,
Will become sugatas within a single eon,
Each called Possessor of the Flower Banner.

There will be no hells there, no animals,
No lower realms, no world of Yama.
In that field there will be no asuras;
All unfavorable states will be utterly destroyed.
For the Garland-Bearing Elephant gods,
Enjoyments will be just like those
Of the Heaven of the Thirty-Three.
The lifespan of beings will be immeasurable.
At that time the word "nonvirtue" will not be heard,
So what need is there to mention doing nonvirtue?
There the people of those supreme guides
Will all be devoted to the Dharma.
Living beings by the myriads of billions,
As many as the sands of the Ganges,
Those of the ten powers will set free,
And they will teach the Dharma of non-abiding.

The relics of those of unblemished fame
Will spread far and wide,
And from their relics as well
Many bodies of victors will emerge.
Seeing the miraculous bodies from the relics
Of those endowed with boundless fame,
Many myriads of billions of living beings
Will give rise to the mind set on awakening.

When they had heard and understood the prophecy
Of the Garland-Bearing Elephant gods declared by the Guide,
The whole assembly at that time
Was supremely joyful at heart.

This concludes the thirteenth chapter, the Prophecy of the Garland-Bearing Elephant Gods.

Then the ninety thousand gods of the realm of the Four Great Kings, having seen the offerings of all those beings, from the asuras up to the Garland-Bearing Elephant gods, and having heard the prophecies, were satisfied, delighted, and overjoyed. Supremely glad, filled with joy and happiness of mind, they developed an intense aspiration to attain the qualities of a buddha. It was like this: a man riding in a boat of unbaked clay who has reached the middle of the water, thinking, "The boat is going to break," develops an intense aspiration to get from this shore to the far shore. In the same way, those ninety thousand gods of the realm of the Four Great Kings developed faith in the greatness of the Buddha's Dharma and came to have an intense aspiration to attain the qualities of a buddha. Seeking the Buddha's Dharma, they set about making offerings to the Blessed One.

Together with the Four Great Kings they emanated ninety thousand jeweled canopies, brilliant and beautiful to behold. Most of these were made entirely of red pearl, star-colored gems, beryl, gold, and vajra. They also emanated ninety thousand divine musical instruments and cymbals. Holding those ninety thousand canopies of various precious jewels and playing those ninety thousand instruments and cymbals, they descended from the sky above and circled the Blessed One three times. They scattered upon the Blessed One, scattered widely, and scattered very widely, māndārava flowers and great māndārava flowers, mañjūṣaka flowers and great mañjūṣaka flowers, paruṣaka flowers and great paruṣaka flowers, and emanated flowers. Having made these offerings, they again circled the Blessed One three times and praised him with these verses:

Having seen the qualities of the Victor,
We, the gods, ninety thousand in all,
Have come into your presence, Guide,
With palms joined together.
Free of agitation, free of dejection,
Free of desire, free of regret,
Not foolish, free of astonishment,
To the supreme among two-legged beings we bow.

You have destroyed the streams of existence,
Fully purified unknowing,
Pulled out the stake of craving,
And toppled the banner of pride.
You have drawn out the thorn,
And cast off the poison in the wound.
You act just as you know;
Guide, you have healed the wounds.

You have abandoned all afflictions
And severed all bonds.
Free from every fetter,
Sage, you have passed beyond all attachment.
Victor, your sphere of activity
Is emptiness and signlessness.
Blessed One, in you there is
No wishing of any kind at all.

You have pulled craving out by the roots,
And drawn your mind out of ignorance as well.
The four kinds of distortion
You have abandoned completely, in every way.
The limit of reality, which the world does not fathom,
And before which it grows frightened and afraid
As a deer before the hunter,
You have seen.
Beings have fallen into the view of the perishing aggregates;
Because they do not understand emptiness,
They are wrapped in the darkness of ignorance
And take delight in the prison of saṃsāra.
Truly, just as it is,
You see that world as like an empty fist.
You have taught the light of wisdom
Through which the eye becomes pure.
You have taken the aggregates apart as empty;
Name and form, too, are hollow,
And the elements and the sense sources
You have taken apart as selfless and empty.
Whatever phenomenon is designated
By whatever phenomenon and whatever name,
That phenomenon is empty of that phenomenon:
So you, the Sage, have taught it to be empty.
When a magician conjures
A great crowd of people where there are none,
No sentient being can be designated there,
Just as there is no life-force and no person.
In the same way, the aggregates, the elements,
The faculties and the sense sources
Are not real, they are illusions:
So the holy being has taught.
Just as a painter
Paints the figure of a woman,
And though he shows the level and the uneven,
The heavy and the light,
It deceives the eyes of the childish,
While nothing in it is uneven at all,
So, though the dharmadhātu is even,
The childish are confused about it.
Those who know, well trained in your training,
Do not become confused:
This you, the Omniscient One, have taught
Like a myrobalan fruit in the palm of the hand.
You of great wisdom
Have lit the lamp of wisdom,
Turned the wheel of Dharma,
And taught the Dharma to beings.
So may we too swiftly
Attain unsurpassed and peaceful awakening,
And, just like you, O Guide,
Turn the wheel of Dharma.
To beings who wander
On these wrong paths of saṃsāra,
Just as the Guide has taught,
We too shall swiftly teach the Dharma.

Then the Blessed One, understanding the superior intention of the gods of the realm of the Four Great Kings, smiled. Then the venerable Aśvajit addressed the Blessed One in verse:

Perfect Buddha, the Guide
Does not smile without a cause.
Great Hero, please explain
The cause of the smile you have smiled.
Holy Being, this assembly,
Seeing the Sugata smile today,
Has fallen into great uncertainty;
All of them have come to doubt.
Who today has defeated Māra?
By whom has hesitation been cut off?
Who today has realized the Dharma?
Supreme Being, please explain.
Who today has made offerings to you?
By whom has the Teacher been served and honored?
Since all beings have come to hesitation,
Guide, please explain.
Supreme among two-legged beings,
Guide, please explain in such a way
That when they hear these explanations
The doubts of beings will be cut off.
Guide, please teach in such a way
That those who abide on the path
To omniscience in every way
May swiftly attain awakening.

Then the Blessed One again addressed the venerable Aśvajit in verse:

For the benefit of beings
You thought, "The perfect Buddha, the protector,
Should be asked the causes of his smile."
Aśvajit, it is good that you have asked.
For the benefit of the whole world
I shall explain what will be a teaching
Pleasing and delightful:
Bring forth mindfulness and listen.
Fully ninety thousand gods
Today have made this kind of offering
To the Tathāgata
With minds of deep faith.
They have praised the Buddha,
They have come to know emptiness,
They have found certainty about phenomena,
And they abide firmly in my teaching.
In the past, too, these gods
Made offerings to eighty koṭis of buddhas,
And in the future as well
They will make offerings to endless koṭis of buddhas.
As long as those guides-to-be
Have not made offerings to koṭis of buddhas
And have not served and honored them,
So long they will not attain awakening.
Then, a very long time after,
All of them will become supreme persons;
Their name will be Bearer of Light Rays,
And they will become sages who know the whole world.
Those lamps of the world
Will have gatherings of śrāvakas:
Eighty gatherings will take place
Of those endowed with supreme understanding.
In the time of those of unobstructed vision,
Human beings will live,
Their life faculty enduring
For a full eighty koṭis of years.
Those of infinite wisdom
Will have śrāvakas free of desire,
Bearers of their final bodies,
Appearing in measureless koṭis.
Of those glorious protectors of the world,
Compassionate toward beings,
When they have passed into nirvāṇa
The relics too will spread most widely.
After the lords of men,
Those glorious guides, have passed into nirvāṇa,
With the stūpas of the Victors
The buddha fields will be beautifully adorned.
To all those stūpas
Of the protectors and saviors of the world,
Hundreds and thousands and hundreds of billions
Of living beings will make offerings;
They will raise the mind toward supreme awakening
And will attain nirvāṇa.
Even after those self-arisen ones have passed into nirvāṇa,
Their holy Dharma will endure,
Remaining for a full eighty koṭis
Of hundreds of billions of years.
Upheld and fully protected
By the sons of the Dharma King, it will remain.
Out of compassion for the world,
And to bring the Śākyas to maturity,
The Lamp of the World gave this prophecy
To those of the realm of the Four Great Kings.
When they had heard it, all the people
Rejoiced and were utterly delighted;
Their hearts content and their minds gladdened,
They praised the Teacher.

This concludes the fourteenth chapter, the Prophecy concerning the Realm of the Four Great Kings.

The sixth fascicle.

Then Śakra, lord of the gods, together with the rest of the eight hundred million gods of the Heaven of the Thirty-Three, saw the offerings made by the asuras, the garuḍas, the nāga maidens, the nāgas, the kumbhāṇḍas, the gandharvas, the yakṣas, the kinnaras, the garland bearers with their elephants, and the gods of the realm of the Four Great Kings. They also heard the prophecies. They were satisfied, delighted, and pleased with the teachings of the Buddha. Supreme joy, delight, and gladness arose in them, and they were deeply moved. Of their own wish, Śakra, lord of the gods, and the others set about making offerings to the Blessed One.

They emanated eight hundred million jewel mansions. These were bright and beautiful to behold, made of the seven precious substances, and adorned with garlands of red pearls and garlands of beryl. They were adorned with various garlands of gold and with garlands of star-colored precious jewels. They were adorned with eight stories, with balconies, with pavilions rising tier on tier, and with windows. They were adorned with parasols, victory banners, and flags, and with lion thrones made of divine substance. They were adorned with great canopies and with nets of bells and tinkling bells, made beautiful by hosts of goddesses, and filled with the resounding music of divine instruments and cymbals.

They also emanated eight hundred million chariots drawn by thoroughbred horses, finely adorned with divine features and made beautiful with parasols, victory banners, and flags.

The whole great city of Kapilavastu and the country around it, for sixty yojanas in every direction, they filled completely with māndārava flowers and with those divine offerings.

They also emanated eight hundred million Airāvaṇa elephants. Each of these Airāvaṇa elephants had eight hundred million heads. Each head of these Airāvaṇa elephants had six tusks. Each tusk held seven ponds. Each pond held seven thousand lotuses. Each lotus had eighty thousand petals. On each petal stood seven goddesses, and each of these goddesses had a retinue of seven. All of them were adorned with every kind of ornament.

Having emanated all this, they placed the jewel mansions in front, set the Airāvaṇa elephants in the middle, and arranged the chariots behind. Then they set out and made offerings to the Blessed One.

From those jewel mansions there rained down powders of divine sandalwood, aloeswood, utpala, and tamāla leaf, and a rain of gold dust. There also rained down divine flowers: māndārava and great māndārava, mañjūṣaka and great mañjūṣaka, paruṣaka and great paruṣaka, white flowers and great white flowers, and emanated flowers. There also rained down flowers of gold, silver, beryl, crystal, red pearl, coral, emerald, and all the seven precious substances.

Of those goddesses, some sang, some danced, some played music, and some moved their bodies in graceful postures, just like the offerings of the asuras. Having made offerings with those Airāvaṇa elephants and those jewel mansions, they then mounted the eight hundred million chariots drawn by thoroughbred horses, circled the Blessed One three times, and presented a great many divine articles of every kind. Eight hundred million divine instruments and cymbals also resounded from the sky. On each of those eight hundred million chariots they emanated eight hundred million further goddesses, and of these goddesses too, some sang, some danced, some played music, and some moved their bodies in graceful postures, just as in the offerings of the asuras.

Then those eight hundred million gods of the Heaven of the Thirty-Three thought, "Since these emanated goddesses are making offerings to the Blessed One, all phenomena must surely be like emanations." They thought, "All these phenomena are just as the Tathāgata has taught: they are like emanations." Knowing all phenomena to be like emanations, they gained freedom from doubt about the Dharma. Free of doubt and without hesitation, they circled the Blessed One three times, bowed their heads at the Blessed One's feet, and sat down to one side.

They contemplated all phenomena as like emanations. They saw themselves too as like emanations, saw those offerings too as like emanations, and saw the Blessed One too as like an emanation. Then they knew correctly that the teachings the Blessed One had taught were also like emanations. Knowing that all phenomena are like emanations, they were free of doubt and without hesitation, and at that time they praised the Blessed One in verse:

"Whatever offerings all beings have made,
And whatever these emanated offerings have done,
And the worship the gods have offered to the Buddha,
All these phenomena are likewise just emanation.

Whatever teachings the Guide has spoken,
And all the prophecies the Tathāgata has made,
'All these are like emanations':
Such understanding arose in these hosts of gods.

Blessed One, just as these emanations are,
So too the Teacher is like an emanation.
Since we have understood the Victor's prophecy,
About this we have no doubt.

Knowing ordinary beings, the Victor
Designated them as having reached the stage of the childish.
All of these too are just like emanations;
So too is the Sugata's prophecy of beings.

Those in training and those beyond training,
The Blessed One's śrāvakas who have reached the stage of the tamed,
All of them too, Blessed One, are like emanations.
About the Blessed One's teaching we have no doubt."

"Blessed One, those who are devoted to silence,
who, like the rhinoceros, are without a companion and have abandoned the afflictions,
they too, Blessed One, are like the emanation.
I have no doubt about the Sugata's teaching.

"Those who, for the happiness and benefit of all beings,
carefully practice the conduct of the Victor's awakening,
the bodhisattvas, the Sugata's heirs, they too
are no different: they are like this emanation.

"The Sugatas, self-arisen, supreme guides,
with the ten powers, boundless wisdom and compassion,
exalted in the power of gnosis, lords of the world,
they too share the nature of an emanation such as this.

"Whatever dharmas the Guide has taught here,
without abode, peaceful and cool, free of afflictions,
pacifying suffering, final, beyond all counting,
they too are just like these emanations.

"Blessed One, here there are no dharmas whatsoever
that are not of the nature of emanation.
Now that we have understood this teaching of Gautama,
we have abandoned every doubt.
Seeing all dharmas as like this emanation,
we too shall become protectors of the world.
Those who practice in this domain of the Sugata
will without doubt become self-arisen guides."

Then the Blessed One, perceiving at that time the superior intention of those eight hundred million gods of the Heaven of the Thirty-Three, smiled. Then the venerable Aśvajit addressed the Blessed One in verse:

"You of measureless fame and boundless splendor,
the Sugatas do not smile without a cause.
Since the Guide has smiled here,
Victor, speak words about the smile and dispel our doubt.
Knowing that this smile has come from the face of the self-arisen Sugata,
the world is filled with wonder.
Sage, please clear away the doubts of beings;
make these beings free of doubt.

"These hosts of gods, with minds full of delight,
have praised the Victor and worshipped him with divine substances.
Sugata, you have smiled for their sake;
Victor, please speak and gladden this assembly.

"You whose light is stainless like the moon, of boundless splendor,
so that, by hearing this word of the Sugata,
all beings, O Protector, may be gladdened,
Victor, knower of the gods' offering, please speak.

"Sugata, these hosts of gods
have fully realized all dharmas to be like emanations.
Possessor of the ten powers, declare this meaning;
you who are free of all dimness of vision, tell of the training of the gods.
These beings, freed from doubt,
will undertake the conduct of awakening, as the Sage did.
When this most heroic assembly has heard it,
understanding your intent, they will undertake the vast."

Then the Blessed One replied to the venerable Aśvajit in verse:

"Knowing that these hosts of gods are filled with delight,
Aśvajit, the timely words you have spoken
have come at the proper time.
The smile you asked about I now explain.

"Knowing the gods' thoughts, you asked about the smile,
and by that you have done great good for beings.
I shall reveal the cause of my smiling;
listen to me with a joyful and faithful mind.

"These hosts of gods have understood
all these dharmas without exception to be like emanations;
with unattached gnosis and boundless vision
they will become protectors of the world, free of all dimness.

"In the stream of existence, formerly, wandering in saṃsāra,
they worshipped lords of men as many as the sands of the Ganges,
yet in that stream of existence, from those sages,
they did not see dharmas to be like emanations.

"Because they have made this supreme offering to me here,
they directly see dharmas to be like emanations.
Whatever is done for this teaching of the Sugata
is never wasted and will meet no obstacle.

"These hosts of gods have made offerings to me,
and because they know all dharmas to be like emanations,
when the eon called Padma comes,
they will become moons among speakers, supreme among men.

"The supreme name they will bear, those foremost of the herd,
will be Sugata Indraketu King, the supremely skilled.
Teaching all dharmas to be like emanations,
they will free countless multitudes of beings.

"Those who are careful in the teachings of those Sugatas
contemplate these dharmas as like emanations.
Whoever relies carefully on that calm abiding
will swiftly attain the unsurpassed, supreme, peaceful awakening."

This was the fifteenth chapter, the Prophecy to the Heaven of the Thirty-Three.

Then, at that time, four hundred million Yāma gods saw the offerings of all those beings, from the asuras up to the gods of the Heaven of the Thirty-Three, and heard the prophecies. They were satisfied, pleased and delighted, and joy, gladness and happiness arose in them. They knew that the Blessed One is unobstructed in giving prophecies, and that the qualities of a buddha are delightful: once these are attained, there is nothing whatsoever that is unknown, unseen, unrealized or unactualized, and there is no arising, no ceasing, no karma and no ripening of karma. Thus they developed a longing for the qualities of a buddha.

For the Tathāgata has fully comprehended the two, the conventional and the ultimate, and what is to be known is contained entirely within the conventional and the ultimate. Moreover, because the Blessed One has thoroughly seen, thoroughly known and perfectly actualized emptiness, he is therefore called the Omniscient One. Of these, the conventional the Tathāgata has seen as the conduct of the world.

The ultimate is inexpressible. It is not to be known, not to be cognized, and not to be thoroughly known. It is not shown, not taught, not heard, and not made to be heard. It is not known, not cognized, not shown, and not seen. It is not examined, it does not allow itself to be grasped, it is not realized, not attained, and not obtained. It is not approached, not cast off, not set down, and not abandoned. It is without activity, without agent, and without pacification. It is not reviled and not praised. It is not gain and not loss, not pleasure and not pain, not fame and not disrepute. It is not form and not formless. It is without number and without counting, without world and without separation from the world, without affliction and without purification, without going and without turning back. It is not examined and not analyzed, unmoved, not thought, unstirred, uncompounded, unelaborated, and free of conceptual elaboration.

That is to say, the sign of form is not apprehended. The signs of feeling, perception, formations, and consciousness are not apprehended. The signs of the eye, ear, nose, tongue, body, and mind are not apprehended. The signs of forms, sounds, smells, tastes, tangibles, and mental phenomena are not apprehended. The signs of eye consciousness, and of ear, nose, tongue, body, and mind consciousness, are not apprehended. The signs of eye contact, and of ear, nose, tongue, body, and mind contact, are not apprehended. The sign of feeling arising from eye contact is not apprehended, and so on up to the sign of feeling arising from mind contact, which is not apprehended. The sign of the perception of form is not apprehended, and so on up to the sign of the perception of mental phenomena, which is not apprehended. The sign of intention toward form is not apprehended, and so on up to the sign of intention toward mental phenomena, which is not apprehended. The sign of attention is not apprehended. The signs of the earth, water, fire, wind, space, and consciousness elements are not apprehended. The signs of the desire, form, and formless realms are not apprehended. The signs of the conditioned and the unconditioned are not apprehended.

Thus, whatever phenomenon is expressed, and whatever intrinsic nature it is expressed by, that phenomenon is not expressed by that nature which expresses it. Since the qualities of a buddha are delightful in this way, when childish ordinary people who lack learning and understanding hear of them, they become afraid, terrified, and frightened. Having become afraid, they fall away from the wisdom of omniscience. They are to be mourned by the world with its gods, for they are a stream of suffering in saṃsāra.

Thinking in this way, the gods generated aspiration for the qualities of a buddha, and for the sake of those qualities they strove to make offerings to the Blessed One. They made offerings to the Blessed One surpassing the offerings of the Heaven of the Thirty-Three, bowed their heads at the Blessed One's feet, circumambulated the Blessed One three times, and sat down to one side. Having sat down to one side, those Yāma gods praised the Blessed One in verse:

"Victor, you see the aggregates as void,
And likewise the elements and the sense sources.
The town of the senses, void of signs,
All of it, O Sage, you see just as it is.

Knower of the world, there are two truths,
Which you did not hear from others but saw yourself:
They are conventional truth and the ultimate.
There is no third truth whatsoever.

That through which beings come to give rise
To faith in the Sugata for the sake of happiness,
That convention the Victor taught
For the sake of beings, to benefit the world.

The six destinies of the hosts of beings that were taught,
Hell-beings, animals, hungry ghosts,
The asura class, humans, and gods,
The Lion among Humans designated as convention.

Low castes and likewise the high castes,
Wealthy households and poor households,
Hosts of slaves and likewise hosts of servants,
Hosts of women, men, and eunuchs,

Whatever distinctions there may be among beings,
Which you, the Incomparable, have taught the world,
The world's benefactor, expert in conventional truth,
Understood and taught to humankind.

For beings in saṃsāra who delight in that,
The eight worldly concerns do not arise:
Gain and loss, fame and disrepute,
Praise and blame, pleasure and pain.

When gain is found, attachment to it arises;
When it is not gained, that too brings agitation.
The others, not stated, should be known in this way.
By these eight diseases one's being is harmed.

Those who proclaim that convention to be ultimate
Should be known as having mistaken minds.
Those who call the unattractive attractive, suffering happiness,
And speak of the selfless as being by nature a self,

Who declare impermanent things permanent,
And, delighting in that, abide in signs,
When they hear the speech of the Sugata,
Become afraid, do not understand it as it is, and reject it.

Having rejected the speech of the Sugata,
Those beings experience unbearable suffering in the hells.
Although they seek happiness in improper ways,
The childish experience the eight sufferings.

Whoever, with an unmistaken mind,
Fully understands the teaching of the world's benefactor
Casts it off completely, like a snake its old skin,
Passes beyond all existence, and attains peace.

All these phenomena are void by nature;
Empty and signless: this is the ultimate.
Those who, hearing this, give rise to joy
Will attain unsurpassed awakening.

In the Dharma as taught by the Sugata
The gods have no doubt at all.
In order to liberate all beings
They seek unsurpassed awakening.

Supreme Being, we pray you, give rise to joy
In those with minds of awakening, those with pure minds.
Hearing that, in order to benefit all beings,
They will attain unsurpassed awakening."

Then, at that time, the Blessed One, understanding the superior intention of the four hundred million gods of the Yāma class, smiled.

Then the venerable Aśvajit addressed the Blessed One in verse:

"Benefactor of the world, the smile that you have smiled
Has caused your assembly to fall into doubt.
Supreme among humans, please declare the cause of that smile;
It will make this assembly rejoice greatly.
If they hear that prophecy about the gods,
This assembly will be exceedingly joyful and pleased,
And to this very teaching, endowed with reason,
They will happily apply themselves with diligence.
Victor, this assembly of yours is endowed with excellence.
Great sage, Buddha, vessel of good qualities,
In order to gather these beings,
Please utter well your pleasing speech.
If they hear of the qualities and powers of the Buddha,
It will be of great benefit to all creatures.
If they hear that prophecy made by the Sugata,
The wise will apply themselves properly.
Here, may the best of humans, supreme among speakers,
Dispel well the hesitation of doubt.
The whole assembly sits, delighting to hear;
Please declare the prophecy about the gods.
All, respectfully lending their ears in service
To the joyous prophecy that you will give,
Have fully cast off obscurations and listen to it.
For the benefit of the world, the supreme person speaks."

Then the Blessed One replied once more to the venerable Aśvajit in verse:

"The wise one smiles for the benefit of the world.
Aśvajit, your question is timely.
If they hear of the power of the Buddha's qualities,
It will be for the benefit of great multitudes of people.
Those whose understanding of the Buddha's many qualities is weak,
Those with minds of attachment and minds of aversion,
Those whose minds have been contentious for a hundred lifetimes,
And those with deluded minds will not come to feel joy.
Those who delight in what the Victors have taught,
Those who in the past questioned the Victors,
And those who are endowed with great compassion,
All of them will come to possess the qualities of a buddha.
Those lions among humans who have seen beings seized by grahas
And have given rise to compassion,
Having heard these qualities of the Buddha,
Will carry them on their heads like a garland of utpala flowers.
This assembly of mine that will rejoice so greatly
In the past served the Victors of former times.
These delightful qualities of the Buddha
They regard correctly, like a garland of flowers.
The Yāma gods served all the Victors of the past
And abandoned attachment.
They censure the afflictions and will possess a buddha's understanding,
Compassionate, with an understanding free of afflictions.
In the past they served all the Victors, lions among humans,
As many as the sands of the Ganges.
Aspiring to unsurpassed awakening,
They all have performed these boundless virtues.
Knowing sentient beings to be sunk in the mire of afflictions,
They give rise to compassion for those who suffer.
Of the supreme humans, the benefactors of beings,
They have fully inquired into boundless modes of the Dharma.
Having heard these, they will become guides,
And will teach the way to enter the guides' Dharma.
Knowing well the afflicted conduct of humans,
They will teach them the vast Dharma as well.
Those guides, most excellent of holy beings,
Will also teach emptiness, the absence of intrinsic nature.
Having heard that speech of the Victors,
They fully realize all beings to be empty.
If one asks, empty of what? Empty of the aspect of signs.
They proclaim the absence of intrinsic nature and the absence of signs.
Having understood the Victors' teachings in this way,
All of them abide in the qualities of the buddhas.
They have made vast offerings to me.
Since I have prophesied in accordance with the Dharma,
In the excellent eon called Equal to the Hosts of Stars
They will attain unsurpassed awakening.
That eon will be completely filled with peerless sugatas,
Twenty koṭis upon twenty.
They will be known by the name Pure Intellect,
And they will fully purify inconceivable sentient beings.
The intentions of the gods who dwell in the Yāma heaven
Were fully inquired into by Aśvajit;
At the prophecy of the Victor, the great sage who has conquered the foe,
The world together with its gods rejoices."

This concludes the sixteenth chapter, the prophecy to the Yāma gods.

Then eighty koṭis of gods of the Tuṣita realm, having seen the offerings of all those beings from the asuras up to the gods of the Yāma realm, and having heard the prophecies as well, thought: "What is that dharma which the Blessed One has prophesied to unsurpassed, perfect awakening? Is it form? Or is it feeling? Or perception? Or formations? Or is it consciousness that the Blessed One has prophesied to unsurpassed, perfect awakening?" Having wondered this, they thought: "The Blessed One has not prophesied form to unsurpassed, perfect awakening. Nor has the Blessed One prophesied feeling, perception, formations, or consciousness to unsurpassed, perfect awakening."

Why is that? Since form is unborn and awakening too is unborn, how could the unborn fully and perfectly awaken to the unborn? Since feeling, perception, formations, and consciousness are also unborn, and awakening too is unborn, how could the unborn there fully and perfectly awaken to the unborn?

Form is without cessation, and awakening too is without cessation. Everything up to consciousness is without cessation, and awakening too is without cessation. Form is free of conceptual elaboration, and awakening too is free of conceptual elaboration. Everything up to consciousness is free of conceptual elaboration, and awakening too is free of conceptual elaboration. Form is nondual, and awakening too is nondual. Everything up to consciousness is nondual, and awakening too is nondual. Form is unmoving, and awakening too is unmoving. Everything up to consciousness is unmoving, and awakening too is unmoving. Form cannot be observed, and awakening too cannot be observed. Everything up to consciousness cannot be observed, and awakening too cannot be observed. That being so, how could what is without cessation, free of elaboration, nondual, unmoving, and unobservable fully and perfectly awaken to what is without cessation and so on, up to what is unobservable?

When, in this way, no phenomena are observed, what here is a buddha? What is a bodhisattva? What is awakening? What is a prophecy? Form is empty of form. Feeling, perception, formations, and consciousness are empty of consciousness. A buddha is empty of the essential nature of a buddha. A bodhisattva is empty of the essential nature of a bodhisattva. Awakening is empty of the essential nature of awakening. A prophecy is empty of the essential nature of a prophecy. Since all phenomena are thus empty by their intrinsic nature, "form" and so on up to "consciousness," and "buddha" and so on up to "prophecy," are mere conventions, mere names, mere signs, mere relative truths, mere expressions, mere designations. The wise should not cling to them.

It is like this. Suppose a man, asleep and dreaming, is enjoying himself, endowed with the five qualities of sense pleasure, and then wakes from sleep. He no longer sees those sense pleasures, and even when he recalls them he cannot find them, and because he cannot find them he suffers. In the same way, those who have truly entered the bodhisattva vehicle, when they seek awakening, will not meet with awakening; not finding awakening and not tasting its flavor, they suffer. Why is that? Because all phenomena are deceptive, like a dream. The meaning of those phenomena is one way, and ordinary beings cling to them in another way.
