Having outwardly imagined a woman to be a woman, he becomes attached and wishes to unite with the external woman. Because they wish to unite, they unite. Then there comes a time when, through the condition of their union, the embryo arises.

Great King, both what is imagined and what imagines do not exist: in a woman there is no woman, and in a man there is no man. Thus, though they do not exist, false imagination arises, and that imagination too is without essential nature. Just as with imagination, so the union and the embryo are also without essential nature. How could what has no essential nature produce hardness?

Great King, having thus understood imagination, one should understand hardness itself, so as to know that when hardness arises it does not come from anywhere.

Great King, there comes a time when this body ends up in the charnel ground. When its hardness is destroyed and ceases, it does not go to the east, nor does it go to the south, the west, the north, above, below, or the intermediate directions. Great King, this is how the internal earth element should be viewed.

Great King, there comes a time when, where this world had stood, there is only space, and the mansion of Brahmā, made of the seven kinds of precious substance, stands there. Great King, when its hardness arises, it does not come from anywhere. And when the mansions of the Vaśavartin gods, made of the seven kinds of precious substance, come into being, Great King, their hardness too, when it arises, does not come from anywhere.

Though the Cakravāla and the Great Cakravāla ranges stand, mountains made of vajra, hard, firm and massive, their hardness, when it arises, does not come from anywhere. Though there stand the Sumerus, kings of mountains, the Yugaṃdharas, the Nimiṃdharas, the Īṣādharas, the Vajra Holders, the Khadirakas, the Vinatakas, the Aśvakarṇas, the Sudarśanas, the Mahāsudarśanas, the Bare Peaks, the Gandhamādanas, the snow mountains, and the other black mountains; though there stands the trichiliocosm, the great thousandfold world system, complete in every respect; and though there stands the great earth, eighty-four thousand yojanas deep and sixty thousand yojanas wide, Great King, that hardness, when it arises, does not come from anywhere.

Great King, when this world is destroyed, there comes a time when this great earth is burned by fire, or dissolved by water, or scattered by wind. When it is burned by fire, not even smoke appears, and no remnant of ash appears. It is just as when butter or sesame oil is burned by fire in open space: not even smoke appears, and no remnant of ash appears. In the same way, when this trichiliocosm, the great thousandfold world system, is burned by fire, not even smoke appears, and no remnant of ash appears.

When it is dissolved by water, no remnant whatever appears. It is just as when salt dissolves in water, not the slightest remnant appears. In the same way, when this trichiliocosm, the great thousandfold world system, is dissolved by water, not the slightest remnant appears.

When it is scattered by wind, not the slightest remnant appears. It is just as when a little bird is overtaken by a scattering gale, not the slightest trace of it appears. In the same way, when this trichiliocosm, the great thousandfold world system, is scattered by wind, not the slightest remnant appears.

Great King, thus the arising of the earth element is empty, and the destruction and arising of the earth element are empty of essential nature. Great King, apart from mere convention, the earth element is not apprehended as the earth element itself, and that convention too is neither female nor male. Great King, understand this thus, as it truly is, with wisdom.

Great King, the water element is of two kinds: internal and external. What is the internal water element? Whatever within this body, inside it, is water, watery, wateriness, moisture, moist, moistness, or wetness, belongs to the body and is appropriated. What is that? It is tears, sweat, saliva, nasal mucus, lymph, fat, bile, phlegm, pus, blood, milk, and urine. Whatever else within this body, inside it, is water, watery, wateriness, moisture, moist, moistness, or wetness, belongs to the body and is appropriated, is called the internal water element.

What is the external water element? It is whatever is external water, watery, the nature of water, moisture, moist, the nature of moisture, wetness, that does not belong to the body and is not appropriated. This is called the external water element.

Great King, when the internal water element arises, it does not come from anywhere. Great King, tears arise on seeing someone dear; tears arise when one is afflicted by suffering, or through the force of the Dharma; and tears arise when one is struck by wind, or in other ways too. Even on examination, there is a time when the internal water element increases, yet, Great King, when that water element arises, it does not come from anywhere.

Great King, there is a time when the internal water element dries up completely, and when it dries up and ceases, it does not go anywhere. Great King, there is a time when the internal fire element becomes predominant and scorches the internal water element, and when it is scorched, it does not go anywhere. Great King, in the same way, when the internal water element arises it does not come from anywhere, and when it ceases it does not go anywhere.

Great King, there is a time when this world comes into being. When this world system comes into being, thirty-two masses of very dense cloud gather throughout this sky, and by their gathering this entire great trichiliocosm, complete in every part, is covered over. Then for five intermediate eons a stream of rain falls, each stream as thick as a plough-pole. For five intermediate eons rain falls in streams as thick as an elephant's member. For five intermediate eons rain falls without interruption. For five intermediate eons rain falls in great drops. By this, everything from this great earth up to the world of Brahmā is filled and pervaded with water. Yet, Great King, even when that great mass of water arises, it does not come from anywhere.

Great King, there is a time when this world is destroyed. When this world system is destroyed, two suns appear in the world, and because two suns have appeared in the world, the springs and streams dry up. Then three suns appear in the world, and because three suns have appeared in the world, the great springs and the great rivers dry up. Then four suns appear in the world, and because four suns have appeared in the world, even the great lake Anavatapta, from which these four great rivers flow, dries up completely, sinks away, and vanishes. Then five suns appear in the world, and because five suns have appeared in the world, the water of the great ocean is depleted and diminished by as much as one yojana. It is depleted and diminished by as much as two yojanas, three, four, five, ten, twenty, thirty, forty, and fifty yojanas. The water of the great ocean is depleted and diminished by as much as a hundred yojanas, two hundred, three hundred, four hundred, five hundred yojanas, a thousand yojanas, two thousand, three thousand, and four thousand yojanas.

Great King, there is a time when forty thousand yojanas of water remain in the great ocean; when thirty thousand yojanas, twenty thousand, nine thousand, eight thousand, seven thousand, six thousand, five thousand, four thousand, three thousand, two thousand, and one thousand yojanas of water remain; when nine hundred yojanas of water remain; when eight hundred yojanas, seven hundred, six hundred, five hundred, four hundred, three hundred, two hundred, and one hundred yojanas of water remain; when fifty yojanas, forty, thirty, twenty, ten, nine, eight, seven, six, five, four, three, two, and one yojana of water remain; when water to the height of ten palm trees remains, nine, eight, seven, six, five, four, three, and as much as two palm trees; when water to the height of ten men standing remains, nine, eight, seven, six, five, four, three, and as much as two men standing; when water remains only deep enough to reach the neck, only deep enough to reach the armpits, only deep enough to reach the navel, only deep enough to reach the knees; down to the time when there remains only as much water as fills a cow's hoofprint.

Great King, there is a time when nothing but scattered patches of moisture remain of the great ocean. It is like this: when great drops of rain have fallen, at first there are only scattered patches of moisture on the great earth. Great King, in the same way there is a time when nothing but scattered patches of moisture remain of the great ocean. Great King, there is a time when there does not remain in the great ocean even enough water to wet the tip of a finger.

Great King, even when the water element ceases to that extent, it does not go anywhere: it does not go to the east, the south, the west, the north, above, below, or the intermediate directions. Great King, that water element is empty in its arising and empty in its destruction, and even while it abides, that water element is empty of intrinsic nature. Great King, apart from mere convention, that water element is empty of the water element and is not apprehended, and that convention too is neither female nor male. Understand it in this way, truly as it is, with correct wisdom.

Great King, the fire element is of two kinds: internal and external. What is the internal fire element? It is whatever there is in this body that is fire, fiery, warmth, or warm, that belongs to the body and is appropriated.

And what is that? It is that by which this body is heated, thoroughly heated and completely heated; that by which what is eaten, drunk, chewed and tasted is well and comfortably digested; that which, when it predominates, comes to be reckoned as "fever, fever"; and, besides these, whatever in this body, internally and within, is fire or fiery, warmth or warm, belonging to the body and appropriated. This is called the internal fire element.

What is the external fire element? It is whatever is external fire or fiery, warmth or warm, not belonging to the body and not appropriated. It is this: people take a fire-drill, or grass, or powdered cow dung, or balls of cotton, and produce fire. Once it has arisen, it burns a village and the area around a village, a town or the area around a town, a country or a region of a country, an island, a heap of grass, a forest, trees, grass or cow dung, and spreads. This, and whatever else is of a similar kind, is called the external fire element. Great King, when the external fire element arises, it does not come from anywhere, and when it ceases, it does not go anywhere to be gathered. Because it is devoid of intrinsic nature, it comes into existence from nonexistence, and having come into existence, it becomes nonexistent again.

Great King, the wind element is also of two kinds: internal and external. What is the internal wind element? It is whatever in this body is wind or windlike, lightness and mobility, belonging to the body and appropriated. And what is that? Namely: the upward-moving wind, the downward-moving wind, the wind moving across, the wind moving toward the back, the wind dwelling in the back, the wind dwelling in the belly, the cutting winds, the splitting winds, stabbing pains, trembling, nasal sounds, wind tumors, the winds of exhalation and inhalation, and the winds dwelling in the major and minor limbs; and, besides these, whatever in this body, internally and within, is wind or windlike, lightness and mobility, belonging to the body and appropriated. This is called the internal wind element.

What is the external wind element? It is whatever is external wind or windlike, lightness and mobility, not belonging to the body and not appropriated. As for this external wind, there is the east wind; there are the south, west and north winds. There are dusty winds and dustless winds, small winds and great winds. There are also all-pervading winds, scattering winds and whirlwinds. Great King, when a great mass of wind arises, it breaks trees, and it topples the tops of walls and the peaks of mountains, and having toppled them leaves no trace of them. And there are times when beings stir up a breeze with the hem of a garment, or with bellows, or with fans. This, and whatever else is of a similar kind, is called the external wind element. Great King, when the internal and external wind elements arise, they do not come from anywhere, and when they cease, they do not go anywhere to be gathered. Because they are devoid of intrinsic nature, they come into existence from nonexistence, and having come into existence, they become nonexistent again.

Great King, the space element is also of two kinds: internal and external. What is the internal space element? It is whatever in this body, internally and within, is present in the body and appropriated, and is reckoned as space, spacelike and "internal": what is unfilled and is not to be filled by skin, flesh and blood. And what is that? In this body it is what is called the inside of the eye, or the inside of the ears and nose, or the mouth, or the inside of the mouth, or the opening of the mouth, or the throat, or the gullet; that place where one swallows and where things remain, and that by which what is eaten, drunk, chewed and tasted passes downward. And, besides these, whatever in this body, internally and within, is present in the body and appropriated: the space and spacelike quality of what has become the aggregates, the elements or the sense sources, what is unfilled and not to be filled by skin, flesh and blood, reckoned as internal, the hollows and what are called the orifices. That is the internal space element.

What is the external space element? It is whatever is external space or spacelike, unfilled, not to be filled by anything that has become form, unmixed, hollow, become an opening. This is called the external space element.

Great King, there comes a time when, conditioned by karma, the sense sources arise. They come to obscure the space element, and it comes to be reckoned as what is called the internal space element, yet it has not come from anywhere. Great King, there comes a time when form is destroyed and everything becomes space. Why is that? Because the space element is inexhaustible. It should be understood that this is what is called the internal space element: abiding and unmoving.

Great King, just as the unconditioned is the realm of nirvāṇa, so too the space element should be seen as pervading everything.

Great King, suppose a man were to dig a well, or a pool, or a long well, or a tank on level ground. Great King, what do you think of that? From where did its space come?

He replied, "Blessed One, it did not come from anywhere."

"Great King, suppose that man were to fill them in again, from the well to the tank. Great King, what do you think of that? Where did that space go?"

He replied, "Blessed One, it did not go anywhere. And why is that? Because the space element is not established as going or coming, and is not established as the state of being female or the state of being male."

The Blessed One declared, "Great King, thus the external space element is unmoving and unchanging."

Why is that? Because the space element is empty of the space element, and the space element is devoid of the space element. The space element does not abide as the state of a woman, nor does it abide as the state of a man. Understand it in this way, as it really is, with correct wisdom.

Great king, what is the consciousness element? The eye faculty is the dominant condition, and form is the object. It is the cognizing of each form: cognizing blue, or cognizing yellow, red, or multicolored; or cognizing long, short, thick, or thin. Great king, in this way the cognizing of any color or shape whatever is called the eye-consciousness element.

Likewise, from "the ear faculty is the dominant condition" up to "the mind faculty is the dominant condition, and phenomena are the object": the cognizing of sound, the cognizing of smell, the cognizing of taste, the cognizing of touch, or the cognizing of phenomena, these are the elements from the ear-consciousness element up to the mind-consciousness element. Thus the six faculties are the dominant conditions and the six sense-objects are the objects, and this cognizing of objects is called the consciousness element.

Great king, that consciousness element does not rest on the faculties. It does not come from the objects, and it does not abide in the middle between them. It is not inside, it is not outside, and it is not in what is neither. That consciousness element ceases once it has cognized each thing. When it arises it does not come from anywhere, and when it ceases it does not go anywhere. The arising of that consciousness element is empty, and its perishing too is empty. Even though that consciousness element arises, it is empty of intrinsic nature.

Great king, in this way, apart from mere convention, that consciousness element is empty of the consciousness element itself, and that convention too is neither male nor female. Understand it in this way, as it really is, with correct wisdom.

Great king, what is the eye sense base? It is the clear matter of the four great elements, namely the earth element, the water element, the fire element, and the wind element. Among these, the clear matter of the earth element is not the eye sense base. Nor is the clear matter of the water element, nor the clear matter of the fire element, nor is the clear matter of the wind element the eye sense base. Why is that? The clear matter of the earth element does not abide as a sense base for any phenomenon, nor as something through which a sense base is to be attained. Likewise, the clear matter of the elements up to the wind element does not abide as a sense base for any phenomenon, nor as something through which a sense base is to be attained. Why is that? Those phenomena are without volition, without agency, and capable of nothing. Great king, when phenomena are searched for one by one, apart from mere convention, no eye sense base is found.

Why is that? The clear matter of the earth element is empty of the clear matter of the earth element, and so on up to the clear matter of the wind element, which is empty of the clear matter of the wind element. How could phenomena that are empty of intrinsic nature become either clear or turbid? And how could those in which neither clearness nor turbidity is found look at forms? Thus the eye sense base is utterly empty of the intrinsic nature of the eye sense base. Because it has no coming and no going, it is not found at the prior extreme, and it is not found at the later extreme. Because it has no intrinsic nature, its abiding is not found either. What has no intrinsic nature is neither female nor male. How could what is neither female nor male give rise to conceit? Great king, conceit is the domain of Māra. Freedom from conceit is the domain of the buddhas. Why is that? All phenomena are free of conceit.

Great king, when one understands as it really is, the eye sense base is empty of the eye sense base. Why is that? This eye sense base has no sign; therefore this sign of the eye sense base is empty of the eye sense base, and this is emptiness. The eye sense base is free of signs, and this is signlessness. It makes no aspiration, and so this is wishlessness. Great king, in this way the eye sense base is the three doors of liberation; the eye sense base is directed toward liberation.

Great king, what then are the sense bases from the ear sense base up to the body sense base? They are the clear matter of the four great elements. Great king, in this way everything, up to all phenomena, is directed toward liberation, certain in the dharmadhātu, reaching to the limits of the space element, impossible to designate, beyond convention, inexpressible, and not to be spoken of.

Great king, that on which the faculties impinge is called the objects. Because the eye impinges on forms, forms are therefore called the objects of the eye. Likewise the ear impinges on sounds, the nose on smells, the tongue on tastes, the body on tangibles, and the mind on phenomena; therefore phenomena are called the objects of the mind.

Here, the statement "the eye impinges on forms" teaches impingement by way of alighting. That is, the eye alights on forms in three ways: it alights on agreeable forms with the perception of attractiveness, it alights on disagreeable forms with the perception of anger, and it alights on those that are neither agreeable nor disagreeable with the perception of equanimity.

Likewise, the statement "the mind impinges on phenomena" teaches impingement by way of alighting. That is, the mind alights on phenomena in three ways: it alights on agreeable ones with attachment, it alights on disagreeable ones with anger, and it alights on those that are neither agreeable nor disagreeable with delusion. Those objects on which it alights are called the domain of the mind; because the mind moves and ranges among them, they are therefore called the domain of the mind.

"Great King, when the mind engages with agreeable forms with attachment, desire arises in it. When it engages with disagreeable forms with anger, aversion arises in it. When it engages with forms that are neither agreeable nor disagreeable with bewilderment, delusion arises in it. In the same way, with sounds, smells, tastes, and tangible objects too, one comes to experience these three kinds of phenomena as objects: one becomes attached, angered, or bewildered. When the mind engages with agreeable phenomena, the objects of mental cognition, with attachment, desire arises in it. When it engages with disagreeable phenomena with anger, aversion arises in it. When it engages with phenomena that are neither agreeable nor disagreeable with bewilderment, delusion arises in it.

"Great King, the sense faculties should be understood to be like an illusion, and their objects like a dream. Great King, suppose, for example, that a man asleep dreams that he is enjoying himself with the most beautiful woman in the land, and that when he wakes from sleep he remembers that woman, the most beautiful in the land. Great King, what do you think of that? Does the most beautiful woman in the land whom he saw in the dream exist?"

He replied, "Blessed One, she does not."

The Blessed One said, "Great King, what do you think of that? Would a man who dreams of the most beautiful woman in the land, and who becomes fixated on playing and delighting with that woman, be of a wise disposition?"

He replied, "Blessed One, he would not. Why is that? Blessed One, the most beautiful woman in the land seen in the dream does not exist at all and cannot be found, so how could there be any enjoying oneself with her? Otherwise, the man who is fixated on her would be one whose lot is only want and weariness."

The Blessed One said, "Great King, in the same way, when ignorant, unlearned ordinary beings see forms with the eye, they become fixated on those that make the mind happy. Being fixated, they become attached. Having become attached, they become thoroughly attached. Having become thoroughly attached, they perform actions born of desire: three kinds with the body, four kinds with speech, and three kinds with the mind. Yet the actions so performed are, from the very first, exhausted, ceased, departed, and transformed. They do not abide resting in the east. They do not abide resting in the south, the west, the north, below, above, or the intermediate directions. They are not here, not over there, and not in between.

"At another time, when the time and moment of death draw near, the life faculty ceases, the lifespan is exhausted, and the actions of the same kind as that life are exhausted too. When the final consciousness ceases, the mind takes those actions as its object, just as, for example, one remembers the most beautiful woman in the land on waking from sleep. Great King, then, through two conditions, the final consciousness as the governing condition and those actions as the object, the first consciousness, belonging to rebirth, arises. It arises either among the beings of hell, or in the womb of an animal, or in the world of Yama, or in the realm of the asuras, or among humans, or among the gods. Immediately after that first consciousness belonging to rebirth ceases, without interruption, there arises a mental continuum of the same kind, in which the karmic maturation is evidently experienced.

"Here, the ceasing of the final consciousness is counted as what is called death. The arising of the first consciousness is called birth. Great King, thus no phenomenon at all passes from this world to the next world, and yet death and birth do appear. Great King, when that final consciousness arises, it comes from nowhere, and when it ceases, it goes nowhere. When those actions arise, they come from nowhere, and when they cease, they go nowhere. When that first consciousness arises, it comes from nowhere, and when it ceases, it goes nowhere. Why is that? Because they have no essential nature. The final consciousness is empty of the final consciousness. Action is empty of action. The first consciousness is empty of the first consciousness. Death is empty of death. Birth is empty of birth. And yet actions do not become fruitless, and the experience of karmic maturation does appear. Yet apart from mere names and conventions, there is no agent and no experiencer at all.

"Great King, suppose, for example, that a man asleep dreams that he is fighting with an enemy, and that when he wakes from sleep he remembers just that. Great King, what do you think of that? Does that enemy he dreamed of in the dream, or the fight with that enemy, exist?"

He replied, "Blessed One, it does not."

The Blessed One said, "Great King, what do you think of this? Would a man who is fixated on the enemy in the dream, or who fights with that enemy, be of a wise disposition?"

He replied, "Blessed One, he would not. Why is that? Blessed One, even the enemy seen in the dream does not exist at all and cannot be found, so how could there be any fighting with him? Otherwise, the man who is fixated on him would be one whose lot is only want and weariness."

The Blessed One said, "Great King, in the same way, the immature ordinary person who lacks learning also becomes fixated on the forms that make him unhappy when he sees them with his eye. Having become fixated, he becomes angry. Having become angry, he becomes hostile. Having become hostile, he performs actions born of aversion: three kinds with the body, four kinds with speech, and three kinds with the mind. Yet the action so performed is, from the very first, exhausted, ceased, gone, and transformed. It does not abide in the east, nor does it abide in the south, the west, the north, below, above, or the intermediate directions. It is not here, it is not there, and it is not in between. Then, at another time, when the time of death draws near, the life faculty ceases, the lifespan is wholly exhausted, and the actions that share its lot are also exhausted. When the final consciousness ceases, that action appears to the mind as an object, just as the enemy fought in a dream appears to one who has woken from sleep.

"Great King, then, through two conditions, the force of the final consciousness and that action which has become its object, the first consciousness belonging to the birth arises, and it is counted as 'being born.' Immediately after that first consciousness ceases, there arises without interruption a mental continuum of similar kind in which the karmic maturation is evidently experienced. Here, the cessation of the final consciousness is counted as 'death,' and the arising of the first consciousness is called 'birth.' Thus, Great King, no phenomenon whatsoever passes from this world to the world beyond, and yet death and birth are evident.

"Great King, when that final consciousness arises, it does not come from anywhere, and when it ceases, it does not go anywhere. That action, that birth, and that first consciousness belonging to the birth likewise do not come from anywhere when they arise, and do not go anywhere when they cease. Why is that? Because they lack any essential nature. The final consciousness is empty of all consciousness; the first consciousness is empty of the first consciousness; action is empty of action; death is empty of death; and birth is empty of birth. Yet actions do not go without results, and the experience of karmic maturation is evident. Even so, apart from the conventions of names, there is no agent and no one who experiences."

The thirteenth section.

"Great King, suppose, for example, that a man, while asleep, dreams that he is seized by a demon, and he becomes utterly bewildered with fear. Then, on waking from sleep, he remembers that demon and his utter bewilderment. Great King, what do you think? Do the demon seen in the dream and that utter bewilderment exist?"

The king replied, "Blessed One, they do not."

The Blessed One said, "Great King, what do you think? In that case, is the man who is fixated on the demon seen in the dream and utterly bewildered by it someone of wise disposition?"

He replied, "Blessed One, he is not. And why? Blessed One, since the dream demon does not exist at all, how could there be utter bewilderment? Otherwise, the man who is fixated on it will become one whose lot is only distress and weariness."

The Blessed One said, "Great King, in the same way, the immature ordinary person who lacks learning also becomes fixated on the forms toward which he feels equanimity when he sees them with his eye. Through that fixation he becomes utterly bewildered. Having become utterly bewildered, he becomes thoroughly afflicted. Having become thoroughly afflicted, he performs actions born of delusion: three kinds with the body, four kinds with speech, and three kinds with the mind. Yet the action so performed is, from the very first, exhausted, ceased, gone, and transformed. It does not abide in the east, nor does it abide in the south, the west, the north, below, above, or the intermediate directions. It is not here, it is not there, and it is not in between. Then, at another time, when the time of death draws near, the life faculty ceases, the lifespan is wholly exhausted, and the actions that share its lot are also exhausted. When the final consciousness ceases, that action appears to the mind as an object, just as the demon and the utter bewilderment appear to one who has woken from sleep.

"Great King, then, through two conditions, the force of the final consciousness and the action that has become its object, the first consciousness belonging to the birth arises. Immediately after that first consciousness ceases, there arises without interruption a mental continuum of similar kind in which the karmic maturation is evidently experienced. Here, the cessation of the final consciousness is counted as 'death,' and the arising of the first consciousness is called 'birth.' Thus, Great King, no phenomenon whatsoever passes from this world to the world beyond, and yet death and birth are evident.

"Great King, when that final consciousness arises, it does not come from anywhere, and when it ceases, it does not go anywhere. That action also does not come from anywhere when it arises, and does not go anywhere when it ceases. That first consciousness also does not come from anywhere when it arises, and does not go anywhere when it ceases. Why is that? Because they lack any essential nature. The final consciousness is empty of the final consciousness; action is empty of action; the first consciousness is empty of the first consciousness; death is empty of death; and birth is empty of birth. Yet it is evident that actions do not go without results, and the experience of karmic maturation is also evident. Even so, apart from mere conventions of names, there is no agent and no one who experiences."

"Great King, it is like this. Suppose, for example, that a man asleep dreams that he hears the sweet song of the fairest woman in the land as she sings, and the sweet sound of her playing the lute, and he dreams that he enjoys that song and music. Then, waking from sleep, he remembers that very song and music. Great King, what do you think of that? Do the fairest woman in the land, the song, and the sound of music seen in the dream exist?"

The king replied, "Blessed One, they do not."

The Blessed One said, "Great King, then would the man who clings to the fairest woman in the land, the song, and the sound of music seen in the dream be of a wise disposition?"

The king replied, "Blessed One, he would not. Why is that? The fairest woman in the land seen in the dream does not exist at all and cannot be found. How then could the sound of her song and music exist? Were it otherwise, the man who clings to them would be one whose lot is frustration and weariness."

The Blessed One said, "Great King, in the same way, the childish, ordinary person without learning, hearing sounds with the ear, clings to those that are pleasing. Clinging to them, he becomes attached. Having become attached, he becomes utterly enamored. Having become utterly enamored, he performs karma born of desire: three kinds with the body, four kinds with speech, and three kinds with the mind. And that karma, once performed, is from the very outset exhausted, ceased, departed, and transformed. It does not abide resting in the east. It does not abide resting in the south, the west, the north, below, above, or the intermediate directions. It is not here, not beyond, and not in between. Yet at another time, when the hour of death draws near, the life faculty ceases, the lifespan is fully exhausted, and the karmas that share its lot are exhausted too. Then, as the last consciousness ceases, that karma appears to the mind as its object, just as the song and music of the fairest woman in the land appear to one who has woken from sleep.

"Great King, then, through two conditions, the dominance of the last consciousness and that karma as its object, the first consciousness belonging to rebirth arises. Immediately upon the cessation of that first consciousness belonging to rebirth, there arises a mind-stream of corresponding kind, in which the experiencing of maturation becomes evident.

"Here, the ceasing of the last consciousness is reckoned as what is called death and transference, and the arising of the first consciousness is called birth. Great King, thus no phenomenon whatsoever transfers from this world to the world beyond, and yet death and transference and birth are evident. Great King, when the last consciousness arises, it comes from nowhere, and when it ceases, it goes nowhere. When that karma arises, it comes from nowhere, and when it ceases, it goes nowhere. When the first consciousness arises, it comes from nowhere, and when it ceases, it goes nowhere. Why is that? Because they are empty of intrinsic nature. The last consciousness is empty of the last consciousness. Karma is empty of karma. The first consciousness is empty of the first consciousness. Death and transference are empty of death and transference. Birth is empty of birth. And yet karmas do not go without result, and the experiencing of their maturation is evident too. Yet apart from mere conventional designation, there is no agent and no experiencer whatsoever.

"Great King, it is like this. Suppose, for example, that a man asleep dreams that he is separated from his dead mother, or his dead father, or any loved one whatever, and that, separated from his loved one, he grieves and weeps in distress. Then, waking from sleep, he remembers that separation from his loved one and his weeping in suffering. Great King, what do you think of that? Does that separation from the loved one in the dream exist?"

The king replied, "Blessed One, it does not."

He said, "Great King, then would the person who clings to the separation from the loved one in the dream and to the weeping in suffering be of a wise disposition?"

The king replied, "Blessed One, he would not. Why is that? Blessed One, since even the loved one seen in the dream does not exist at all, how could separation from them, grieving, weeping, and lamenting exist? Were it otherwise, the man who clings to the separation from the loved one, the suffering, and the weeping seen in the dream would be one whose lot is frustration and weariness."

The Blessed One said, "Great King, in the same way, the childish, ordinary person without learning, hearing sounds with the ear, clings also to those that are displeasing. Clinging to them, he becomes angry. Having become angry, he becomes hostile. Having become hostile, he performs karma born of aversion: three kinds with the body, four kinds with speech, and three kinds with the mind. And that karma, once performed, is from the very outset exhausted, ceased, departed, and transformed. It does not abide resting in the east. It does not abide resting in the south, the west, the north, below, above, or the intermediate directions. It is not here, not beyond, and not in between. Yet at another time, when the hour of death draws near, the life faculty ceases, the lifespan is fully exhausted, and the karmas that share its lot are exhausted too. Then, as the last consciousness ceases, that karma appears to the mind as its object, just as the separation from the loved one and the weeping in suffering appear to one who has woken from sleep.

Great King, then, by the power of the last consciousness and by that karma as its object, these two conditions, the first consciousness belonging to the portion of birth arises. Immediately after that first consciousness has ceased, without interruption, there arises a mental continuum of similar type, in which the experiencing of the ripening becomes manifest. The ceasing of the last consciousness is reckoned as what is called "death-transition," and the arising of the first consciousness is reckoned as what is called "birth." Thus, Great King, no phenomenon whatsoever transfers from this world to the world beyond, and yet death-transition and birth do manifestly occur.

Great King, the last consciousness, when it arises, comes from nowhere, and when it ceases, goes nowhere. Karma, ripening, and the first consciousness likewise, when they arise, come from nowhere, and when they cease, go nowhere. Why is that? Because they are empty of intrinsic nature. The last consciousness is empty of the intrinsic nature of a last consciousness. Karma, ripening, and the first consciousness are empty of the intrinsic nature of a first consciousness. Death-transition is empty of the intrinsic nature of death-transition. Birth is empty of the intrinsic nature of birth. Saṃsāra too is empty of the intrinsic nature of saṃsāra.

Great King, this emptiness of all phenomena in this way is the gateway to liberation that is emptiness. That which, being emptiness, is empty of signs is the gateway to liberation that is signlessness. Where there are no signs, there is no wishing; this is the gateway to liberation that is wishlessness. Thus, Great King, all phenomena are gateways to liberation: they incline toward nirvāṇa, converge in emptiness, are settled in the realm of phenomena (dharmadhātu), and reach to the very limit of the element of space.

Great King, suppose, for example, that a man asleep hears in a dream a sound whose words he does not understand, and that on waking from sleep he remembers that sound. Great King, what do you think? Does that sound heard in the dream exist?

The king replied, "No, Blessed One, it does not."

The Blessed One said, "Great King, what do you think? In that case, would one who clings to having heard in a dream a sound whose meaning he did not understand be of a wise disposition?"

The king replied, "No, Blessed One, he would not. And why? Blessed One, if the very sound heard in the dream does not exist at all and cannot be found, how could there be any understanding or not understanding the meaning of its words? Otherwise, the man who clings to it would become one whose lot is only deprivation and weariness."

The Blessed One said, "Likewise, Great King, ignorant ordinary beings who lack learning, when they hear sounds with the ear, cling even to those sounds toward which they are indifferent, and having clung, they become utterly confused. Being utterly confused, they perform karma born of delusion: three kinds by body, four kinds by speech, and three kinds by mind. And that karma, once performed, is from the very outset exhausted, ceased, gone, and changed. It does not abide located in the east; it does not abide located in the south, the west, the north, below, above, or the intermediate directions. It is not here, not beyond, and not in between. Rather, at another time, when the time of death draws near, the life faculty ceases, the lifespan is wholly exhausted, and the karmas of corresponding type are also exhausted; then, as the last consciousness ceases, that karma becomes an object of the mind, just like the sound whose words one did not understand, recalled on waking from sleep.

Great King, then, by the power of the last consciousness and by that karma as its object, these two conditions, the first consciousness belonging to the portion of birth arises. Immediately after that first consciousness has ceased, without interruption, there arises a mental continuum of similar type, in which the experiencing of the ripening becomes manifest. The ceasing of the last consciousness is reckoned as what is called "death-transition," and the arising of the first consciousness is reckoned as what is called "birth." Thus, Great King, no phenomenon whatsoever transfers from this world to the world beyond, and yet death-transition and birth do manifestly occur.

Great King, the last consciousness, when it arises, comes from nowhere, and when it ceases, goes nowhere. Karma, ripening, and the first consciousness likewise, when they arise, come from nowhere, and when they cease, go nowhere. Why is that? Because they lack essential nature. The last consciousness is empty of the intrinsic nature of a last consciousness. Karma is empty of the intrinsic nature of karma. Ripening is empty of the intrinsic nature of ripening. The first consciousness is empty of the intrinsic nature of a first consciousness. Death-transition and birth are empty of the intrinsic nature of death-transition and birth. Saṃsāra is empty of the intrinsic nature of saṃsāra. And yet karmas are not wasted, and the experiencing of their ripening does manifestly occur. Yet in this, apart from mere conventional designations by name, there is no agent or experiencer whatsoever.

Great King, suppose, for example, that a man asleep dreams that he anoints his body with sandalwood, or tamāla leaves, or any other kind of fragrance, and that on waking from sleep he remembers that sandalwood, or those tamāla leaves, or whatever other kind of fragrance it was. Great King, what do you think? Do the sandalwood, the tamāla leaves, or whatever other kind of fragrance he saw in the dream exist?"

The king replied, "No, Blessed One, they do not."

The Blessed One said, "Great King, what do you think? In that case, would one who clings to the sandalwood, the tamāla leaves, or whatever kind of fragrance he saw in the dream, and to having anointed his body with them, be of a wise disposition?"

The king replied, "No, Blessed One, he would not. And why? Blessed One, if the dream sandalwood, tamāla leaves, or whatever kind of fragrance it was do not exist at all and cannot be found, how could there be any anointing with them? Otherwise, the man who clings to it would become one whose lot is only deprivation and weariness."

The Blessed One said, "Great King, in the same way, when childish, unlearned ordinary people smell odors with the nose, they become fixated on those that please the mind. Being fixated, they become attached. Having become attached, they become thoroughly infatuated. Having become thoroughly infatuated, they perform karma born of desire: three kinds with the body, four kinds with speech, and three kinds with the mind. Yet the karma so performed is, from the very first, exhausted, ceased, gone, and transformed. It does not abide resting in the east. It does not abide resting in the south, the west, the north, below, above, or the intermediate directions. It is not here, it is not beyond, and it is not anywhere in between.

"At another time, when the hour of death is near and its moment has arrived, the life faculty ceases, the lifespan is wholly exhausted, and the karma that sustained it is also exhausted. Then, when the last consciousness ceases, that karma appears to the mind as an object, just as sandalwood, or tamāla leaves, or any other kind of fragrance appears to one who has awakened from sleep. Great King, there, through two conditions, the last consciousness as the dominant condition and that karma as the object, the first consciousness belonging to the side of birth arises. Immediately after that first consciousness ceases, there arises without interruption a continuum of mind of similar kind, in which the experience of karmic maturation becomes manifest.

"In this, the cessation of the last consciousness is reckoned as what is called death, and the arising of the first consciousness is reckoned as what is called birth. Thus, Great King, there is no phenomenon whatsoever that passes from this world to the world beyond, and yet death and birth do appear. Great King, when the last consciousness arises, it comes from nowhere, and when it ceases, it goes nowhere. Karma, karmic maturation, and the first consciousness, too, when they arise, come from nowhere, and when they cease, go nowhere. Why is that? Because they have no essential nature. The last consciousness is empty of the intrinsic nature of a last consciousness. Karma is empty of the intrinsic nature of karma. Karmic maturation is empty of the intrinsic nature of karmic maturation. The first consciousness is empty of the intrinsic nature of a first consciousness. Death and birth are empty of the intrinsic nature of death and birth. Saṃsāra is empty of the intrinsic nature of saṃsāra. And yet karma is not lost, and the experience of karmic maturation does appear. In this there is no agent and no experiencer whatsoever, apart from mere names and conventions.

"Great King, suppose, for example, that a man asleep dreamed that the corpse of a dog, or the corpse of a human, or the corpse of a snake had been hung around his neck, and that when he awoke from sleep he remembered it. Great King, what do you think of that? Do those corpses of a dog, a human, and a snake in the dream exist?"

The king replied, "Blessed One, they do not."

The Blessed One said, "Great King, then would a man who is fixated on the corpses of a dog, a human, and a snake hung around his neck in a dream be of a wise disposition?"

The king replied, "Blessed One, he would not. Why is that? Blessed One, since the very corpses of a dog, a human, or a snake seen in the dream do not exist at all and cannot be apprehended, how could there be their being hung around his neck? Rather, the man who is fixated on that would only reap weariness and exhaustion."

The Blessed One said, "Great King, in the same way, when childish, unlearned ordinary people smell odors with the nose, they become fixated on those that are unpleasing to the mind as well. Being fixated, they become angry. Having become angry, they become hostile. Having become hostile, they perform karma born of aversion: three kinds with the body, four kinds with speech, and three kinds with the mind. Yet the karma so performed is, from the very first, exhausted, ceased, gone, and transformed. It does not abide resting in the east. It does not abide resting in the south, the west, the north, below, above, or the intermediate directions. It is not here, it is not beyond, and it is not anywhere in between.

"At another time, when the hour of death is near and its moment has arrived, the life faculty ceases, the lifespan is wholly exhausted, and the karma that sustained it is also exhausted. Then, when the last consciousness ceases, that karma appears to the mind as an object, just as the corpses of a dog, a human, and a snake appear to one who has awakened from sleep. Great King, there, through two conditions, the last consciousness as the dominant condition and that karma as the object, the first consciousness belonging to the side of birth arises. Immediately after that first consciousness ceases, there arises without interruption a continuum of mind of similar kind, in which the experience of karmic maturation becomes manifest.

"In this, the cessation of the last consciousness is reckoned as what is called death, and the arising of the first consciousness is reckoned as what is called birth. Thus, Great King, there is no phenomenon whatsoever that passes from this world to the world beyond, and yet death and birth do appear. Great King, in this, when the last consciousness arises, it comes from nowhere, and when it ceases, it goes nowhere. Karma, karmic maturation, and the first consciousness, too, when they arise, come from nowhere, and when they cease, go nowhere. Why is that? Because they are empty of intrinsic nature: from the last consciousness being empty of the intrinsic nature of a last consciousness, up to saṃsāra being empty of the intrinsic nature of saṃsāra. That is emptiness, the door of liberation. What is empty of intrinsic nature and nonexistent is signlessness. What is signless is wishlessness. Great King, thus all phenomena are doors of liberation: inclining toward nirvāṇa, converging in emptiness, certain within the realm of phenomena (dharmadhātu), and extending to the limits of the realm of space."

"Great king, suppose, for example, that a man asleep dreams that his own sense of smell has been damaged. On waking from sleep he remembers that very damage to his sense of smell. Great king, what do you think of that? Does the damaged sense of smell he saw in the dream exist?"

The king replied, "No, Blessed One, it does not."

The Blessed One said, "Great king, what do you think of that? In that case, would someone who clings to the damaged sense of smell in the dream be wise by nature?"

The king replied, "No, Blessed One, he would not. Why is that? Blessed One, the sense of smell seen in the dream does not exist at all and cannot be found, so how could its damage exist? Otherwise, a man who clings to it would only be destined for want and weariness."

The Blessed One said, "Great king, in the same way the unlearned, foolish ordinary person also clings to those smells that produce equanimity when the nose senses them. Having clung to them, he becomes utterly confused. Being utterly confused, he forms karma born of delusion: three kinds by body, four kinds by speech, and three kinds by mind. And the karma he has formed is exhausted, ceases, departs, and changes. It does not abide resting in the east, and so on up to: it is not here, it is not there, it is not in between.

"Then at another time, when the time and moment of death draw near, the life faculty ceases, the lifespan is wholly exhausted, and the karma that corresponds to it is also exhausted. As the final consciousness ceases, the mind takes that karma as its object, just as a man who has woken from sleep recalls the damaged sense of smell.

"Great king, from two conditions, the final consciousness as the dominant condition and that karma as the object, there arises the first consciousness belonging to rebirth. Immediately after that first consciousness ceases, without any interval, there arises the corresponding stream of mind in which the experience of the karmic ripening becomes manifest.

"Here, the ceasing of the final consciousness is reckoned as death, and the arising of the first consciousness is reckoned as birth. Thus, great king, no phenomenon whatsoever passes from this world to the next world, and yet death and birth do appear.

"Great king, when that final consciousness arises, it does not come from anywhere, and when it ceases, it does not go anywhere. Karma, karmic ripening, and the first consciousness likewise do not come from anywhere when they arise, and do not go anywhere when they cease. Why is that? Because they lack essential nature. The final consciousness is empty of final consciousness, and so on up to: saṃsāra is empty of the intrinsic nature of saṃsāra. Karma is not wasted, and the experience of karmic ripening does appear. Yet apart from mere conventional names, there is no agent and no experiencer whatsoever.

"Great king, suppose, for example, that a man asleep is hungry and dreams that he eats his fill of food with a hundred flavors. On waking from sleep he remembers that very thing. Great king, what do you think of that? Does the food of a hundred flavors he saw in the dream exist?"

The king replied, "No, Blessed One, it does not."

The Blessed One said, "Great king, what do you think of that? In that case, would someone who clings to the food of a hundred flavors seen in the dream be wise by nature?"

The king replied, "No, Blessed One, he would not. Why is that? Blessed One, the dream food of a hundred flavors does not exist at all and cannot be found, so how could he possibly enjoy it? Otherwise, a man who clings to it would only be destined for want and weariness."

The Blessed One said, "Great king, in the same way the unlearned, foolish ordinary person also clings to those flavors that produce mental pleasure when the tongue tastes them. Clinging to them, he becomes attached. Having become attached, he becomes thoroughly attached. Having become thoroughly attached, he forms karma born of desire: three kinds by body, four kinds by speech, and three kinds by mind. And the karma he has formed is, from the very first, exhausted, ceases, departs, and changes. It does not abide resting in the east, and so on up to: it is not here, it is not there, it is not in between.

"Then at another time, when the time and moment of death draw near, the life faculty ceases, the lifespan is wholly exhausted, and the karma that corresponds to it is also exhausted. As the final consciousness ceases, the mind takes that karma as its object, just as a man who has woken from sleep recalls the food of a hundred flavors.

"Great king, from two conditions, the final consciousness as the dominant condition and that karma as the object, there arises the first consciousness belonging to rebirth. Immediately after that first consciousness ceases, without any interval, there arises the corresponding stream of mind in which the experience of the karmic ripening becomes manifest.

"Here, the ceasing of the final consciousness is reckoned as death, and the arising of the first consciousness is reckoned as birth. Thus, great king, no phenomenon whatsoever passes from this world to the next world, and yet death and birth do appear.

"Great king, when that final consciousness arises, it does not come from anywhere, and when it ceases, it does not go anywhere. Karma, karmic ripening, and the first consciousness likewise do not come from anywhere when they arise, and do not go anywhere when they cease.

Why is that? Because they are empty of essential nature. The last consciousness is empty of the intrinsic nature of a last consciousness, and so on, up to: saṃsāra is empty of the intrinsic nature of saṃsāra. Yet actions are not wasted, and the experience of their ripening is evident as well. There, apart from mere conventional designation, there is no agent at all, and no one who partakes of the result.

Great King, it is like this. Suppose a man, asleep, dreams that he has become extremely hungry and has eaten the seed of a bitter gourd, or the seed of a kośātakī, or a neem seed, and on waking from sleep he remembers that very thing. Great King, what do you think of that? Do the bitter gourd and the other seeds, up to the neem seed, of the dream exist?

The king replied, "Blessed One, they do not."

The Blessed One said, "Great King, then is the person who clings to having eaten the bitter gourd and the other seeds, up to the neem seed, of the dream someone of a wise nature?"

The king replied, "Blessed One, he is not. Why is that? Blessed One, if the bitter gourd and the other seeds, up to the neem seed, seen in the dream do not exist at all and cannot be apprehended, how could there be any eating of them? Otherwise, the man who clings to it would merely become one whose lot is privation and weariness."

The Blessed One said, "Great King, in the same way, when the tongue of a childish ordinary person who has not heard the teaching experiences tastes, he clings even to those that are disagreeable. Having clung, he becomes angry. Having become angry, he feels aversion. Having felt aversion, he performs actions born of aversion: three kinds by body, four kinds by speech, and three kinds by mind. And the action so performed is, from the very first, exhausted, ceased, gone apart, and transformed. It does not abide resting in the eastern direction, and so on, up to: it is not here, it is not beyond, it is not in between. At another time, when the time of death and the term of life draw near, the life faculty ceases, the lifespan is wholly exhausted, and the actions of corresponding share are also exhausted. Then, at the moment the last consciousness ceases, the action comes to appear as an object to the mind, just as the bitter gourd and the other seeds, up to the neem seed, eaten in sleep appear to one who has woken.

"Great King, there, through two conditions, the dominance of the last consciousness and that action as its object, the first consciousness belonging to the portion of birth arises. Immediately upon the cessation of that first consciousness, without interruption, there arises a mental continuum of corresponding kind, in which the experience of ripening becomes evident. There, the ceasing of the last consciousness is reckoned as what is called death and transmigration; the arising of the first consciousness is reckoned as what is called birth. Thus, Great King, no phenomenon whatsoever passes from this world to the world beyond, and yet death, transmigration, and birth are evident.

"Great King, there, when the last consciousness arises, it does not come from anywhere, and when it ceases, it does not go anywhere. Action, ripening, and the first consciousness, when they arise, do not come from anywhere, and when they cease, they do not go anywhere. Why is that? Because they are empty of intrinsic nature. The last consciousness is empty of the intrinsic nature of a last consciousness, and so on, up to: saṃsāra is empty of the intrinsic nature of saṃsāra.

"Great King, thus this emptiness of all phenomena is the door of liberation that is emptiness. That which is empty of the sign of emptiness is the door of liberation that is signlessness. Where there is no sign, there is no wish; this is the door of liberation that is wishlessness. Great King, thus all phenomena are doors of liberation: inclining toward nirvāṇa, converging in emptiness, certain in the dharmadhātu, reaching to the limits of the element of space.

"Great King, it is like this. Suppose a man, asleep, dreams that his own tongue faculty has been impaired, and on waking from sleep he remembers that very impairment of the tongue faculty. Great King, what do you think of that? Does the impairment of the tongue faculty seen in the dream exist?"

The king replied, "Blessed One, it does not."

The Blessed One said, "Great King, what do you think of that? Then is someone who clings to the impairment of the tongue faculty in the dream someone of a wise nature?"

The king replied, "Blessed One, he is not. Why is that? Blessed One, if even the tongue faculty seen in the dream does not exist at all and cannot be apprehended, how could there be any impairment of it? Otherwise, the man who clings to it would merely become one whose lot is privation and weariness."

The Blessed One said, "Great King, in the same way, when the tongue of a childish ordinary person who has not heard the teaching experiences tastes, he clings even to those that are neutral. Having clung, he becomes utterly bewildered. Having become utterly bewildered, he performs actions born of delusion: three kinds by body, four kinds by speech, and three kinds by mind. And the action so performed is, from the very first, exhausted, ceased, gone apart, and transformed. It does not abide resting in the eastern direction, and so on, up to: it is not here, it is not beyond, it is not in between. At another time, when the time of death and the term of life draw near, the life faculty ceases, the lifespan is wholly exhausted, and the actions of corresponding share are also exhausted. Then, at the moment the last consciousness ceases, the action comes to appear as an object to the mind, just as the impairment of the tongue faculty in sleep appears to one who has woken.

Great King, from these two conditions, the dominant influence of the final consciousness and that karma which is its object, the first consciousness, the one that belongs to the portion of birth, arises. Immediately after that first consciousness ceases, without interruption, a mind-stream of similar kind arises, in which the experience of the karmic maturation becomes manifest. The ceasing of the final consciousness is reckoned as "death and transmigration," and the arising of the first consciousness is reckoned as "birth." Great King, in this way no phenomenon whatsoever passes from this world to the world beyond, and yet death, transmigration and birth do appear. Great King, from the final consciousness up to the first consciousness, when they arise they do not come from anywhere, and when they cease they do not go anywhere. Why is that? Because they are empty of intrinsic nature. The final consciousness is empty of final consciousness, and so on up to: saṃsāra is empty of the intrinsic nature of saṃsāra. Actions are not wasted, and the experience of their maturation does appear, yet apart from mere conventional designation there is no agent and no experiencer at all.

Great King, suppose, for example, that a man asleep dreams that he holds the most beautiful woman in the land in his embrace. Having experienced her touch, he wakes from sleep and remembers that very beautiful woman. Great King, what do you think of that? Does that touch of the most beautiful woman of the land, seen in the dream, exist?

The king replied, "Blessed One, it does not."

The Blessed One said, "Great King, what do you think of this? Would that man who clings to the touch of the most beautiful woman of the land seen in the dream be one whose nature is wise?"

The king replied, "Blessed One, he would not. And why? Blessed One, since that very beautiful woman of the dream does not exist at all and cannot be found, how could there be any touch of her?"

The Blessed One said, "Great King, in the same way, the childish, uninstructed ordinary person, when his body meets tangible objects, clings to those that are pleasing to the mind. Clinging to them, he becomes attached. Having become attached, he becomes thoroughly attached. Having become thoroughly attached, he performs actions born of desire: three kinds with the body, four with speech, and three with the mind. And those actions he has performed are, from the very beginning, exhausted, ceased, departed and changed. They do not rest and abide in the eastern direction, and so on up to: they are not here, not over there, and not in between. Then, at another time, when the time and moment of death draw near, the life faculty ceases, the lifespan is entirely exhausted, and the actions of similar kind are also exhausted. When the final consciousness is ceasing, the mind takes those actions as its object, just as a man who has woken from sleep recalls the touch of the most beautiful woman of the land.

"Great King, from these two conditions, the dominant influence of the final consciousness and that karma which is its object, the first consciousness, the one that belongs to the portion of birth, arises. Immediately after that first consciousness belonging to the portion of birth ceases, without interruption, a mind-stream of similar kind arises, in which the experience of the karmic maturation becomes manifest. The ceasing of the final consciousness is reckoned as 'death and transmigration,' and the arising of the first consciousness is reckoned as 'birth.' Great King, in this way no phenomenon whatsoever passes from this world to the world beyond, and yet death, transmigration and birth do appear. Great King, from the final consciousness up to the first consciousness, when they arise they do not come from anywhere, and when they cease they do not go anywhere. Why is that? Because they are empty of intrinsic nature. Actions are not wasted, and the experience of their maturation does appear, yet apart from mere conventional designation there is no agent and no experiencer at all.

"Great King, suppose, for example, that a man asleep dreams that he holds a blazing sheet of copper in his embrace, and that, waking from sleep, he remembers it. Great King, what do you think of that? Is there any touch of the blazing copper sheet of the dream?"

The king replied, "Blessed One, there is not."

The Blessed One said, "Great King, then would the person who clings to embracing the blazing copper sheet of the dream be one whose nature is wise?"

The king replied, "Blessed One, he would not. And why? Blessed One, since the blazing copper sheet seen in the dream does not exist at all and cannot be found, how could there be any touch of it? Otherwise, the man who clings to it would be destined for distress and weariness."

The Blessed One said, "Great King, in the same way, the childish, uninstructed ordinary person, when his body meets tangible objects, also clings to those that are unpleasing to the mind. Clinging to them, he becomes angry. Having become angry, he becomes hostile. Having become hostile, he performs actions born of aversion: three kinds with the body, four with speech, and three with the mind. And those actions he has performed are, from the very beginning, exhausted, ceased, departed and changed. They do not rest and abide in the eastern direction, and so on up to: they are not here, not over there, and not in between. Then, at another time, when the time and moment of death draw near, the life faculty ceases, the lifespan is entirely exhausted, and the actions of similar kind are also exhausted. When the final consciousness is ceasing, the mind takes those actions as its object, just as a man who has woken from sleep recalls the blazing sheet of copper."

"Great King, then, by two conditions, the force of the final consciousness and that action taken as its object, the first consciousness belonging to the portion of birth arises. Immediately after that first consciousness has ceased, there arises without interruption a mental continuum of similar kind, in which the karmic maturation comes to be manifestly experienced.

Here, the ceasing of the final consciousness is reckoned as what is called 'death-transference.' The arising of the first consciousness is reckoned as what is called 'birth.' Great King, in this way no phenomenon whatsoever transfers from this world to the world beyond, and yet death-transference and birth do appear. From the final consciousness to the first consciousness, when it arises it comes from nowhere at all, and when it ceases it goes nowhere at all. Why is that? Because it is empty of intrinsic nature. The final consciousness is empty of the intrinsic nature of the final consciousness, and so on up to: saṃsāra too is empty of the intrinsic nature of saṃsāra.

Great King, in this way this emptiness of all phenomena is the door of liberation that is emptiness. That which is empty of the sign of emptiness is the door of liberation that is signlessness. Where there is no sign, there is no wishing; this is the door of liberation that is wishlessness. Great King, in this way all phenomena are doors of liberation: they incline toward nirvāṇa, they converge completely in emptiness, they are certain in the realm of phenomena (dharmadhātu), and they reach to the limit of the element of space.

Great King, suppose, for example, a man, sleeping, dreamed that the faculty of his own body had been impaired, and on waking from sleep he remembered it. Great King, what do you think? Does that impaired bodily faculty seen in the dream exist?"

He replied, "Blessed One, it does not."

The Blessed One said, "Great King, what do you think? Then is one who clings to the impaired bodily faculty in the dream of a wise nature?"

He replied, "No, Blessed One, he is not. Why is that? Blessed One, since the very bodily faculty seen in the dream does not exist at all and is not apprehended, how could its impairment exist?"

The Blessed One said, "Great King, in the same way, the childish ordinary person who has not heard the teaching, when touching tangible objects with the body, clings even to those that give rise to equanimity, and having clung, becomes utterly bewildered. Being utterly bewildered, he forms actions born of delusion: three with the body, four with speech, and three with the mind. And those actions he has formed are, from the very beginning, exhausted, ceased, departed and changed. They do not rest and abide in the eastern direction, and so on up to: they are not here, not over there, and not in between. Then, at another time, when the time and moment of death draw near, the life faculty ceases, the lifespan is entirely exhausted, and the actions of similar kind are also exhausted. When the final consciousness is ceasing, the mind takes those actions as its object, just as a man who has woken from sleep recalls the impaired bodily faculty.

Great King, then, by two conditions, the force of the final consciousness and that action taken as its object, the first consciousness belonging to the portion of birth arises. Immediately after that first consciousness has ceased, there arises without interruption a mental continuum of similar kind, in which the karmic maturation comes to be manifestly experienced.

Here, the ceasing of the final consciousness is reckoned as what is called 'death-transference.' The arising of the first consciousness is reckoned as what is called 'birth.' Great King, in this way no phenomenon whatsoever transfers from this world to the world beyond, and yet death-transference and birth do appear. Great King, from that final consciousness to the first consciousness, when it arises it comes from nowhere at all, and when it ceases it goes nowhere at all. Why is that? Because it is empty of essential nature. The final consciousness is empty of the final consciousness, and so on up to: saṃsāra is empty of the intrinsic nature of saṃsāra. Yet actions are not wasted, and the experiencing of their maturation also appears. But in this there is no agent or experiencer whatsoever, apart from mere conventional designations of names.

The fourteenth fascicle.

Great King, suppose, for example, a man, sleeping, dreamed that he was endowed with the five qualities of sensual pleasure conjured by a master magician, and that he enjoyed them to complete satisfaction, and on waking from sleep he remembered those qualities of sensual pleasure. Great King, what do you think? Do those five qualities of sensual pleasure seen in the dream, conjured by the master magician, exist?"

He replied, "Blessed One, they do not."

The Blessed One said, "Great King, what do you think? Then is one who clings to those five qualities of sensual pleasure seen in the dream, conjured by the master magician, of a wise nature?"

He replied, "No, Blessed One, he is not. Why is that? Blessed One, since the very magician of the dream does not exist at all and is not apprehended, how could those five conjured qualities of sensual pleasure and the enjoyment of them exist? On the contrary, the man who clings to them becomes one whose lot is want and weariness."

The Blessed One said, "Great King, in the same way, the childish ordinary person who has not heard the teaching, when cognizing mental objects with the mind, clings to those that give rise to mental pleasure. Through that clinging he becomes attached. Having become attached, he becomes thoroughly attached. Having become thoroughly attached, he forms actions born of desire: three with the body, four with speech, and three with the mind. And those actions he has formed are, from the very beginning, exhausted, ceased, departed and changed. They do not rest and abide in the eastern direction, and so on up to: they are not here, not over there, and not in between. Then, at another time, when the time and moment of death draw near, the life faculty ceases, the lifespan is entirely exhausted, and the actions of similar kind are also exhausted. When the final consciousness is ceasing, the mind takes those actions as its object, just as a man who has woken from sleep recalls the five qualities of sensual pleasure conjured by the magician.

"Great King, then, by two conditions, the power of the final consciousness and the karma that has become its object, the first consciousness, which belongs to the portion of birth, arises. It arises either among the hell beings, or in the animal realm, or in the world of Yama, or among the classes of the asuras, or among humans, or among the gods. Immediately after that first consciousness has ceased, without interruption, there arises a mental continuum of similar type, in which the ripening is manifestly experienced.

"Great King, in this way karma is observed and its ripening is also observed, yet apart from a mere conventional designation by name there is no one at all who experiences it. Great King, the cessation of the final consciousness is reckoned as what is called 'death and transmigration.' The arising of the first consciousness is reckoned as what is called 'birth.' Great King, in this way no phenomenon whatsoever passes over from this world to the world beyond, and yet death, transmigration and birth do manifestly occur.

"Great King, from that final consciousness up to the first consciousness, when it arises it does not come from anywhere, and when it ceases it does not go anywhere. Why is that? Because it is empty of essential nature. The final consciousness is empty of final consciousness, and so on up to saṃsāra, which is empty of the intrinsic nature of saṃsāra. Nirvāṇa too is empty of the intrinsic nature of nirvāṇa. All phenomena too are empty of the intrinsic nature of all phenomena. Great King, this emptiness of all phenomena is the door of liberation that is emptiness. What is empty of the sign of emptiness is signlessness. Where there is no sign, there is no wishing, and this is wishlessness. Great King, in this way all phenomena are doors of liberation: they fully converge in emptiness, have signlessness as their essential nature, are free of wishing, incline toward nirvāṇa, are certain within the dharmadhātu, and reach to the limits of the element of space.

"Great King, it is like this. Suppose a man, while asleep, dreamt that he and his attendants were carried away by a great river, and that he experienced there many kinds of suffering and unhappiness arising from the mind. Then, waking from sleep, he remembered it. Great King, what do you think? Does that great river of the dream exist?"

The king replied, "Blessed One, it does not."

The Blessed One said, "Great King, what do you think? In that case, is the person who, in a dream, clings to seeing himself carried off by a great river and to his suffering and unhappiness someone of a wise nature?"

The king replied, "Blessed One, he is not. Why is that? Blessed One, if in the dream the great river itself, whose water carried that man away, does not exist at all and cannot be observed, how could his suffering and unhappiness exist? Otherwise, the man who clings to it would merely be one fated to weariness and exhaustion."

The Blessed One said, "Great King, in the same way, the childish ordinary person who has not heard the teaching, when he cognizes phenomena with the mind, clings even to those things that bring unhappiness. Having clung, he becomes angry. Having become angry, he becomes malicious. Having become malicious, he becomes hateful. Having become hateful, he performs actions born of aversion: three kinds with the body, four kinds with speech, and three kinds with the mind. And the action thus performed is, from the very first, exhausted, ceased, separated and transformed. It does not abide supported in the east, and so on in the other directions; it is not here, not beyond, and not in between.

"At another time, when the time of death draws near and the hour is close, the life faculty ceases, the lifespan is exhausted, and the actions of similar type are also exhausted. Then, when the final consciousness ceases, just as one who wakes from sleep recalls being carried away by the great river and his unhappiness, the mind takes that karma as its object. Great King, then, by two conditions, the power of the final consciousness and the karma that has become its object, the first consciousness, which belongs to the portion of birth, arises. Immediately after that first consciousness has ceased, without interruption, there arises a mental continuum of similar type, in which the ripening is manifestly experienced.

"Great King, in this way karma is observed, yet there is no agent at all. The ripening is observed, yet apart from a mere conventional designation by name there is no one at all here who experiences it. Great King, the cessation of the final consciousness is reckoned as what is called 'death and transmigration.' The arising of the first consciousness is reckoned as what is called 'birth.' Great King, in this way no phenomenon whatsoever passes over from this world to the world beyond, and yet death, transmigration and birth do manifestly occur.

"Great King, from that final consciousness up to the first consciousness, when it arises it does not come from anywhere, and when it ceases it does not go anywhere. Why is that? Because it is empty of essential nature. The final consciousness is empty of the intrinsic nature of final consciousness, and so on up to saṃsāra, which is empty of the intrinsic nature of saṃsāra. Nirvāṇa too is empty of the intrinsic nature of nirvāṇa. All phenomena too are empty of the intrinsic nature of all phenomena. This is the door of liberation that is emptiness. What is empty of the sign of emptiness is the door of liberation that is signlessness. Where there is no sign, there is no wishing, and this is the door of liberation that is wishlessness. Great King, in this way all phenomena are doors of liberation: they fully converge in emptiness, have signlessness as their essential nature, are free of wishing, incline toward nirvāṇa, are certain within the dharmadhātu, and reach to the limits of the element of space."

Great King, suppose, for example, that a man asleep dreams that he is drunk on liquor and has become utterly deluded.
