In that state he does not know virtue, he does not know non-virtue, he does not know what is blameworthy, he does not know what is blameless, he does not know what helps, he does not know what harms, and he does not know good qualities or faults in any way at all. Then, when he wakes from sleep, he remembers that drunken delusion. Great King, what do you think of that? Does the drunkenness and delusion seen in the dream exist?

The king replied, "Blessed One, it does not."

The Blessed One said, "Great King, what do you think of that? Then is the man who clings to the drunkenness of the dream someone of a wise nature?"

The king replied, "Blessed One, he is not. And why is that? Blessed One, if even the liquor seen in the dream does not exist at all and cannot be found, how much less could there be delusion? Otherwise, the man who clings to it would be one whose lot is deprivation and weariness."

The Blessed One said, "Great King, in the same way, childish ordinary beings who are without learning, when they cognize phenomena with the mind, come to cling even to those phenomena that are neutral. Having clung to them, they become utterly bewildered. Having become utterly bewildered, they perform actions born of delusion: three kinds by body, four kinds by speech, and three kinds by mind. And the action so performed is, from its very first moment, exhausted, ceased, separated, and transformed. It does not abide, resting in the east, and so on, up to: it is not here, it is not in the beyond, and it is not in between.

At another time, when the time of death is near, the life faculty ceases, the lifespan is exhausted, and the actions of like fortune with it are also exhausted. Then, when the last consciousness ceases, the mind apprehends that action, just as someone who has woken from sleep recalls the drunken delusion.

Great King, then, by two conditions, the force of the last consciousness and that apprehended action, the first consciousness arises among the destinies. Immediately after that first consciousness ceases, without interruption, there arises a continuum of mind of like fortune, in which the maturation will be manifestly experienced.

Great King, thus action is apprehended, yet there is no agent whatsoever. Maturation too is apprehended, yet apart from mere conventional designation by name, there is no one at all here who experiences it.

Great King, the ceasing of the last consciousness is what is reckoned as death. The arising of the first consciousness is what is reckoned as birth. Great King, thus there is no phenomenon whatsoever that passes from this world to the next world, and yet death and birth are evident.

Great King, when the last consciousness arises, it comes from nowhere at all, and when it ceases, it goes nowhere at all. Action, maturation, death, birth, and the first consciousness, when they arise, come from nowhere at all, and when they cease, go nowhere at all. Why is that? Because they are empty of essential nature. The last consciousness is empty of the intrinsic nature of the last consciousness. Likewise birth, destruction, action, maturation, the first consciousness, death, birth, and saṃsāra; and nirvāṇa is empty of the intrinsic nature of nirvāṇa. All phenomena too are empty of the intrinsic nature of all phenomena. This is the door of liberation that is emptiness. That which is empty of the sign of emptiness is the door of liberation that is signlessness. Where there is no sign, there is no wish: this is the door of liberation that is wishlessness.

Great King, thus all phenomena are doors of liberation: converging in emptiness, of the essential nature of signlessness, free from aspiration, inclined toward nirvāṇa, determined as the realm of phenomena (dharmadhātu), reaching to the limits of the element of space.

Great King, you should settle your mind firmly on this, and strive at settling your mind firmly. Do not depend on others; you should understand it by yourself. This is the awakening of the buddhas, the blessed ones, of the past, the future, and the present. It utterly destroys all clinging to sovereignty, completely dries up all craving, crushes all pride, and cuts off entirely every kind of view and all doubt. It is the sameness of all phenomena, which is not for childish ordinary beings, is not reached by the śrāvakas, and is not the domain of the pratyekabuddhas. It is the conduct of the bodhisattvas, and is realized by the perfectly awakened buddhas.

Great King, you should set your mind on this, thinking: 'How shall I become the eye of the world with its gods, its lamp, its light, its illumination, its boat, its shore, its landing place, its guide, its leader, its teacher, its caravan leader, and its forerunner?' And thinking: 'Having been freed, I shall free others. Having crossed over, I shall carry others across. Having found relief, I shall bring others relief. Having passed completely into nirvāṇa, I shall bring others completely into nirvāṇa.'

Great King, as for sovereignty, not even the limit of what has been experienced in the past can be discerned. Great King, thus the senses are like an illusion: they never know contentment and cannot be satisfied. Sense objects are like a dream: they are incapable of satisfying and never bring fulfillment.

Great King, long ago, in times past, there lived a king called Anantayaśas. Great King, that king Anantayaśas had palanquins, armies, and mounts without limit, and he ruled with an unobstructed wheel. He possessed an unshakable vehicle and perfect conduct. He had developed roots of virtue under former buddhas and possessed the power of having his wishes fulfilled: whatever he thought of, all of it was accomplished for him, and not one of his intentions was ever in vain.

In this way, by the splendor of his own roots of virtue, whatever that king intended came about just as he wished, and nothing was ever in vain.

Great King, then King Anantayaśas thought, "I should test whether the power of my merit has diminished." He thought, "Ah! May every tree there is in my world system of four great continents bear fruit just as I wish!" The very instant King Anantayaśas formed this thought, every tree there was in the world system of four great continents bore fruit just as he wished.

Great King, furthermore, King Anantayaśas wished once more to test the power of his roots of virtue, and he thought, "Ah! May all the sentient beings dwelling in this world system of my four great continents be free of every harm, and may the thoughts in their minds be fruitful!" The very instant King Anantayaśas formed this thought, all the sentient beings, however many dwelt in the world system of four great continents, were free of every harm, and all the thoughts in their minds became fruitful.

Great King, furthermore, King Anantayaśas wished to test the power of his roots of virtue, and he thought, "Ah! May a rain of scented water pour down upon this world system of my four great continents!" Great King, the very instant King Anantayaśas formed this thought, a rain of scented water poured down over the whole world system of four great continents.

Great King, then again King Anantayaśas wished to test the power of his roots of virtue more extensively, and he thought, "Ah! May a rain of flowers pour down upon this world system of my four great continents!" Great King, the very instant King Anantayaśas formed this thought, a rain of flowers poured down over the whole world system of four great continents.

Great King, furthermore, King Anantayaśas thought, "Ah! May a rain of divine garments, a rain of wealth, a rain of gold, a rain of various jewels, and a rain of every kind of requisite pour down upon this world system of my four great continents!" Great King, the very instant King Anantayaśas formed this thought, by the cause of vast roots of virtue accumulated in common with all sentient beings, a rain of divine garments, a rain of wealth, a rain of gold, a rain of various jewels, and a rain of every kind of requisite poured down.

Great King, at that time this Jambudvīpa was eighteen thousand yojanas in extent, and at that time there were six hundred million cities in Jambudvīpa. In the middle of Jambudvīpa was the royal capital, the city called Adorned with Jewels, twelve yojanas in length and twelve in width, square, even, spotless, and well laid out.

Great King, that royal capital Adorned with Jewels was completely encircled by seven rows of tāla trees. Those tāla trees were made of four kinds of precious substance: they were all tāla trees of gold, of silver, of beryl, and of crystal. The golden tāla trees had leaves, flowers, and fruit of silver. The silver tāla trees had leaves, flowers, and fruit of gold. The beryl tāla trees had leaves, flowers, and fruit of crystal. The crystal tāla trees had leaves, flowers, and fruit of beryl.

Above the royal capital Adorned with Jewels were stretched seven layers of bright and beautiful nets of bells and tinkling chimes made of the seven precious substances. The seven precious substances of the great jeweled nets were these: gold, silver, beryl, crystal, red pearl, agate, and emerald, these seven.

The royal capital Adorned with Jewels was surrounded on all sides by deep moats filled with water, half a yojana deep and one yojana wide, brimming to the edge with water possessing the eight qualities, so full that a crow could drink from it, filled throughout with utpala, lotus, kumuda, and white lotus flowers, and encircled by seven layers of spread golden sand. All around those moats were set bright and beautiful staircases made of four precious substances, namely gold, silver, beryl, and crystal. On the golden frames were laid silver steps, on the silver frames golden steps, on the beryl frames crystal steps, and on the crystal frames beryl steps. Those staircases, too, were encircled on all sides by seven rows of bright and beautiful railings of the seven precious substances.

Atop each of those jeweled staircases were erected bright and beautiful archways of the seven precious substances. At the head of each staircase were planted bright and beautiful plantain trees of Jambū River gold, and on the waters of each of the moats were bright and beautiful boats of the seven precious substances.

Great King, by the splendor of the merit of King Anantayaśas, eighty thousand bright and beautiful parks came into being all around the royal capital Adorned with Jewels, belonging to no one and claimed by no one. King Anantayaśas gave them for all people to enjoy, and the people went into those parks and played and took their enjoyment there with the supreme and perfect happiness.

In each of those pleasure groves eight ponds were laid out, and every one of those ponds was half a krośa across. Each of those ponds had eight bright and beautiful stairways made of four kinds of jewel, namely gold, silver, beryl and crystal. Above those stairways bright and beautiful archways of the seven precious substances were set up. At the head of each stairway bright and beautiful plantain trees of Jambu River gold were planted. Those ponds were filled to the brim with water of the eight qualities, so full that a crow could drink from them. In those ponds such water-born flowers as these were made to grow: utpala lotuses, padma lotuses, kumuda lotuses, white lotuses, and fragrant ones. All around, such land-born flowers as these were made to grow: atimuktaka, campaka, varṣaka, sumanā flowers, mayuka, dhanuskari, and many other land-born flowers besides.

In the sky above that royal palace city adorned with jewels a great net of bells and tinkling bells was spread out. When the wind stirred the tāla trees of the royal palace city adorned with jewels, those nets of jewels and those nets of bells and tinkling bells, a sound arose that was sweet, delightful and pleasing to hear, like the sound of five-part music and cymbals played well by skilled musicians. At that time all the people who lived in that royal palace city adorned with jewels took delight in that sound and enjoyed it. That royal palace city adorned with jewels was prosperous, happy and blessed with good harvests, filled with a great multitude of people, and filled with utpala lotuses, padma lotuses, kumuda lotuses and white lotuses.

Then King Anantayaśas thought, "Now I shall go to the eastern continent of Pūrvavideha." No sooner had the thought arisen than he rose into the sky together with his fourfold army and went to where the eastern continent of Pūrvavideha lay, and there he ruled as king for many hundreds of years and many thousands of years.

Then King Anantayaśas thought, "Now I shall go to the western continent of Aparagodānīya." No sooner had the thought arisen than he rose into the sky together with his fourfold army and went to where western Aparagodānīya lay, and there he ruled as king for many hundreds of years and many thousands of years.

Then King Anantayaśas thought, "Now I shall go to the northern continent of Uttarakuru." No sooner had the thought arisen than he rose into the sky together with his fourfold army and went to where the great northern continent of Uttarakuru lay, and there, for many hundreds of years and many thousands of years, he directed his own army.

Then, at a time when a long while had passed, King Anantayaśas thought, "If there are gods called the Thirty-Three who dwell on the summit of Sumeru, I shall go to where the gods of the Heaven of the Thirty-Three are." No sooner had the thought arisen than he rose into the sky together with his fourfold army, mounted on the back of the precious elephant, and went up onto Sumeru, the king of mountains.

Then King Anantayaśas said to his driver, "Driver, how do the four great continents, Sumeru the king of mountains, and the great ocean look to you?"

The driver said, "Great King, just as a potter's wheel spins round when it is set going with a stick, so too I see the four great continents, Sumeru the king of mountains, and this great ocean spinning round."

The king said, "Driver, in that case the precious elephant has not yet put forth its stride."

Again King Anantayaśas said to his driver, "Driver, how do the four great continents, Sumeru the king of mountains, and this great ocean look to you now?"

The driver said, "Great King, I now see the four great continents, Sumeru the king of mountains, and the great oceans moving, moving strongly, and moving strongly all over."

The king said, "Driver, in that case the precious elephant has still not put forth its stride."

Again King Anantayaśas said to his driver, "Driver, how do the four great continents, Sumeru the king of mountains, and this great ocean look to you now?"

The driver said, "Great King, now I see the four great continents, Sumeru the king of mountains, and the great oceans as well standing without stirring, moving or shaking."

The king said, "Driver, now that the king of elephants has raised its stride, before long we shall reach the summit of Sumeru, the king of mountains."

Then King Anantayaśas, together with his fourfold army, before long reached the summit of Sumeru, the king of mountains, and Śakra (Indra), lord of the gods, saw King Anantayaśas coming from far away. Seeing him, he moved over to half of his seat and said, "Come here, Great King! Welcome, Great King! Great King, sit on half of the seat!" King Anantayaśas sat on half of the seat of Śakra, lord of the gods, and there, for many hundreds of thousands of years, he exercised sovereign lordship over half the kingdom of the gods of the Heaven of the Thirty-Three.

Then, a long time later, King Anantayaśas thought, "Ah! I should sit upon the whole of Śakra's (Indra's) seat." The very instant he formed that thought, he fell from that half of the seat, together with his fourfold army, and came to be in Jambudvīpa. The people living at the enclosure of the pleasure park of the royal city called Jewel-Adorned saw that King Anantayaśas had come into the jewel pleasure park of the royal city Jewel-Adorned and was staying there. As soon as they saw this, they hurried with all speed to the royal city Jewel-Adorned and told King Nandaka, who was ruling as king there.

When King Nandaka heard that a man of the gods was staying in the Jewel Casket pleasure park together with a fourfold army, he quickly mounted his finest vehicle, came out of the royal city Jewel-Adorned with his fourfold army, and went to where the Jewel Casket pleasure park was. All who lived in the royal city Jewel-Adorned were amazed and came running as well.

Then King Nandaka got down from his vehicle and went to where King Anantayaśas was. He offered perfume, flowers, garlands, ointments and incense, draped his robe over one shoulder, set his right knee on the ground, bowed with joined palms toward King Anantayaśas, and asked King Anantayaśas, "Who are you?"

The king said to him, "Have you heard of one called King Anantayaśas?"

King Nandaka replied, "I have indeed heard of him. The elders say that the universal monarch called King Anantayaśas went to the heavens together with his retinue, army and mounts. That is what I have heard."

He said, "I am that one called King Anantayaśas."

Then King Anantayaśas, whose eyes could not take in the splendor of humans, and who could not bear human enjoyments, began to sink and could not stay. Just as a lump of butter or a lump of fresh butter placed on hot sand melts and does not stay, so King Anantayaśas too sank and could not stay.

Then King Nandaka said this to King Anantayaśas as he was sinking: "If people ask what fine words King Anantayaśas spoke, what shall I declare to the world?"

He said, "Great King, declare this: 'Great King Anantayaśas held sovereign power over the four great continents. His wishes were accomplished just as he intended and never came to nothing. He experienced all trees bearing fruit at will, all harm brought to an end, the thoughts in the minds of all beings fulfilled, a rain of scented water, a rain of divine wish-fulfilling garments, a rain of treasure, a rain of gold, a rain of various jewels, and a rain of every necessity. Dwelling in the four great continents and holding sovereign power over the four great continents, he occupied half of Śakra's seat, yet great desire did not let him go, and with his desire still unsatisfied he died.'" Having said this, King Anantayaśas died.

Great King, if you suspect and doubt that the universal monarch called King Anantayaśas who lived at that time was someone else, do not see it that way. Why is that? Great King, I myself was, at that time, the universal monarch called King Anantayaśas. Therefore, Great King, you should understand that the senses are like an illusion and their objects like a dream. Having understood this, you should settle your mind firmly, and not leave it to depend on others.

Then the Blessed One (the Buddha) spoke these verses:

From every form of lordship over desires
the wise move the mind away;
toward the joy of lordship over the Dharma
they direct the mind in truth.
Giving up lordship over the pleasures of desire,
setting the mind on mastery of the Dharma,
if one relies wholly on taming the mind,
the afflictions are utterly destroyed.
For those who rely on destroying the afflictions,
the path of karma too is cut off.
And when the path of karma is cut off,
one becomes a shrine for the world.
Not afflicted by desires,
fully seeing the faults of desire,
with a mind intent on benefiting beings:
for this he is called a shrine of offering.
When the faults of desire are heard,
the Omniscient One purifies
people's minds of attachment to desires:
for this he is called a shrine of offering.
The abandoning of beings' hatred
the supreme person fully knows;
since he frees the mind from hatred,
for this he is called a shrine of offering.
The abandoning of beings' delusion
the Bull of the Śākyas fully knows;
since he purifies the mind of delusion,
for this he is called a shrine of offering.
The abandoning of beings' pride
the guide of beings knows as well;
since he purifies the mind of pride,
for this he is called a shrine of offering.

The Blessed One further said to King Śuddhodana: "Great King, long ago in time past there lived a king called Bhūdeva, a righteous Dharma king possessed of the seven precious things. His seven precious things were these: the precious wheel, the precious elephant, the precious horse, the precious jewel, the precious woman, the precious householder and the precious minister, seven in all. The father of King Bhūdeva was called King Earth-Arising. The eldest son of King Earth-Arising was Prince Bhūdeva. Then, when King Earth-Arising was dying, Prince Bhūdeva was consecrated into the kingship.

Then King Bhūdeva became an anointed king of the kṣatriya caste. Later, on the poṣadha day of the full moon, King Bhūdeva, the anointed kṣatriya king, washed his hair and put on new cotton cloth. He had his hair and beard trimmed, put on garlands and ornaments, and put on jewels, earrings and bracelets. He was seated on the upper story of his palace, surrounded by a retinue of women arrayed before him. As he sat there, a divine wheel jewel appeared from the east. It had a thousand spokes, a hub and a rim, and it was supreme in every aspect. It was radiant and luminous, seven cubits high, and made entirely of gold.

King Bhūdeva had heard before that an anointed kṣatriya king who, on the poṣadha day of the full moon, is surrounded by a gathering of women arrayed before him, as described above, and to whom there appears from the east a divine wheel jewel with a thousand spokes, made entirely of gold, as described above, becomes a wheel-turning king. So he thought, "Am I perhaps a wheel-turning king? I shall put this to the test." King Bhūdeva rose from his seat, arranged his robe over one shoulder, and set his right knee on the ground. Bowing with joined palms toward the divine wheel jewel, he spoke to it: "O wheel jewel, come down and rest on the ground!" At this the divine wheel jewel came down from the sky and rested on the ground.

Then King Bhūdeva wiped the divine wheel jewel with a clean fine cloth, set it in motion with his right hand, and said: "O wheel jewel, conquer the east!" The wheel jewel then turned toward the east. At that very moment, throughout the lands of the east, there appeared the road of the wheel-turning kings of the past: level, very pleasant, delightful, made of the substance of the seven precious things, and covered with all kinds of scattered flowers. The low-lying places along it rose up, and the ridges sank down. Its wells, springs, lakes, ponds and dried-up rivers filled to the brim with water possessing the eight qualities, namely: cool, sweet, light, soft, clear, free of foul odor, not harming the throat when drunk, and soothing the stomach when drunk. The flowering trees came into blossom, and the fruit trees bore fruit. The blossoming trees blossomed constantly, and the fruit-bearing trees bore fruit constantly.

The wheel-turning king followed behind the wheel jewel together with his fourfold army, and wherever the wheel jewel came to rest, there the wheel-turning king also halted with his fourfold army. Then the kings of the east, the provincial kings, the townspeople and the farmers all brought golden bowls filled with pieces of silver and silver bowls filled with gold dust. They went out to welcome the wheel-turning king and said: "Come, Your Majesty! Welcome, Your Majesty! These lands under Your Majesty's dominion are rich and flourishing, with good harvests, and filled with many people. We pray Your Majesty to rule over these lands and over us."

When they had said this, the wheel-turning king said to them: "I have no need of these riches. Let them remain your own wealth, and live on them. Follow me in this way: if you give up killing, give up taking what is not given, and so on up to giving up wrong view, then I shall consider that I have conquered these lands, and that those people who honor their mothers, honor their fathers, honor śramaṇas, honor brahmins, and serve the elders of their clans are my followers. From now on, do not indulge in unrighteous conduct or improper conduct within the realm. If you do so, I shall consider that I have conquered these lands and that these people are my followers." They answered him, "Excellent!" and diligently did just that.

His divine wheel jewel conquered the east, crossed the eastern ocean, and stood before him like the planted pole of a chariot. In the same way it conquered far and wide in the south and in the west. In the north it conquered as far as the great northern continent of Uttarakuru, crossed the northern ocean, and stood above the gate of the wheel-turning king's palace like the planted pole of a chariot.

As soon as King Bhūdeva had set the wheel jewel turning, at that very moment all four great continents came to be of the nature of jewels, brilliant and beautiful. The seven kinds of jewels are these: gold, silver, beryl, crystal, red pearl, musāragalva, and emerald; these are the seven.

As soon as King Bhūdeva had set the wheel jewel turning, all the hell beings were cut off. All the births as animals, all of Yama's world, all the realms of the asuras, and all the unfavorable states were cut off as well. Even the sounds of nonvirtue vanished entirely. Thus, through King Bhūdeva's former aspiration, sāli rice grew throughout all four great continents without being plowed or sown, and wish-fulfilling trees of every kind appeared. Among those beings, every kind of illness was cut off except three, namely desire, hunger, and old age. The lifespan of those beings was a full ten million years.

Among those beings even the suffering of suffering did not appear at all, and it did them no harm. At that time these and other boundless marvels came about.

Great King, then King Bhūdeva thought, "If there are such enjoyments in the human world, are there or are there not some other enjoyments more vast than these and more exalted than these?" Then he thought, "I have heard it said that there are gods called the gods of the Heaven of the Thirty-Three, who dwell on the summit of Sumeru. What sort of enjoyments do they have?"

Then King Bhūdeva, because he had not abandoned desire, disparaged human enjoyments and longed for divine enjoyments, and he resolved, "In that case, I shall go to the abode of the Thirty-Three." Great King, the very moment Bhūdeva formed that resolve, he went together with his fourfold army to the summit of Sumeru, king of mountains. Then Śakra (Indra), lord of the gods, seeing King Bhūdeva coming from far away, spoke these words: "Great King, come here! Great King, welcome!" He offered him half of his seat and said, "Great King, whenever you see fit, sit upon this seat." And King Bhūdeva sat upon half of Śakra's seat. There, for many hundreds of thousands of years, he held sway over half of the royal sovereignty among the gods of the Heaven of the Thirty-Three.

When a long time had passed, under the power of great desire he came to think, "Ah, I shall hold sway over all of the royal sovereignty among the gods of the Heaven of the Thirty-Three!" The very moment he formed that thought, he fell from half of the seat of Śakra, lord of the gods, and came back to dwell in Jambudvīpa, in the city called Happy Abode. Because he had come from such splendor to dwell in Jambudvīpa, he could not endure the splendor of humans, and he sank and could not remain. Then, as King Bhūdeva was sinking into the earth, he spoke these verses:

"Vast royal sovereignty
Does not quench the craving for desires;
It is like grass and wood to a fire.
Therefore abandon desires completely.

Though one indulges desires in an endless stream,
One never comes to be sated,
Just as one tormented by thirst,
Though drinking deep of salt water, burns all the more.

The rivers never
Fill the ocean to satiety;
Likewise, when one indulges desires,
One is never sated by more.

Though wood and grass are burned in plenty,
The fire is never sated;
Likewise, when one indulges desires,
One never knows satiety in sensual enjoyment.

Just as a mirror is never
Sated with reflections,
Likewise, when one indulges in forms,
One never knows satiety in sensual enjoyment.

Just as an echo is never
Sated with sounds,
Likewise, when one indulges in sounds,
One never knows satiety in sensual enjoyment.

Just as a censer is never
Sated with incense,
Likewise, though one indulges in smells,
The nose never knows satiety in smells.

Just as a ladle is never
Sated with the finest flavors,
Likewise the tongue is never
Sated with the finest flavors.

Just as space is never
Sated with winds,
Likewise the body is never
Sated with tactile objects.

Just as drinking water
In a dream one dreams brings no satiety,
Likewise the mind attached to desires
Never comes to be sated.

When beings indulge in desires,
Their craving grows again and again;
When they closely indulge in objects,
They are never sated by desire.

Whoever sees that suffering arises
From desires that are like thirst and fire,
Their craving will be pacified,
Like water poured upon a fire."

Great King, should you doubt, be uncertain, or be of two minds, thinking that the one who at that time, on that occasion, was the universal monarch called Bhūdeva was someone else, you should not regard it so. Why is that? Great King, I myself at that time, on that occasion, was the universal monarch called Bhūdeva. Although I held sway over a sovereignty so great and so vast, I died without having been sated by that sovereignty. Why is that? Great King, the faculties are unsated and cannot be sated, and objects do not bring satiety. The faculties are like a mirror, and objects are like reflections. The faculties are like an echo, and objects are like reverberations. The faculties are like an illusion, and objects are like a dream. Therefore, Great King, set your mind upon this, and do not depend on others.

The fifteenth and final fascicle.

Great King, furthermore, long ago in times gone by, there was a king called Māndhātā, renowned for his great miraculous power, great might, and great authority, who was born from the crown of the head of King Upoṣadha. He had previously gathered roots of virtue, had seen countless buddhas, had perfectly accomplished roots of virtue, and had fully accomplished pure qualities under many koṭis of niyutas of buddhas. He held sway over the four great continents. While he dwelt in Jambudvīpa, among all the beings of the desire realm, humans and gods alike, who indulged in desires, King Māndhātā was proclaimed supreme in enjoying desires in this way.

At that time Jambudvīpa was prosperous, flourishing, peaceful, and blessed with good harvests, populous and full of people. Its villages, cities, market towns, districts, provinces, and royal capitals were close together, and the villages were so near one another that a cock could reach the next in a single flight. Because it was governed by the Dharma, this great earth was free of gravel and pebbles, rich in gold and silver, and adorned with parks, pleasure groves, pools, and ponds.

The royal palace and its surroundings of King Māndhātṛ became the city called Ayodhyā. From east to west it measured twelve yojanas in length, and from south to north seven yojanas in width. It became the first city of the kings, those born in the lineage renowned as the great clan of the wheel-turning kings who arose at the beginning of the eon. It was like the great city of the gods of the Heaven of the Thirty-Three called Sudarśana, which is spacious, with nets of bells and small bells hung from above.

In the same way, King Māndhātṛ's spring residence was the furnished palace called Moonrise. His summer residence was the furnished palace called Essence of Beryl, which was like Vaijayanta, the palace of Śakra (Indra), lord of the gods. King Māndhātṛ's winter residence was the furnished palace called Lofty Sunbeams, which was also like Vaijayanta, the palace of Śakra, lord of the gods.

When King Māndhātṛ stayed with his queens in the palace Moonrise, his body became cool, as if anointed with sandalwood. When he stayed in the palace Essence of Beryl, his body was comfortable in keeping with the season, as if anointed with tamāla leaves. When he stayed in the palace Lofty Sunbeams, his body became warm, as if anointed with aloeswood.

Great King, through the power of King Māndhātṛ's wishes, those palaces gave forth a touch that was pleasant to feel. Through the power of his wishes the wind would rise, rain would fall, and the sound of music would arise. Through the power of his wishes, happiness, wealth, and every kind of enjoyment appeared.

Great King, then King Māndhātṛ thought, "Oh, may a rain of riches fall in my palace for seven days!" The moment he formed this thought, a rain of riches fell in King Māndhātṛ's palace for seven days.

Great King, when those seven days had passed, King Māndhātṛ reflected on that rain of riches that had fallen and thought, "How wondrous and marvelous it is that, through the maturation of thoroughly pure karma, my wishes are seen to be fulfilled exactly as I intend, and that merit too does not deceive!" Then he thought further, "I have ruled over the great continent of Jambudvīpa, which is prosperous and flourishing, peaceful and with good harvests, full of many beings and people. Since a rain of riches has fallen in my house for seven days, now I shall go to the western continent of Aparagodānīya."

The moment he formed this thought, Great King, King Māndhātṛ rose up into the sky together with his fourfold army, went from Jambudvīpa to the western continent of Aparagodānīya, and there for many hundreds of thousands of years he ruled as king, with enjoyments that surpassed those of humans yet had not become divine substances. While he dwelt in that great western continent of Aparagodānīya too, the moment he formed the thought, a rain of riches fell for seven days, just as it had fallen in Jambudvīpa.

Great King, then King Māndhātṛ thought, "I have ruled over Jambudvīpa, prosperous and flourishing and full of many beings and people, and a rain of riches fell in my house for seven days. I have also ruled over the great western continent of Aparagodānīya, and here too in my house a rain of riches has fallen for seven days. There is the great eastern continent called Pūrvavideha, so I shall go to where the great eastern continent of Pūrvavideha lies."

The moment he formed this thought, Great King, King Māndhātṛ rose up into the sky together with his fourfold army and went to the great continent of Pūrvavideha, and there too for many hundreds of thousands of years he ruled as king, with enjoyments that surpassed those of humans yet had not become divine substances.

Great King, then King Māndhātṛ thought there as well, "Since I have ruled over Jambudvīpa, the western Aparagodānīya, and the eastern Pūrvavideha, now I shall go to the great northern continent of Uttarakuru. Although its people have no sense of 'mine' and no possessiveness, I shall go there nonetheless and instruct my own army."

The moment he formed this thought, King Māndhātṛ rose up into the sky together with his fourfold army and went from the great continent of Pūrvavideha to the great northern continent of Uttarakuru. There, among people who had no sense of 'mine' and no possessiveness, he instructed his army for many hundreds of thousands of years.

Great King, then, after many hundreds of thousands of years had passed, King Māndhātṛ thought, "I have ruled over Jambudvīpa, and a rain of riches fell in my house for seven days. I have also ruled over the great western continent of Aparagodānīya. I have also ruled over the great eastern continent of Pūrvavideha. I have also ruled over the great northern continent of Uttarakuru. Now, it is said that there are gods called the Thirty-Three who dwell on the summit of Sumeru, so I shall go to the abode of the Thirty-Three."

Great King, the moment King Māndhātṛ formed this thought, he rose up into the sky together with his fourfold army and arrived at the summit of Sumeru. At that time the gods of the Heaven of the Thirty-Three were gathered and seated in the assembly hall of the gods called Sudharmā. Śakra, lord of the gods, saw King Māndhātṛ coming from far away. Seeing him, he moved over to half of his seat and said, "Come here, Great King! Welcome, Great King! Whenever you deem it fitting, please sit on half of this seat." And King Māndhātṛ sat down on half of the seat of Śakra, lord of the gods.

As soon as King Māndhātṛ sat on half of Śakra's seat, he outshone all the gods of the Heaven of the Thirty-Three in ten divine respects. What are the ten? Divine lifespan, divine fame, divine complexion, divine happiness, divine command of sovereignty, divine form, divine sound, divine smell, divine taste, and divine touch. These are the ten, and by them he outshone everyone except Śakra, lord of the gods. Why was that? The gods could tell "This is King Māndhātṛ" and "This is Śakra, lord of the gods" only by the blinking and not blinking of their eyes. Apart from that, King Māndhātṛ and Śakra, lord of the gods, were equal in form, equal in conduct, and equal in enjoyments. Great King, it was a great wonder that the two, one born a human and the other born a god, came to have bodies so alike.

Great King, there King Māndhātṛ ruled over half the kingdom among the gods of the Heaven of the Thirty-Three for many hundreds of thousands of years. Then, Great King, King Māndhātṛ held sway over the dominion of the four great continents, and held sway over half the kingdom of the gods of the Thirty-Three. Yet, unsatisfied with that dominion, he gave rise to this thought: "Ah! I shall hold sway over the whole dominion of the kingdom of the gods of the Thirty-Three." As soon as this thought arose, he fell from the abode of the Thirty-Three together with his fourfold army, and arrived at the king's finest pleasure grove in Jambudvīpa.

When he came to be in Jambudvīpa, all the directions became bright. Even the light of the sun was outshone, just as the light of the moon is outshone by the sun. The sun standing in the sky was no longer radiant, just as the disc of the moon in the sky above cannot be seen when the sun outshines it. Then the people stationed in the supreme palace of the city called Unassailable saw that the king had arrived at that pleasure grove. Seeing this, they were amazed. They went into the city and cried aloud, proclaiming, "Friends, a son of the gods has come down from the sky and arrived at this royal pleasure grove!"

Great King, when the people of the realm and their king heard this, they hurried in great haste, carrying songs and music, flowers, incense, garlands, fumigating incense, unguents, parasols, victory banners, pennants, and canopies. Conducting themselves in purity, they came out of that city and went to where the pleasure grove was. Great King, as soon as King Māndhātṛ had settled in that pleasure grove, this great earth quaked in six ways.

Great King, then the people of the realm and their king approached King Māndhātṛ, giving off the overpowering smell of human beings. King Māndhātṛ, accustomed to divine enjoyments, could not bear the things of humans, and he began to sink into the ground and could not stay upright, just as a lump of butter or a lump of fresh butter placed on hot sand melts and cannot hold its shape. When the people of the realm and their king saw him sinking, they asked, "Who are you?"

Then King Māndhātṛ said to the king and the people of the realm, "Have you heard that there once was someone called King Māndhātṛ?"

They replied, "We have heard from the elders that one called King Māndhātṛ went to heaven in that very body, together with his fourfold army."

King Māndhātṛ said to them, "Friends, the one called King Māndhātṛ is I. I have fallen from heaven and dropped down here."

When he had said this, the king and the people of the realm addressed him in verses of song:

"We have heard from the elders
That there was a lord of men, of great miraculous power,
Free from sorrow and of great renown,
Called Māndhātṛ.
With palanquins, troops, and mounts,
With his attendants, he went to the heavenly world.
That foremost lord of men dwells in the abode of the gods,
And guards the earth by Dharma too, so we have heard."

When they said this, that lord of men,
Afflicted by impermanence, now destitute,
Having lost his happiness and met with suffering,
Said, "That king is I."
When he said this, all the people
Joined their palms and touched his feet:
"King, what are we to say?
Please tell us of this marvel, this wonder."
At this the king, fallen from the gods, said:
"Hear the wonder of one who has found suffering.
Anyone who wishes for lasting happiness
Should always be heedful.
Māndhātṛ, supreme among kings,
Enjoyed the four continents as king,
And possessed the finest pleasures of the senses,
Yet he passed on, never sated by desire.
In his house, for seven days,
There fell a rain of showering treasure.
Having enjoyed the four continents,
Never sated by desire, he sank down.
On half of the seat of Śakra, lord of the gods,
The king sat;
Ruined by the thoughts of conceptual thinking,
Desiring for himself, crushed by pride, he declined.
The unwise, in this ocean of saṃsāra,
Sink again and again among the destinies.
When one indulges deeply in desires,
One does not find the satisfaction the Victor praised.
Like water drunk in a dream,
Which, though drunk, brings no relief from thirst,
So too with the pleasures of the senses:
Indulged in, they bring no satisfaction.
Those who are endowed with intelligence
Dispel the darkness with knowledge,
And, for the sake of satisfaction, rightly examine
The noble wisdom of the Blessed One.
The intelligent one who destroys existence
Examines aging, death, and birth;
Having fully abandoned craving,
He abandons existence and does not grasp.
He knows the aspects of feeling to be like fire,
And knows contact too to be like fire;
Toward ignoble feeling
And toward that contact he has no craving.
Like cymbals struck in music,
Object and faculty do not act on their own.
Those who follow the Victor's teaching understand the faculties;
Seeing their intrinsic nature, they cast the faculties aside.
Although the six sense sources do not exist,
The mind is stirred in connection with form.

Because it is imputed as consciousness,
Feeling is said to be thorough conceptualization.
Knowing it thus, the noble ones
Do not place their trust in the stream of existence.
Satisfied by noble wisdom,
They become still, like a fire without fuel.
Having taught that existence is impermanent
To the kings, and to the realm together with its people,
Māndhātā, having declared this,
That lord of men passed away.

Great King, you may suspect, doubt, or hesitate, thinking that the king called Māndhātā who appeared at that time was someone else. If so, you should not regard it that way. Why is that? Great King, at that time I myself was the universal monarch called Māndhātā. Although I held vast sovereign power over a kingdom, I died still unsatisfied by desires. Therefore, Great King, understand it in this way, abandon the pride of power, and be heedful. Abiding in heedfulness, you should truly accomplish the roots of virtue. Great King, one who delights in heedfulness will accomplish his aims. Therefore, Great King, through heedfulness you should truly realize the sameness of the elements. Great King, the conditioned element and the unconditioned element are not female and not male. They are not past, not future, and not present. Therefore, Great King, you should set your mind firmly on this and not depend on others.

Great King, long ago, in times past, there was a king called Nemi. He was righteous, a Dharma king devoted to heedfulness. Whatever undertaking he began, he conducted and carried out all of it with heedfulness. Great King, King Nemi applied himself to the sameness of the three times, thinking: "All these phenomena are the same through the sameness of the three times. In the past, too, all phenomena are devoid of intrinsic nature. In the future and in the present, too, all phenomena are devoid of intrinsic nature."

Because he saw all phenomena as the sameness of the three times, he had no attachment to any phenomenon. He saw that beings' minds had been distorted by the four distortions of mind: perceiving the unattractive as attractive, perceiving suffering as happiness, perceiving the impermanent as permanent, and perceiving what is without self as a self. Then he thought: "These beings do not see that all phenomena are empty. Alas, alas, this world we live in is ruined! Alas, this world we live in is utterly ruined!" Having thought this, he reflected: "I shall gather these beings through the four means of gathering. If I gather them through the four means of gathering, I will be able to set them in the Dharma."

Great King, then King Nemi, putting skillful means first, gathered beings through the four means of gathering. He then taught them with instructions on the sameness of the three times: "O beings, all phenomena are empty of intrinsic nature. O beings, a phenomenon that has no intrinsic nature is neither past, nor future, nor present. Why is that? Because it has no intrinsic nature, it cannot be designated 'past,' nor can it be designated 'future' or 'present.'"

Great King, King Nemi urged those beings in this way toward the sameness of the three times. As a result, eighty hundred thousand koṭis of niyutas of living beings attained acceptance of the unborn nature of phenomena.

At that time the gods of the Heaven of the Thirty-Three had gathered and were seated in Sudharmā, the assembly hall of the gods. In the course of their gathering, this talk arose among them: "This Nemi is a righteous Dharma king endowed with skillful means. Through skillful means he correctly teaches what is free of distortion to beings whose minds are distorted. Oh, how fortunate are the people whose king he is! Oh, those people have truly gained a great gain!"

Then Śakra (Indra), lord of the gods, went to the Sudharmā hall of the gods. Having arrived, he sat on his own seat and said to the gods of the Heaven of the Thirty-Three, "You who are seated here now, what are you talking about?"

When he had said this, the gods of the Heaven of the Thirty-Three said to Śakra, lord of the gods, "Lord of the gods, while we were here in the Sudharmā hall of the gods, this talk arose among us: 'This Nemi is a righteous Dharma king endowed with skillful means. Through skillful means he teaches beings whose minds are distorted, without distortion, that all phenomena are without intrinsic nature. Oh, how fortunate are the people whose king he is! Oh, those people have truly gained a great gain!' That is what we were saying."

When they had said this, Śakra, lord of the gods, said to those gods of the Heaven of the Thirty-Three, "Friends, since the king called Nemi possesses inconceivable skill in means, do you wish that King Nemi come here so that you may see him, or do you not?"

The gods of the Heaven of the Thirty-Three said, "Lord of the gods, we too wish that Nemi come here so that we may see him."

Then Śakra (Indra), lord of the gods, said to his charioteer Mātali, "Mātali, come here! Take the divine chariot drawn by thoroughbreds, with its thousand spokes, go to wherever King Nemi is, and say this to King Nemi: 'Great King, I have come bringing this chariot of the gods of the Heaven of the Thirty-Three, drawn by thoroughbreds and with a thousand spokes. Great King, please mount it without hesitation. The gods of the Heaven of the Thirty-Three will rejoice to see you.' Say this, and when he has mounted the chariot, say this: 'Great King, by which road shall I take you? Shall we go by the realm of error, where those who have fallen into error dwell, or by the realm free of error, where those without error dwell?'"

Saying "Very well," the charioteer Mātali obeyed Śakra, lord of the gods, descended, and spoke to King Nemi just as he had been instructed. When he had said this, King Nemi said to the charioteer Mātali, "Mātali, in that case, let us go by both roads." Then the charioteer Mātali drove King Nemi through both the realm of error, where those who have fallen into error dwell, and the realm free of error, where those without error dwell, and so they went.

Then, Great King, King Nemi said to the charioteer Mātali, "Mātali, so that I may look upon the realm of error, let the chariot stop for a moment." Then, in just the interval for which the charioteer Mātali held the chariot still, King Nemi established eight hundred million living beings in the meditative absorption of the definitive teaching of reality, because he was one who sports in heedfulness and in the higher knowledges. They, too, attained acceptance of the nonarising of phenomena. Yet the charioteer Mātali did not know what deed King Nemi had done.

Then the charioteer Mātali drove on in the same way and arrived with King Nemi at the summit of Sumeru. Then King Nemi saw from far away a dense forest, blue-green in every way, and on seeing it he said to the charioteer Mātali, "Mātali, this dense forest is without doubt the abode of beings who are free of error."

Mātali said, "Great King, this is Sudharmā, the assembly hall of the gods of the Heaven of the Thirty-Three, and the gods of the Heaven of the Thirty-Three are gathered here. Great King, please go in without hesitation. The gods of the Heaven of the Thirty-Three wish to see you."

Then, Great King, King Nemi went in without hesitation, and Śakra, lord of the gods, saw King Nemi coming from far away. Seeing him, he spread out half of his seat. Then King Nemi sat on half of Śakra's seat. Then Śakra, lord of the gods, knowing that King Nemi was seated on the seat, greeted him with great delight and said, "Great King, since you have thus established the teaching of the buddhas, the blessed ones, in the world, you have truly gained a fine gain."

Then Śakra, lord of the gods, said to the gods of the Heaven of the Thirty-Three, "O friends, this King Nemi possesses inconceivable skill in means, for in a single instant he has tamed eight hundred million living beings in the Dharma of the Buddha."

Then King Nemi said this to Śakra, lord of the gods: "Lord of the gods, I wish to go back to Jambudvīpa. Why is that? So that I may go there and uphold the teaching of the Tathāgata."

Śakra said to him, "Great King, go whenever you see fit."

Then King Nemi went back to Jambudvīpa, and because he was endowed with great compassion and thoroughly trained in skillful means, countless tens of millions of beings were tamed in the teaching of the Buddha.

Great King, if you should have doubt or hesitation, thinking that the one who was the king called Nemi at that time, on that occasion, was someone else, Great King, you should not see it that way. Why is that? Great King, I myself was the one called King Nemi at that time, on that occasion. Great King, look how King Nemi, taking no delight in half of the seat of Śakra, lord of the gods, generated the power of heedfulness. Therefore, Great King, you should strive heedfully in the qualities of the Buddha. And, Great King, what are those qualities of the Buddha? Great King, all phenomena are the qualities of the Buddha.

When he had said this, King Śuddhodana said this to the Blessed One: "Blessed One, in that case, if all phenomena are the qualities of the Buddha, then all sentient beings, too, are buddhas."

The Blessed One said, "Great King, those who are free of error are buddhas. Great King, what is called 'buddha' is a synonym for seeing reality. What is called 'seeing reality' is a synonym for the true limit. What is called 'the true limit' is a synonym for the realm of phenomena. Great King, what is called 'the realm of phenomena' cannot be taught otherwise than as a mere name, a mere sign, a mere convention, a mere conventional truth, a mere expression, or a mere designation.

Great King, this nonarising of all phenomena is a door of dhāraṇī. Why is that? Because in it all phenomena are unmoving, unwavering, and free of conceit; for that reason it is a door of dhāraṇī. Great King, the nonceasing of all phenomena is a door of dhāraṇī. Why is that? Because in it all phenomena are unmoving, unwavering, and free of conceit; for that reason it is a door of dhāraṇī."

In the same way, all phenomena are without defining characteristics, signless, unborn, and without intrinsic nature. They are without cognition, unmade, and unconditioned. They are without coming and without going. They are without a sentient being, without a life principle, without a nourisher, and without a person. They are without conceptual elaboration, signless, without designation, without entanglement, without latent tendencies, without affliction, and without purification. They are without attachment, without anger, without bondage, and without liberation. They are without going, without running, without turning back, without lowness, without abiding, without sitting, without meditative equipoise, and without error. They are without a knower, without a cognizer, without a seer, and without a non-seer. They are without moral discipline and without the absence of moral discipline, without regret and without the absence of regret. They are without delight and without the absence of delight, without joy and without the absence of joy, without pliancy and without the absence of pliancy, without happiness and without unhappiness. They are without meditative absorption and without the absence of meditative absorption. They are without the correct, as it really is, and without the wrong. They are without disenchantment and without the absence of disenchantment, without freedom from desire and without the absence of freedom from desire, without view and without non-view, without liberation and without non-liberation, without knowing and without not knowing, without seeing and without not seeing. They are without karma and without karmic maturation, without proper attention and without improper attention. Great King, one should enter into all phenomena through these seventy gateways of aspects.

Great King, the intrinsic nature of form has not arisen, will not arise, and does not exist at present. Great King, in the same way, the intrinsic nature of feeling, perception, formations, and consciousness has not arisen, will not arise, and does not exist at present.

Great King, it is like this: the intrinsic nature of the form of a reflection, for example, has not arisen, will not arise, and does not exist at present. Great King, in the same way, the intrinsic nature of form, and the intrinsic nature of feeling, perception, formations, and consciousness, has not arisen, will not arise, and does not exist at present.

Great King, it is like this: the intrinsic nature of the sound of an echo, for example, has not arisen, will not arise, and does not exist at present. In the same way, the intrinsic nature of form, feeling, perception, formations, and consciousness has not arisen, will not arise, and does not exist at present.

Great King, it is like this: the intrinsic nature of the water of a mirage, for example, has not arisen, will not arise, and does not exist at present. In the same way, the intrinsic nature of form, feeling, perception, formations, and consciousness has not arisen, will not arise, and does not exist at present.

Great King, it is like this: a lump of foam, for example, has no core; it has not arisen, will not arise, and does not exist at present. In the same way, everything from the intrinsic nature of form up to the intrinsic nature of consciousness has not arisen, will not arise, and does not exist at present.

Great King, it is like this: a beautiful woman who is the object of a dream, for example, has not arisen, will not arise, and does not exist at present. In the same way, everything from the intrinsic nature of form up to the intrinsic nature of consciousness has not arisen, will not arise, and does not exist at present.

Great King, it is like this: the birth of a barren woman's son in a dream, for example, has not occurred, will not occur, and does not exist at present. In the same way, everything from the intrinsic nature of form up to the intrinsic nature of consciousness has not arisen, will not arise, and does not exist at present.

Great King, it is like this: space, for example, has no location. In the same way, everything from form up to consciousness has no location.

Great King, form has no arising. In the same way, up to consciousness, there is no arising. Great King, form has no cessation. In the same way, up to consciousness, there is no cessation.

Great King, it is like this: in the realm of nirvāṇa, for example, there is no arising or cessation. In the same way, in everything from form up to consciousness there is no arising or cessation. Great King, it is like this: in the realm of phenomena, for example, there is no arising or cessation; in the same way, in everything from form up to consciousness there is no arising or cessation.

Great King, these are the domain of the Tathāgata, an inconceivable domain. It is a domain not shared with any childish ordinary beings, and not shared with any śrāvakas or pratyekabuddhas. It is not diminished and not increased, not attained and not lost. It is not perfectly and fully awakened, nor not perfectly and fully awakened. It is not directly known, nor not directly known; not thoroughly known, nor not thoroughly known; not abandoned, nor not abandoned; not cultivated, nor not cultivated; not actualized, nor not actualized. It is not taught, nor not taught; not fully taught, nor not fully taught; not proclaimed, nor not proclaimed; not made to be heard, nor not made to be heard; not heard, nor not heard.

Why is that? Great King, because no phenomenon that could be added to or taken away is apprehended. Great King, all phenomena are without intrinsic nature. Great King, one should rejoice wholeheartedly in this, and not depend on others.

Then King Śuddhodana thought: "Here no phenomenon whatsoever is apprehended, by the realization of which one is called a buddha. Nevertheless, the Tathāgata has taught designations for phenomena that cannot be apprehended." At that moment seventy thousand Śākyas, King Śuddhodana among them, attained acceptance of the unborn nature of phenomena.

Then the Blessed One, knowing their superior intention, smiled.

This is the nature of reality of the buddhas, the blessed ones. Whenever they smile, rays of light of various colors, of many hues, issue from the Blessed One's mouth. They fill limitless, boundless world systems with their brilliance and rise up as far as the world of Brahmā. Then they turn back and dissolve into the crown of the Blessed One's head.

Then the venerable Aśvajit rose from his seat, draped his upper robe over one shoulder, and placed his right knee on the ground. Bowing with palms joined toward the Blessed One, he addressed the Blessed One in verse:

"Guide of the world,
Great Hero, you have smiled.
You who bring light to the world,
Please tell the cause of your smile.
Omniscient One, Guide,
For what reason have you shown a smile?
Make it known to beings,
And swiftly cut off my doubts.
For the sake of the assembly of Śākyas
Gautama has smiled.
Hero who brings joy to beings,
Please dispel the doubts of beings.
Great meditator, when they hear your speech,
The doubts of beings are cut off;
Their minds become supremely joyful,
And all of them attain your teaching.
If your heirs come to understand
This deed of the Victor's smile,
Through diligence they will be fully certain
And will follow it with understanding.
Bull among Victors, please
Dispel these doubts of beings.
When the doubts of the world are cut off,
It will attain vast happiness."

When he had said this, the Blessed One replied to the venerable Aśvajit in verse:

"Aśvajit, hear from me
Why it is that I have smiled.
I have foretold, truly as it is,
The certainty of mind of the Śākyas.
That phenomena cannot be apprehended
The Śākyas truly understand.
Therefore on the qualities of a buddha
These have firmly set their minds.
Relying on non-apprehension,
The Śākyas of great renown,
Having gained certainty about all phenomena,
Will attain unsurpassed awakening.
These Śākyas who have gained certainty,
When they pass away from among humans,
Will go to the world of Sukhāvatī,
Into the presence of Amitābha.
As fearless bodhisattvas,
While they dwell in Sukhāvatī,
They will make offerings to countless millions
Of buddhas throughout the ten directions.
These wise ones, in a single realm,
Will honor and serve the buddhas dwelling there,
Will seek the good of all sentient beings,
And make aspirations for supreme awakening.
Making offerings to the tathāgatas,
They will wander from field to field;
Wherever a buddha appears,
They will go there by the power of miracles.
For countless millions of eons,
With flowers of every kind,
They will worship the tathāgatas,
And after that they will become guides.
At that time each of those buddhas
Will tame great multitudes of sentient beings
For the sake of awakening, as buddhas,
Guiding beings toward the buddhas' intent.
In the fields of all the buddhas
They will teach the bodhisattvas.
There, not a single buddha
Will ever come to train śrāvakas.
For each of those buddhas,
The span of life there will be such
That they will remain teaching the Dharma
For countless millions of years.
After they have passed into parinirvāṇa,
Fearless bodhisattvas,
Heirs of the Victor, will uphold the true Dharma
For a thousand million years.
Those sons of the lord of men, too,
By teaching the Dharma of emptiness,
Will train inconceivable millions of beings
Toward awakening.
Those who are conscientious,
Abiding in conscientiousness
And relying on the Dharma of emptiness,
Will surely go forth into omniscience."

When they heard this teaching by the perfect Buddha
About the destiny of the sons of the Śākyas,
The world with its gods rejoiced
And directed their minds toward awakening.

This concludes the twenty-sixth chapter, the Prophecy to the Śākyas.

Then the Blessed One said to the venerable Śāriputra, "Śāriputra, this bodhisattva samādhi, the Definitive Teaching of Suchness, should be proclaimed to bodhisattvas who have reached the consummation. Why is that? Śāriputra, this samādhi is without a person; in it the Tathāgata has not apprehended any phenomenon whatsoever. What is not apprehended has not been fully and perfectly awakened to. What has not been fully and perfectly awakened to has not been taught. What has not been taught has not been known. What has not been known is the teaching of the buddhas, the blessed ones, of the past, the future, and the present. It is the great drum of the Dharma of the tathāgatas. Therefore, Śāriputra, I entrust this to you, so that this bodhisattva samādhi, the Definitive Teaching of Suchness, may be held, read, and also correctly taught at length to others.

"Śāriputra, suppose a son or daughter of good family who has correctly entered the bodhisattva vehicle were to practice the five perfections, devoid of the perfection of wisdom, for ten eons. Śāriputra, one who even merely hears this bodhisattva samādhi, the Definitive Teaching of Suchness, will generate far more merit than that. Śāriputra, compared with one who listens to it for ten eons, one who teaches it to even a single sentient being will generate far more merit than that. Compared with one who listens to it for ten eons and explains it to others, one who meditates on it for as long as a snap of the fingers will generate far more merit than that.

"Therefore, Śāriputra, bodhisattvas should be instructed in this very bodhisattva samādhi, the Definitive Teaching of Suchness, with all diligence. Why is that? Śāriputra, because by meditating on this bodhisattva samādhi, the Definitive Teaching of Suchness, one swiftly attains patience regarding the nonarising of phenomena. Śāriputra, all these assemblies of bodhisattvas whom I have prophesied to unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening are, without exception, firmly established in this bodhisattva samādhi, the Definitive Teaching of Suchness."

When the Blessed One had spoken thus, the venerable Śāriputra, those monks, King Śuddhodana, Śakra (Indra), Brahmā, those guardians of the world, that entire assembly, and the world with its gods, humans, asuras, and gandharvas rejoiced and praised what the Blessed One had said.

This concludes "The Meeting of the Father and Son," the sixteenth chapter, which definitively teaches suchness, from the Dharma discourse of the noble Great Heap of Jewels in one hundred thousand chapters.

It was translated and edited by the Indian preceptors Śīlendrabodhi, Jinamitra, and Dānaśīla, and the chief editor and translator, the monk Yeshé Dé, and was then revised according to the new standard terminology and finalized.

This concludes the translation. The following are translator's notes.

The long series of paired epithets reproduces standard Indic stock terms (for example "one who had bathed" for snātaka, "the single guard" for ekārakṣa, "the four reliances" for the four apāśrayas, "partial truths" for pratyekasatya, "the unshakable" for āneñjya). Several are compressed and are rendered literally. The Buddha's reply to Kauṇḍinya is terse. It appears to mean that, as a most senior and highly honored disciple, he should not go himself but stay.  In the second verse line the Tibetan reads "foremost in family and in deeds" (rigs dang byed); this is very likely a slip for rigs dad byed, "bringing families to faith," which echoes the Buddha's prose statement just before, and has been translated accordingly. "Crazed by a death" (shi bas myos) is read in light of the following verse, which speaks of a woman maddened by her husband's death.  The king's name, rendered here as "Truth Increaser," could not be securely identified with a Sanskrit form (possibly Satyavardhana), so it is translated by meaning. Matigarbha and Candradatta are the usual Sanskrit equivalents of the Tibetan names. The line about "discarded mucus and ashes" is somewhat obscure in the Tibetan, and the rendering is approximate.  The names of the prince and king in the jātaka verses (Essence of Intellect, Increase of Truth) and of the Śākya youths Joyful Face and Well Awakened are translated literally, since their Sanskrit forms could not be identified with confidence; plausible originals would be Matigarbha, Satyavardhana, Nandamukha and Suprabuddha. In the description of the wheel-turning king's rule, ཆད་པ is taken in its sense of "punishment," not the doctrinal "annihilation."  The lines describing Udāyin as one who "carries the Tathāgata's food" render བཤོས་སྐྱེལ and བཤོས་བསྐུར literally; the sense may be that of an envoy or bearer sent by the Buddha, but the Tibetan wording speaks of food or provisions. The second line of Udāyin's reply ("wherever it abides") is somewhat elliptical, and could alternatively be understood as asking where the king dwells.  In the verse on the bull's gait, the text reads བསྔགས "praised," which makes little sense in context; I have read it as the similar བསྔངས "frightened," since the bull's bellow is a standard image for terrifying rival teachers.  The thirteenth verse has only two lines in the Tibetan as given; it is translated as it stands. In the third verse the doubled verb "did just as he had said" has been read as a single verb. The final stanza is completed in the next piece.  "The excellent cloud of the four and the two" renders the Tibetan literally. It may mean "four times two," that is, the eightfold path, or it may refer to two sets of teachings, such as the four truths and two others. The text does not specify. The verb rendered "ferry across" in the king's recollection is used in its ordinary sense of rescuing across the flood of existence, not in its ritual sense.  The large numbers bye ba (koṭi, ten million) and khrag khrig (niyuta, a very large number) are kept as Indian units because their exact values vary between sources. In the list of gods, the word "king of the gods" before Ābhāsvara may refer to that class or to its ruler; the list is rendered as the Tibetan gives it.  In the fifth stanza, "bde sogs khyim thab sbyin ldan" is taken as Śakra with his consort Śacī and his other wives, with "sbyin ldan" read as the epithet Maghavan, "the Bountiful One"; this reading is tentative. The kings of the heavens in the verses are identified as Suyāma (Yāma heaven), Santuṣita (Tuṣita), Sunirmita (Nirmāṇarati), and the unnamed ruler of the Paranirmitavaśavartin gods ("the king of the gods who wields control"). The "three stains" are desire, hatred, and delusion.  མཚན་མ་ལྔ is rendered "the five royal insignia," taking it as the standard five emblems of royalty. In this narrative context རྟ་མཆོག means "excellent horses," not the deity Paramāśva.  The name Siddhārtha means "one whose aim is accomplished"; the king's final verse plays on this meaning, saying the name has now proven true.  The number word ཁྲག་ཁྲིག is taken as ten to the eleventh power, giving "six trillion" for ཁྲག་ཁྲིག་ཕྲག་དྲུག་ཅུ; the exact value assigned to this term varies among sources. In the verse portion, བྱེ་བ་ཁྲག་ཁྲིག is rendered loosely as "countless myriads." The "lords of the secret ones" (guhyakādhipati) are taken as the yakṣa lords. The verb སྦྱངས in "arranged hanging fringes" is read in the sense of fitting or setting up.  The flower names rendered "white-hued" and "pale-hued" (with their "great" and "variegated" forms) are literal translations of Tibetan names whose Sanskrit equivalents could not be identified with confidence. The ornament rendered "strings of tinkling ornaments" is an uncertain interpretation. The jewel terms follow common equivalents: "agate" for musāragalva, "emerald" for aśmagarbha, and "chrysoberyl" for karketana. Khrag khrig is rendered "niyuta," an indefinitely vast number. The phrase "hundreds, sixty each" is rendered as "six thousand."  The flower names rendered "white-hued," "pale," and "variegated pale" translate Tibetan descriptive terms whose Sanskrit equivalents I cannot identify with confidence; they are given by meaning. The large number ཁྲག་ཁྲིག is rendered as a hundred billion following the standard Tibetan numeral series.  The asura lords རབ་སིམ and ཤིང་རྟ་བཟང could not be identified with confidence; their names are translated by meaning as "Fully Soothed" and "Excellent Chariot." The flower names སྐྱ་སྣར (here "pale flowers") and its compounds are of uncertain identification and are rendered by their apparent sense. སྤུག / སྦུག is taken as musāragalva and རྡོའི་སྙིང་པོ (aśmagarbha) as emerald. The last stanza of the second praise sets out the three doors of liberation: emptiness, signlessness, and wishlessness.  The asura lord བདེ་མཆོག is here taken as the asura Śambara rather than the tantric deity Cakrasaṃvara, since he appears in a list of asura kings. The names གཡུལ་ལ་མངོན་པར་དགའ་བ and འཇིག་རྟེན་གསུམ་རྣམ་པར་གནོན་པ could not be identified with confidence and are translated by meaning ("Delighting in Battle," "Subduer of the Three Worlds"). Suratha and Sunetra are probable reconstructions of ཤིང་རྟ་བཟང and མིག་བཟངས. The flowers called སྐྱ་སྣར་ཁྲ་བོ are of uncertain identity; rendered descriptively as "flowers of pale and variegated hues."  The names of two asura lords could not be identified with confidence and are translated by meaning: "Subjugator of the Three Worlds" and "Release-and-Hold." The Tibetan spells Vemacitrin's name with ཟངས where the standard form has བཟང; this is taken as the usual name.  ཁྲག་ཁྲིག is a very large number whose exact value varies between Indian and Tibetan numeral lists. "Trillion" is used here as an approximation. "The Delightful Eon" renders ཡིད་འོང་བསྐལ་པ. It may simply mean "a delightful eon" rather than the name of an eon. The shared name of the future buddhas is given in English as "Victor Victorious over Foes," since the Sanskrit form cannot be identified with confidence. In "ཁྲག་ཁྲིག་དྲུག་ཅུར་ཚངས་པ," ཚངས is read as ཚང ("full, complete"). The verb འདེམས ("choose") is rendered loosely as "lead."  The Tibetan sentence about body, speech, and mind literally says there were no beings "endowed with completely pure" conduct, which contradicts the list of things absent from a pure buddha field. A negation appears to have dropped out, and the evident sense, "not completely pure," has been translated. In the verse, "unflagging in eloquence" renders the obscure phrase spobs mi bas, read as describing the praising buddhas' undiminished eloquence.  The verb rendered "gathered" may also mean that the Tathāgata fostered or brought these beings to maturity. "Nothing dreadful left unabandoned" translates a phrase whose sense is somewhat obscure. It may instead refer to anxiety or doubt left unabandoned.  The names of the eastern world system and its buddha are translated from the Tibetan, since their Sanskrit forms could not be identified with confidence (possibly something like Candraraśmyalaṃkṛta and Tejodgatarāja).  The precious substance rendered "agate" is uncertain; it is variously identified as coral, agate, or cat's eye. The phrase rendered "day and night" reads the honorific compound literally meaning "midday and night" in this sense. The place to which Airāvaṇa carries the gods is read as the Pārijāta tree of the Thirty-Three.  The lines on the future buddhas' names are ambiguous: "wholly beautiful" may itself be their name, and "Banner of Sumeru" may be the name of the buddhas rather than of the eon; I have translated the names literally since I could not identify a secure Sanskrit form. The line beginning "The one who overpowers all" is a narrative frame within the Buddha's own verses, as in the Tibetan.  "Crane-foot ornaments" renders ཁྲུང་ཁྲུང་རྐང literally; it is evidently a kind of hanging ornament that sounds in the wind, but its exact form is uncertain. The two lines after the first wish ("Just as you who possess the ten powers... so may we too teach the Dharma") are read as continuing the maidens' aspiration; the second line could also be taken as a statement that the Buddha himself teaches and frees beings.  The large numbers rendered "tens of millions and trillions" translate the Tibetan terms for koṭi and a much larger unit; they convey indefinitely vast quantities. The eon name "Like the Stars" is identified as Tārakopama.  "Nine hundred million garlands" reads ཕྱེ་བ as a slip for བྱེ་བ (koṭi), matching the "nine hundred million nāgas" elsewhere in the passage. The rare word ཨར་མོ་ནིག (a kind of smooth, polished stone) is rendered descriptively.  The line ཤཱཀྱ་སེང་གེ་བཀའ་སྩོལ་ཐེ་ཚོམ་ཁུམ has an unusual final verb, ཁུམ; it is rendered by sense as "settle our doubt." The phrase ཆོས་ཉིད་ཡབ, applied to the Sugata, is obscure and is rendered literally as "the father, the nature of reality itself."  The line ending "taught thus to the son" is ambiguous in the Tibetan; "the son" may refer to the Buddha's royal father's son context of this sūtra or to a spiritual heir. The precious substance སྤུག is rendered "mother-of-pearl" (Skt. musāragalva) and རྡོའི་སྙིང་པོ as "emerald" (Skt. aśmagarbha); identifications of these gems vary.  The name of the future buddhas, rendered "Beyond Wonder," translates the Tibetan literally; it echoes the epithet of the Buddha as one "never moved to wonder" earlier in the verses. I could not identify a Sanskrit form with confidence. In the verse "they will bring people to liberation," the Tibetan reads "the weary," which appears to be a slip for "people," as the similar-looking word fits the context.  "Earth Guardian" translates ས་སྲུང, the name of the progenitor of these royal elephants; I could not securely identify the Sanskrit form. The flower name སྐྱ་སྣར could not be identified and is given in phonetic form as "skyasnar." In the verse on suchness, བརྗེད་པ ("forgetting") makes no sense in context and is read as བརྗོད་པ ("expression"), a likely copyist's slip.  "With horses and the finest elephants" reads the Tibetan "rta med" ("without horses") as an evident slip for "rta dang" ("horses and"). The line "As buddhas they will bear the name Infinite Realization" takes the phrase as a buddha-name given in the prophecy. It could also be read as "the buddhas' marks are realized as limitless," so the name is a literal translation, not an identified Sanskrit form.  In the line on signlessness the Tibetan reads "shows" (stön); I have read it as "empty" (stong), a similar-sounding syllable that fits the parallel lines on either side. In the line "the wise know this, not the childish," the particle after "know" has been read as the instrumental marking the knower.  "Wise in the World" translates the Tibetan name of the future buddhas literally; it may render a Sanskrit name such as Lokavid, but this cannot be confirmed. In the verse on space, "ཆོས་ཀྱི་རྣམས" was read as "ཆོས་རྣམས" (dharmas). In the line about karma being exhausted, "through abandonment" interprets the somewhat compressed phrase literally rendered "karma exhausted by what abandons."  ལྗོན་པ, the addressee of these verses, is rendered "Druma," taking it as the Sanskrit name the Tibetan normally translates (as in the kinnara king Druma). If earlier pieces rendered it differently, that form should be kept. "The mindless Dharma" (སེམས་མེད་ཆོས) is translated literally; it refers to the teaching that mind has no arising or real existence.  The line rendered "all phenomena are equal in their nonexistence" reads literally "all phenomena are equal/without equal, nonexistent" and is open to more than one construal. The letter a is the syllable of non-arising (anutpāda), the basis of the "door of A" dhāraṇī teaching.  The god class ལྷ་ཕྲེང་ལྡན་གླང་པོ་ཅན is uncertain: ཕྲེང་ལྡན is taken as the Mālādhara gods, one of the classes below the Four Great Kings, and གླང་པོ་ཅན is translated literally as "Elephant Bearers." The line ལྷ་མིར་བཅས་པའི་འགྲོ་དང་བགྱི is read as "do likewise for beings"; it could also be read as "inspire faith in beings."  "Garland-Bearing Elephant gods" renders ཕྲེང་ལྡན་གླང་པོ་ཅན་ལྷ literally, since I could not securely identify the underlying Sanskrit name. The same applies to the buddha name "Possessor of the Flower Banner" (perhaps Puṣpadhvaja) and the eon "Supreme Joy" (perhaps Pramudita). The line འགྲོ་བ་དང་བགྱིས་བྱུང་མཐོང་ནས is obscure; I have taken it to mean that beings and the assembly together saw the smile appear. The last verse alludes to the three doors of liberation: emptiness, signlessness, and wishlessness.  The future buddhas' name "Bearer of Light Rays" translates the Tibetan literally; a Sanskrit form such as Raśmidhara is possible but not certain. The number rendered "hundreds of billions" translates khrag khrig, a large numeral of the Indian series (about ten to the eleventh power). The line rendered "drawn your mind out of ignorance" has an instrumental where an ablative would be expected; the sense of liberation from ignorance seems clear.  The class rendered "the garland bearers with their elephants" translates the Tibetan literally. It is not separated from its neighbors in the list, so it is taken as a single class of being, and its Sanskrit name is not identified with confidence. "Coral" and "emerald" render two of the seven precious substances whose exact identification varies in the tradition.  The verb rendered "free" in "they will free countless multitudes of beings" is written འགྲེལ ("explain, untie"). It may be a slip for སྒྲོལ ("liberate"). Either reading gives a similar sense.
